### RUSTSEC-2026-0276 — Path traversal in apimock's file-serving fallback
- Aliases: GHSA-72g6-wgrg-vhm7, RUSTSEC-2026-0277 | Published: 2026-08-26 | Modified: 2026-09-02 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:P/PR:N/UI:N/VC:H/VI:N/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/apimock: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:4.8.1 introduced:5.0.0 fixed:5.0.0

The file-serving fallback joined a request-derived path onto the
configured response directory and checked only that the result existed,
never that it stayed inside that directory. A request containing a raw
`..` segment could read any file readable by the process, returned with
HTTP 200.

Read-only: no write, no code execution.

On the 4.x line `apimock` is a single crate containing the serving code.
Fixed in 4.8.1 by canonicalising each resolved path and rejecting
anything outside its base directory.

**apimock 5.0.0 and later are not affected by this advisory.** From
5.0.0 the serving code moved to the `apimock-server` crate, which
`apimock` depends on; that crate carries its own advisory for the same
issue, fixed in 5.19.1.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/apimock [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0276.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/apimokka/apimock-rs/commit/a9c05fec2d36a750c30e797291a0557f230c8faf [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0277 — Path traversal in apimock-server's file-serving fallback
- Aliases: GHSA-72g6-wgrg-vhm7, RUSTSEC-2026-0276 | Published: 2026-08-26 | Modified: 2026-09-02 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:P/PR:N/UI:N/VC:H/VI:N/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/apimock-server: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:5.19.1

The file-serving fallback joined a request-derived path onto the
configured response directory and checked only that the result existed,
never that it stayed inside that directory. A request containing a raw
`..` segment could read any file readable by the process, returned with
HTTP 200.

Read-only: no write, no code execution.

Exposure depends on the bind address. The default is `127.0.0.1`;
deployments binding `0.0.0.0` or a LAN address are reachable from the
network. Most HTTP clients normalise `..` away before sending, so
reaching it requires a client that does not.

All published versions before 5.19.1 are affected. Fixed in 5.19.1 by
canonicalising each resolved path and rejecting anything outside its
base directory.

**This advisory also covers `apimock` 5.x**, which depends on
`apimock-server`. `apimock` 4.x predates the crate split and carries its
own advisory for the same issue, fixed in 4.8.1.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/apimock-server [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0277.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/apimokka/apimock-rs/commit/ff0dd152ecfccb3bf00e15cc92bd4d57e485c854 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0284 — Double free in `Map::into_iter` and an uninitialized `Arc` in `SharedIncin::clear`
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-08-26 | Modified: 2026-09-14 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/lockfree: introduced:0.0.0-0

Two independent soundness problems, both reachable from safe Rust.

`Map::into_iter` drops `builder` (the caller-supplied hasher `H`) and `incin` by
hand, then commits the ownership transfer with `mem::forget(self)`. `H::drop` is
user code and may panic. If it does, `mem::forget(self)` is skipped and the
still-live `Map` unwinds, whose field drop glue destroys `builder` a second
time. An empty map is enough — no entries or concurrency required.

`SharedIncin::clear` writes `mem::uninitialized::<Arc<_>>()` into `self.inner`
before taking the real `Arc` out. `Arc` has a validity invariant, so this is
undefined behaviour at the point of creation, with no panic or concurrency
involved. Between that write and the repairing one, `self.inner` also holds
garbage while `self` is still droppable, so an unwind from `incin.clear()` or
`Arc::new` decrements a refcount through an uninitialized pointer. The
`make_shared_incin!` macro is instantiated five times, so this covers `queue`,
`stack`, `map`, `channel::spmc` and `channel::mpmc`.

## Impact

* CWE-415 (Double Free): the same allocation is freed twice.
* CWE-416 (Use-After-Free): a freed allocation is accessed during a repeated `Drop`.
* CWE-908 (Use of Uninitialized Resource): an `Arc` is constructed from uninitialized bytes.

## Fix

No fixed release is available. The crate has had no release since 2018-11-18 and
the maintainer has not responded to the report.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/lockfree [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0284.html [ADVISORY]
- https://gitlab.com/bzim/lockfree/-/issues/7 [REPORT]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0272 — Panic-safety unsoundness in `Stack::pop`, `Fifo::pop_front` and `Value::replace_stable` (use-after-free / double-free)
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-08-27 | Modified: 2026-08-31 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/stack_dst: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.8.2

Three removal and replacement paths destroy an initialized value before
committing the metadata change that removes it. Destruction runs `T::drop()`,
which is user code and may panic. If it does, the commit is skipped and the
container still claims ownership of the already-destroyed value, so its own
destructor drops that value a second time.

`Stack::pop` drops the top value before reducing `next_ofs`. `Stack`'s
destructor pops in a loop, so an unchanged `next_ofs` re-drops the same value.

`Fifo::pop_front` (via `pop_front_inner`) drops the front value before advancing
`read_pos`. An unchanged `read_pos` still points at the destroyed value.

`Value::replace_stable` drops the existing value before writing the replacement.
If the destructor panics the replacement is never written, but the backing
storage still holds the old value's metadata.

This is separate from RUSTSEC-2021-0033, which covered a clone panic on
insertion in `push_cloned` and was fixed in 0.6.1.

## Impact

A heap-owning value is freed twice, corrupting the allocator. Reachable from
safe Rust with any element type whose `Drop` can panic — no `unsafe` on the
caller's side.

* CWE-415 (Double Free)
* CWE-416 (Use-After-Free)

Confirmed under AddressSanitizer, which reports `attempting double-free` on all
three paths.

## Fix

Fixed in 0.8.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/stack_dst [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0272.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/thepowersgang/stack_dst-rs/issues/16 [REPORT]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0278 — `zbus_polkit`: authorization bypass via PID reuse
- Aliases: CVE-2026-78422 | Published: 2026-08-31 | Modified: 2026-09-02 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:L/AC:L/AT:P/PR:L/UI:N/VC:H/VI:H/VA:H/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/zbus_polkit: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:5.1.0

`Subject::new_for_owner()` in the `zbus_polkit` crate encodes the uid entry of a unix-process polkit subject as an unsigned 32-bit integer (D-Bus type u), whereas the org.freedesktop.PolicyKit1.Authority interface specifies a signed 32-bit integer (D-Bus type i). Because of this type mismatch, polkit silently discards the caller-supplied UID and instead determines the subject's owner itself by looking up the PID in /proc, a lookup that is inherently subject to a time-of-check/time-of-use race.

Consequently, an application that passes a UID obtained from a trustworthy source — for example `SO_PEERCRED` Unix socket peer credentials — in order to defend against PID reuse receives no protection, and the supplied UID has no effect on the authorization decision. A local unprivileged attacker who can cause an authorized process to terminate and then win the race to have their own process assigned the same PID can be authorized under the identity of the terminated process, bypassing the polkit authorization check and performing actions the attacker is not entitled to.

This issue affects `zbus_polkit` before 5.1.0.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zbus_polkit [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0278.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/z-galaxy/zbus_polkit/pull/101 [WEB]
- https://bugzilla.suse.com/show_bug.cgi?id=1277659 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0300 — Use-after-free in `clear` and `retain` when an element's `Drop` panics
- Aliases: GHSA-x6j6-3ffp-qrfr | Published: 2026-09-02 | Modified: 2026-09-22 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/skiplist: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:1.1.1

`SkipList::clear` drops the node chain and only then resets `tail` and `len`.
The drop runs each element's `Drop`, and `T` carries no bounds excluding a
panicking one. If it unwinds, `tail` still points at the freed node while `len`
stays non-zero.

`back()`, `back_mut()`, `last_key_value()` and `last()` dereference `tail`
through `unsafe`, so reading the container after the unwind is a use-after-free
(CWE-416). `Drop for SkipList` calls `Box::from_raw` on `self.head`, which
`clear` already destroyed, so dropping the container is a double free
(CWE-415).

`retain`, `retain_mut` and `dedup_by` reach the same state through
`Node::filter_rebuild`, which frees nodes and runs a user predicate before the
caller commits `tail` and `len`. There the head links are left partially
rewired, so traversal can also reach freed nodes.

## Mitigation

Update to 1.1.1.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/skiplist [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0300.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/JP-Ellis/rust-skiplist/pull/334 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0487 — Panic on a malformed `alpn` value when parsing SVCB or HTTPS records from text
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123572, GHSA-hx8c-fjhc-hmf5 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-proto: introduced:0.20.1 fixed:0.26.2

When an SVCB or HTTPS record is parsed from its text (zone file) form, the `alpn` service parameter value is split into a list of protocol names. That step called `expect` on the result, on the assumption that it could not fail. A malformed value, such as one with an unbalanced quote (`alpn="h`), two commas in a row or an invalid escape, makes it fail, so the parser panicked instead of returning an error.

Code that parses zone files or record text from an untrusted source can be made to panic, which is a denial of service. Records decoded from the DNS wire format are not parsed this way.

The fix returns a parse error instead. It is in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0487.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/80eaf9e79f7561e2bc1cf5b89eaba7fd25de3284 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0488 — Integer underflow when reporting a malformed TSIG record
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123571, GHSA-x962-5xwx-fr8x | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-proto: introduced:0.26.0 fixed:0.26.2

When a TSIG record is decoded from the DNS wire format, its MAC length and "other data" length are checked against the end of the record data. If a check failed, the error value was built from `end_idx - decoder.index()`. For a malformed record the decoder can already be past `end_idx`, so this unsigned subtraction underflows.

In builds with overflow checks enabled (the `dev` profile by default) the underflow panics, so a peer that sends a crafted TSIG record can crash the process. In builds without overflow checks the result wraps and is only used in the returned error.

The fix returns a plain "insufficient bytes" error without the subtraction. It is in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0488.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/b1246a8a21b6dcc34d427a7d39dd96e10825c384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0489 — Panic when an SVCB or HTTPS parameter value is a single quote character
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123573, GHSA-wgfr-mphw-j5g4 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-proto: introduced:0.20.1 fixed:0.26.2

When an SVCB or HTTPS record is parsed from its text (zone file) form, surrounding double quotes are removed from each service parameter value by checking that the value starts and ends with `"` and slicing off the first and last character. A value that is a single `"` (for example `alpn="`) passes that check, and the slice `[1..0]` panics.

Code that parses zone files or record text from an untrusted source can be made to panic, which is a denial of service. Records decoded from the DNS wire format are not parsed this way.

The fix rejects a lone quote character with a parse error before the slice. It is in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0489.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/c663b60a4b4b98deb45d16ccbf10ce2f76cd88a9 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0490 — Zone file lexer panics on a token of 4096 or more characters
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123574, GHSA-4rph-pmrw-mwpw | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-proto: introduced:0.24.0 fixed:0.26.2

The zone file lexer read each token in a loop capped at 4,096 iterations, with an `assert!` on the loop counter as a safety bound. A single token long enough to reach that bound, for example a very long name or character string in a zone file, triggered the assertion and the parser panicked instead of returning the token or an error.

Code that parses zone files from an untrusted source can be made to panic, which is a denial of service.

The fix removes the iteration bound, so the lexer ends only when it reaches the end of a token or an error. It is in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0490.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/0494e15c88fba612f9ecb4f013eb3b221822c867 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0491 — Memory amplification from record counts in the DNS message header
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123570, GHSA-67wc-6jq8-ghrc | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-proto: introduced:0.18.0 fixed:0.26.2

When a DNS message is decoded, the vectors for the question, answer, authority and additional sections were created with `Vec::with_capacity` set to the counts in the message header. Those counts come from the sender and are not checked against the size of the message, so a single small packet that claims 65,535 records makes the decoder reserve memory for that many records (about 18 MB, according to the upstream advisory) before it reads any record and fails.

Any code that decodes untrusted DNS messages is exposed. That includes servers, and clients and resolvers that receive spoofed responses. Repeated packets can drive memory use up and cause a denial of service.

The fix caps the initial capacity at 8 entries and lets the vectors grow as records are actually decoded. It is in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0491.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/280c0e5c4e3002d73ba0762835ef82272d1d33a6 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0504 — Inverted NSEC3 comparison in DNSSEC validation (hickory-proto 0.25)
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123601, GHSA-588m-chg6-8jqj, RUSTSEC-2026-0506 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-proto: introduced:0.25.0 fixed:0.26.0-beta.1

In hickory-proto 0.25.0 through 0.26.0-alpha.1, DNSSEC validation handled the last NSEC3 record of a zone's hash chain with its two comparisons reversed. That record was treated as covering almost every hash it does not cover, so an attacker who can answer or modify DNS responses could replay it, with its RRSIG, to forge proofs that existing names have no records.

From 0.26.0-beta.1 the DNSSEC validation code lives in the hickory-net crate, where this was fixed in hickory-net 0.26.2. No hickory-proto release contains a fix. Users of DNSSEC validation in hickory-proto 0.25 (directly or through hickory-resolver or hickory-recursor 0.25) should move to the 0.26.2 or later releases, which validate through hickory-net.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0504.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/87ee4f81aa9689d81847770d3a74e13982c9bcdc [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0505 — NSEC3 owner names not checked against the zone in DNSSEC validation (hickory-proto)
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123580, GHSA-p2jv-r3m3-7wf4, RUSTSEC-2026-0509 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/hickory-proto: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.0-beta.1

Up to 0.26.0-alpha.1, hickory-proto's DNSSEC validation matched NSEC3 records to a name only by the hashed first label of the NSEC3 owner name, without checking that the rest of the owner name was the zone in question. A signed NSEC3 record from any zone the attacker controls could therefore be used, by someone who can answer or modify DNS responses, to prove that a name in another zone does not exist.

From 0.26.0-beta.1 the DNSSEC validation code lives in the hickory-net crate, where this was fixed in hickory-net 0.26.2. No hickory-proto release contains a fix. Users of DNSSEC validation in older hickory-proto releases (directly or through hickory-resolver or hickory-recursor) should move to the 0.26.2 or later releases, which validate through hickory-net.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0505.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/10840b8432feb08d369f82e2bb6ce99ae23d7afa [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0506 — Inverted NSEC3 comparison lets one record "prove" that almost any name does not exist
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123601, GHSA-588m-chg6-8jqj, RUSTSEC-2026-0504 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-net: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.2

When DNSSEC validation looks for the NSEC3 record that covers a hashed name, the last record in the zone's hash chain needs special handling, because its next hashed owner name sorts before its own hash. For that record the two comparisons were reversed: it was treated as covering every hash below its owner hash or above its next hash, which is mostly the part of the chain it does not cover.

An attacker who can answer or modify DNS responses can replay that one signed NSEC3 record, with its RRSIG, from the target zone and use it in forged negative answers. The validator then accepts proof that names which do exist have no records.

This affects DNSSEC validation through `DnssecDnsHandle` (resolver, recursor and client) when built with `dnssec-ring` or `dnssec-aws-lc-rs`. The same code was in hickory-proto before it moved to hickory-net.

The fix corrects the comparison in `find_covering_record`. It is in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-net [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0506.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/87ee4f81aa9689d81847770d3a74e13982c9bcdc [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0507 — RRSIG signer name was not checked against the zone of the signed records
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123605, GHSA-2vgh-3wfw-qj7c | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-net: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.2

DNSSEC validation checked an RRset against the DNSKEYs of whatever zone was named in the RRSIG's Signer's Name field. RFC 4035 section 5.3.1 requires that name to be the zone that contains the RRset, but this was not checked.

Anyone who controls a DNSSEC-signed zone, and can answer or modify DNS responses, can therefore sign records for names in an unrelated zone with their own keys and have the validator accept them as secure.

This affects DNSSEC validation through `DnssecDnsHandle` (resolver, recursor and client) when built with `dnssec-ring` or `dnssec-aws-lc-rs`.

The fix ignores an RRSIG whose signer name is not the RRset's owner name or one of its ancestors. It is in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-net [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0507.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/019535e011131c52d19cb6a2c7a2d31270271135 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0508 — NSEC wraparound detection trusted an unvalidated SOA record
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123587, GHSA-qw83-5pm2-ggp5 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-net: introduced:0.26.0 fixed:0.26.2

The last NSEC record in a zone points back to the zone apex. To recognise it, DNSSEC validation treated an NSEC record as covering every name after its owner when its next domain name equalled the owner name of an SOA record in the response. That SOA record did not have to pass validation.

An attacker who can answer or modify DNS responses can replay a genuine NSEC record and its RRSIG, add a forged SOA record whose name equals that NSEC record's next domain name, and so make the validator accept that any name sorting after the NSEC owner does not exist.

This affects DNSSEC validation through `DnssecDnsHandle` when built with `dnssec-ring` or `dnssec-aws-lc-rs`.

The fix recognises a wraparound NSEC record from the chain itself (its next domain name sorts at or before its owner and is the zone of the queried name) and only uses validated SOA records. It is in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-net [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0508.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/e5ca40a7ef149ba5a79bc6f4916f114d153ce9ab [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0509 — NSEC3 records from another zone were accepted in proofs of nonexistence
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123580, GHSA-p2jv-r3m3-7wf4, RUSTSEC-2026-0505 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/hickory-net: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.2

When DNSSEC validation used NSEC3 records to prove that a name or record type does not exist, it compared only the hashed first label of each NSEC3 owner name with the hash of the name being checked. The rest of the owner name, which says which zone the record belongs to, was not checked.

Someone who controls any DNSSEC-signed zone can publish a signed NSEC3 record whose first label is the hash of a name in another zone. An attacker who can answer or modify DNS responses can then use it to make the validator accept that the name in the other zone does not exist.

This affects DNSSEC validation through `DnssecDnsHandle` when built with `dnssec-ring` or `dnssec-aws-lc-rs`.

The fix requires a matching NSEC3 record to belong to a zone that contains the checked name, and ignores NSEC3 records whose owner is not directly below the RRSIG signer name. It is in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-net [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0509.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/10840b8432feb08d369f82e2bb6ce99ae23d7afa [WEB]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/d2ee774a789193010f109d3d463adf72c4c261ab [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0511 — NXDOMAIN accepted for a proven empty non-terminal (hickory-proto)
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123594, GHSA-86vr-jm6c-7cpg, RUSTSEC-2026-0519 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-proto: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.0-beta.1

Up to 0.26.0-alpha.1, hickory-proto's NSEC validation accepted an NXDOMAIN response as secure even when the covering NSEC record's next domain name was below the queried name, which proves the name exists as an empty non-terminal. Because the response code is not signed, someone who can modify DNS responses could turn a genuine signed NODATA response into an accepted NXDOMAIN.

From 0.26.0-beta.1 the DNSSEC validation code lives in the hickory-net crate, where this was fixed in hickory-net 0.26.2. No hickory-proto release contains a fix. Users of DNSSEC validation in older hickory-proto releases (directly or through hickory-resolver or hickory-recursor) should move to the 0.26.2 or later releases, which validate through hickory-net.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0511.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/bfbe7ede4021965526f065be9b415945ce575e29 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0512 — DNSSEC validation accepted an unrelated signed RRset as the answer (hickory-proto)
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123581, GHSA-929p-gjf6-5hqj, RUSTSEC-2026-0520 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.0/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:L/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/hickory-proto: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.0-beta.1

Up to 0.26.0-alpha.1, hickory-proto's DNSSEC validation treated a response with a non-empty answer section as a positive answer if its records validated, without checking that any of them matched the queried name and type. Someone who can answer or modify DNS responses could return a genuine signed RRset for another name or type and have it accepted as a validated answer.

From 0.26.0-beta.1 the DNSSEC validation code lives in the hickory-net crate, where this was fixed in hickory-net 0.26.2. No hickory-proto release contains a fix. Users of DNSSEC validation in older hickory-proto releases (directly or through hickory-resolver or hickory-recursor) should move to the 0.26.2 or later releases, which validate through hickory-net.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0512.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/77ec9c5d2e9481a0753bbaf1be3cd5175d300cff [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0513 — Unbounded retries on truncated responses in the name server pool
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123604, GHSA-6w6g-hm98-mhgm | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-resolver: introduced:0.26.0-beta.1 fixed:0.26.2

When an upstream answer had the TC (truncated) bit set, the resolver's name server pool disabled UDP and sent the query again. It did this for every truncated answer, including answers that already came over TCP, and it kept no count of retries.

A name server that sets TC on every answer, which anyone running an authoritative server the resolver queries can do, kept each lookup re-sending queries over the same connection until the request deadline (5 seconds by default) ran out. This uses up resolver resources and sends many queries per lookup.

From 0.26.2 a truncated answer received after UDP has already been disabled ends the lookup with `NetError::Truncated` instead of retrying again.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-resolver [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0513.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/f9a7bc5d188e5b680b09aea91009d2fbf3fec58a [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0514 — `Resolver::lookup` and `lookup_ip` returned records that failed DNSSEC validation
- Aliases: CVE-2026-93657, GHSA-5j98-2g5x-46v6 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/hickory-resolver: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.2

With DNSSEC validation enabled, `Resolver::lookup()` and `Resolver::lookup_ip()` returned `Ok` with the records even when validation found the response bogus. The result was only visible in each record's proof status, which callers had to inspect themselves.

Applications that enable validation and treat a successful lookup as validated data would therefore accept forged or tampered answers from anyone who can answer or modify their DNS traffic.

From 0.26.2 a lookup whose records are bogus fails with a "DNSSEC validation failed" error (`DnsError::DnssecBogus`), the error is not retried, and the hickory-server forwarder answers SERVFAIL for it.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-resolver [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0514.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/30720f4fb22e5556ecbf26d2c8274ea4a9fdd238 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0515 — Recursor followed every glueless name server in a referral, with no per-query budget
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123606, GHSA-6h5c-jjg5-wj59, RUSTSEC-2026-0521 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-resolver: introduced:0.26.0-beta.1 fixed:0.26.2

When a referral listed name servers outside the delegated zone and gave no glue addresses, the recursor looked up A and AAAA records for every listed name server, recursing into each one. Only the chain-depth limits applied. There was no cap on how many name servers it chased per referral or on the total upstream work per client query.

Someone who controls a zone can return referrals that list many glueless name servers in a victim's domain, and nest them, so that each client query makes the recursor send a large number of queries to the victim's servers (the NXNSAttack pattern). The same can exhaust the recursor's own resources.

This affects the recursive resolver, which is in hickory-resolver behind the opt-in `recursor` feature from 0.26.0-beta.1. Earlier releases shipped it as the separate hickory-recursor crate.

From 0.26.2 the recursor follows at most 5 glueless name servers per delegation and sends at most 200 upstream queries per client request.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-resolver [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0515.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/74062f3a3ef6904b64717dd025945702b2cf4133 [WEB]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/47cd005b8f3d190134bbb943d2328ef8a83ade8c [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0516 — Recursor kept querying cyclic glueless sibling delegations
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123586, GHSA-cx5j-p54p-q756, RUSTSEC-2026-0522 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-resolver: introduced:0.26.0-beta.1 fixed:0.26.2

Two zones can each delegate to name servers named only inside the other zone, with no glue records (the cyclic sibling case in RFC 9471 section 2.3). Such delegations can never be resolved, but the recursor did not stop when it came back to a zone it was already resolving. It kept sending queries until the per-request limits ran out, and zero TTLs on the referral NS records made this worse.

Someone who controls two such zones can make a recursor send many upstream queries for each client query.

This affects the recursive resolver, which is in hickory-resolver behind the opt-in `recursor` feature from 0.26.0-beta.1. Earlier releases shipped it as the separate hickory-recursor crate.

From 0.26.2 each client request tracks the zones on its resolution stack and refuses to enter a zone that is already on it.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-resolver [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0516.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/308dcfd5cd9bfaa50c956427380160505e5de532 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0517 — Spoofed malformed UDP datagrams abort DNS lookups
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123584, GHSA-v44v-c8m4-gc43 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:H/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/hickory-net: introduced:0.26.0 fixed:0.26.2

While waiting for a reply on a UDP connection, the client parsed every datagram that arrived from the name server's address and port before checking its message ID. If parsing failed, the attempt ended with an error instead of dropping the datagram and waiting for the real reply. The receive loop also gave up after three datagrams.

An off-path attacker who can guess the client's source port, but not the transaction ID, can send one malformed datagram with a spoofed source address and make the pending lookup fail.

The fix reads the message ID from the first two bytes before parsing, ignores short datagrams and datagrams with the wrong ID, keeps listening until the outer timeout, and only then parses the response. It is in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-net [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0517.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/07ff35c1b33c1df27e398cee463e424cef951a82 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0518 — DNSSEC validation cache could reuse an Insecure result for a different name
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123592, GHSA-rx82-4p2j-j5cv | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-net: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.2

The DNSSEC validation cache used a 64-bit hash of the query as its key, and two keys with the same hash counted as equal. The `Hash` implementation of `Name` hashed the bytes of the labels but not where one label ends and the next begins, so names that differ only in the placement of dots (such as `ab.c.` and `a.bc.`) hashed to the same value.

An attacker who controls an unsigned delegation whose name collides in this way with a name in a signed zone can first get an Insecure result cached for their own name. A forged response for the signed name is then served from that cache entry without signature checks and returned with the AD bit clear, where validation should have failed.

This affects DNSSEC validation through `DnssecDnsHandle` when built with `dnssec-ring` or `dnssec-aws-lc-rs`.

The fix stores the query in the cache key and compares it on lookup. The `Hash` implementations of `Label`, `Name` and `LowerName` in hickory-proto were also changed to include label boundaries. Both are in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-net [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0518.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/96b87c9ab0f10c1331cef56f88742eb00bce71e1 [WEB]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/9c82ce0c529d33ef4b3d6507e3b8dbbb5cd0dd48 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0519 — NXDOMAIN accepted for an empty non-terminal that the NSEC record proves exists
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123594, GHSA-86vr-jm6c-7cpg, RUSTSEC-2026-0511 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-net: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.2

When an NSEC record's next domain name is below the queried name, the queried name exists as an empty non-terminal (RFC 8020 section 3.1). The NSEC validator did not check for this case and accepted an NXDOMAIN response backed by such a record as secure.

The response code is not covered by any signature. An attacker who can modify DNS responses can take a genuine signed NODATA response for an empty non-terminal, change its response code to NXDOMAIN, and have the validator accept that an existing name does not exist.

This affects DNSSEC validation through `DnssecDnsHandle` when built with `dnssec-ring` or `dnssec-aws-lc-rs`.

The fix rejects NXDOMAIN as bogus, and validates NODATA as secure, when the covering NSEC record's next domain name is below the queried name and is not the zone apex. It is in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-net [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0519.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/bfbe7ede4021965526f065be9b415945ce575e29 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0520 — DNSSEC validation accepted a validly signed but unrelated RRset as the answer
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123581, GHSA-929p-gjf6-5hqj, RUSTSEC-2026-0512 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.0/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:L/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/hickory-net: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.2

When a response had a non-empty answer section, DNSSEC validation treated it as a positive answer as long as the records in it validated. It did not check that any of those records had the queried name and either the queried type or type CNAME.

An attacker who can answer or modify DNS responses can therefore return a genuine, signed RRset for some other name or type, and have it accepted as a validated answer to the query instead of being handled as a response with no matching data.

This affects DNSSEC validation through `DnssecDnsHandle` when built with `dnssec-ring` or `dnssec-aws-lc-rs`.

The fix requires at least one answer record that matches the query name and is either of the queried type or a CNAME, both for positive responses and when NSEC records are checked. It is in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-net [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0520.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/77ec9c5d2e9481a0753bbaf1be3cd5175d300cff [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0521 — Recursor followed every glueless name server in a referral (no fixed hickory-recursor release)
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123606, GHSA-6h5c-jjg5-wj59, RUSTSEC-2026-0515 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-recursor: introduced:0.25.0-alpha.3

For referrals whose name servers are outside the delegated zone and come without glue, the recursor resolved every listed name server, with no cap on referral width and no per-query work budget. Someone who controls a zone can use this to make each client query send a large number of queries to a victim's servers (the NXNSAttack pattern), or to exhaust the recursor.

The recursor code moved into hickory-resolver (opt-in `recursor` feature) in 0.26.0-beta.1, and the fix is in hickory-resolver 0.26.2. No hickory-recursor release contains a fix, so users should move to hickory-resolver 0.26.2 or later with the `recursor` feature.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-recursor [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0521.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/74062f3a3ef6904b64717dd025945702b2cf4133 [WEB]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/47cd005b8f3d190134bbb943d2328ef8a83ade8c [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0522 — Recursor kept querying cyclic glueless sibling delegations (no fixed hickory-recursor release)
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123586, GHSA-cx5j-p54p-q756, RUSTSEC-2026-0516 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-recursor: introduced:0.25.0-alpha.3

Two zones can each delegate to name servers named only inside the other zone, with no glue records (RFC 9471 section 2.3). The recursor did not stop when it came back to a zone it was already resolving, so someone who controls two such zones can make it send many upstream queries for each client query.

The recursor code moved into hickory-resolver (opt-in `recursor` feature) in 0.26.0-beta.1, and the fix is in hickory-resolver 0.26.2. No hickory-recursor release contains a fix, so users should move to hickory-resolver 0.26.2 or later with the `recursor` feature.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-recursor [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0522.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/308dcfd5cd9bfaa50c956427380160505e5de532 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0530 — Parent-side NSEC at a zone cut accepted as proof of nonexistence (hickory-proto)
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123596, GHSA-3r6v-f3jh-vvqm, RUSTSEC-2026-0539 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-13 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-proto: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.0-beta.1

Up to 0.26.0-alpha.1, hickory-proto's DNSSEC validation accepted the NSEC record that a parent zone signs at a delegation as proof that names below the zone cut, or other types at the cut, do not exist. Such a record only proves that no DS record exists there (RFC 6840 section 4.1). Someone who can answer or modify DNS responses could replay the parent's genuine signed records to get a denial for an existing name in the child zone accepted as secure.

From 0.26.0-beta.1 the DNSSEC validation code lives in the hickory-net crate, where this was fixed in hickory-net 0.26.2. No hickory-proto release contains a fix. Users of DNSSEC validation in older hickory-proto releases (directly or through hickory-resolver or hickory-recursor) should move to the 0.26.2 or later releases, which validate through hickory-net.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0530.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/2e3c829ea45000b1c409a64a03bd422618372471 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0531 — Child-side NSEC or NSEC3 record accepted as proof that a delegation has no DS record (hickory-proto)
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123591, GHSA-7php-9j59-g3ch, RUSTSEC-2026-0540 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-13 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:H/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/hickory-proto: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.0-beta.1

Up to 0.26.0-alpha.1, hickory-proto's DNSSEC validation accepted an NSEC or NSEC3 record from the child side of a zone cut, which has the SOA bit set, as proof that the parent has no DS record for the delegation (RFC 4035 section 5.2, RFC 6840 section 4.4). Someone who can answer or modify DNS responses could make a secure delegation look insecure and then forge records in the child zone.

From 0.26.0-beta.1 the DNSSEC validation code lives in the hickory-net crate, where this was fixed in hickory-net 0.26.2. No hickory-proto release contains a fix. Users of DNSSEC validation in older hickory-proto releases (directly or through hickory-resolver or hickory-recursor) should move to the 0.26.2 or later releases, which validate through hickory-net.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0531.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/ec98851f4d175d97b61701bc539523ff63ddbb00 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0532 — Parent-side NSEC3 at a zone cut accepted as proof of nonexistence (hickory-proto 0.25)
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123595, GHSA-624w-vvww-xvpw, RUSTSEC-2026-0541 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-13 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-proto: introduced:0.25.0 fixed:0.26.0-beta.1

In hickory-proto 0.25.0 through 0.26.0-alpha.1, NSEC3 validation picked the closest encloser by hashed owner name alone and accepted the NSEC3 record that a parent zone signs at a delegation as proof that names at or below the zone cut do not exist. Such a record only proves that no DS record exists there (RFC 6840 section 4.1, RFC 5155 section 8.3). Someone who can answer or modify DNS responses could replay the parent's genuine signed records to get a denial for an existing name in the child zone accepted as secure.

From 0.26.0-beta.1 the DNSSEC validation code lives in the hickory-net crate, where this was fixed in hickory-net 0.26.2. No hickory-proto release contains a fix. Users of DNSSEC validation in hickory-proto 0.25 (directly or through hickory-resolver or hickory-recursor 0.25) should move to the 0.26.2 or later releases, which validate through hickory-net.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0532.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/889801333d7f18d59a4468100e8cd1859e083d43 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0539 — Parent-side NSEC at a zone cut accepted as proof of nonexistence below the cut
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123596, GHSA-3r6v-f3jh-vvqm, RUSTSEC-2026-0530 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-13 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-net: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.2

At a delegation, the parent zone signs an NSEC record for the child's name whose type bitmap has NS but not SOA (or has DNAME). RFC 6840 section 4.1 says such a record only proves that no DS record exists at that name. It says nothing about names below the cut or about other types at the cut, because those belong to the child zone.

DNSSEC validation did not make this distinction. It used such a record as a covering NSEC for names below the cut, and as a direct match proving that other types did not exist at the cut. An attacker who can answer or modify DNS responses can replay the parent's genuine signed records to get an NXDOMAIN or NODATA response for an existing name in the child zone accepted as secure. A recursor caches that denial for the negative TTL.

This affects DNSSEC validation through `DnssecDnsHandle` when built with `dnssec-ring` or `dnssec-aws-lc-rs`.

The fix adds `NSEC::is_ancestor_delegation()` in hickory-proto. Validation no longer uses such a record to cover a name below its owner, and treats a direct match on it as bogus unless the query is for DS. It is in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-net [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0539.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/2e3c829ea45000b1c409a64a03bd422618372471 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0540 — Child-side NSEC or NSEC3 record accepted as proof that a delegation has no DS record
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123591, GHSA-7php-9j59-g3ch, RUSTSEC-2026-0531 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-13 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:H/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/hickory-net: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.2

To show that a delegation is insecure, a validator needs proof that the parent zone has no DS record for it. That proof must come from the parent side of the zone cut: RFC 4035 section 5.2, RFC 5155 section 8.9 and RFC 6840 section 4.4 require the matching NSEC or NSEC3 record not to have the SOA bit set, since a record with SOA comes from the child zone's apex.

DNSSEC validation did not check this. An attacker who can answer or modify DNS responses can answer a DS query with the child zone's own signed NSEC or NSEC3 record for its apex, which does not list DS, and have the validator accept that the delegation is insecure. Records in the child zone can then be forged without any signature.

This affects DNSSEC validation through `DnssecDnsHandle` when built with `dnssec-ring` or `dnssec-aws-lc-rs`.

The fix treats a NODATA proof for a DS query as bogus when the directly matching NSEC or NSEC3 record has SOA in its type bitmap. It is in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-net [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0540.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/ec98851f4d175d97b61701bc539523ff63ddbb00 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0541 — Parent-side NSEC3 at a zone cut accepted as proof of nonexistence at and below the cut
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123595, GHSA-624w-vvww-xvpw, RUSTSEC-2026-0532 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-13 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-net: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.2

At a delegation, the parent zone signs an NSEC3 record for the child's name whose type bitmap has NS but not SOA (or has DNAME). RFC 6840 section 4.1 and RFC 5155 section 8.3 say such a record only proves that no DS record exists at that name.

NSEC3 validation picked the closest encloser by matching the hashed owner name alone, without looking at the type bitmap, so it accepted such a record as the closest encloser for names below the cut. For NODATA responses, it accepted a direct match on such a record as proof that any type other than the queried one and CNAME was absent, not only DS. An attacker who can answer or modify DNS responses can replay the parent's genuine signed records to get an NXDOMAIN for a name below the cut, or a NODATA for a non-DS type at the cut, accepted as secure. A recursor caches that denial for the negative TTL.

This affects DNSSEC validation through `DnssecDnsHandle` when built with `dnssec-ring` or `dnssec-aws-lc-rs`.

The fix adds `NSEC3::is_ancestor_delegation()` in hickory-proto. Validation no longer accepts such a record as a closest encloser, and treats a direct match on it as bogus unless the query is for DS. It is in 0.26.2.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-net [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0541.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/889801333d7f18d59a4468100e8cd1859e083d43 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0549 — NSEC3 NODATA at a zone apex accepted without an NSEC3 record matching the query name (hickory-proto)
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123600, GHSA-3jvh-8vj5-65rq, RUSTSEC-2026-0556 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-proto: introduced:0.25.0-alpha.3 fixed:0.26.0-beta.1

In hickory-proto 0.25.0-alpha.3 through 0.26.0-alpha.1, NSEC3 NODATA validation returned a secure result for a query at the zone apex even when no NSEC3 record in the response matched the hash of the query name, which RFC 5155 section 8.5 requires. Someone who can answer or modify DNS responses could replay any genuine signed NSEC3 record from the zone with the zone's signed SOA record and get a denial of any record type at the apex accepted as secure.

From 0.26.0-beta.1 the DNSSEC validation code lives in the hickory-net crate, where this was fixed in hickory-net 0.26.2. No hickory-proto release contains a fix. Users of DNSSEC validation in hickory-proto 0.25 or 0.26.0-alpha.1 (directly or through hickory-resolver or hickory-recursor) should move to the 0.26.2 or later releases, which validate through hickory-net.

- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/ffdfb295d1abaad42df126c8cdd924cac74b81c0 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0550 — Unsigned NSEC or NSEC3 record used in a nonexistence proof (hickory-proto)
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123590, GHSA-57pw-897j-v4j6, RUSTSEC-2026-0557 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-proto: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.0-beta.1

Up to 0.26.0-alpha.1, hickory-proto's DNSSEC validation kept an NSEC or NSEC3 record for a nonexistence proof if any authority record with the same owner name had validated, not only if that record itself had. Someone who can answer or modify DNS responses could pair a correctly signed record with a forged NSEC or NSEC3 record of the same owner name and get a forged denial accepted as secure.

From 0.26.0-beta.1 the DNSSEC validation code lives in the hickory-net crate, where this was fixed in hickory-net 0.26.2. No hickory-proto release contains a fix. Users of DNSSEC validation in older hickory-proto releases (directly or through hickory-resolver or hickory-recursor) should move to the 0.26.2 or later releases, which validate through hickory-net.

### RUSTSEC-2026-0551 — Records with a DNS class other than the query's marked secure without being validated (hickory-proto)
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123599, GHSA-wjgj-fvg9-65w9, RUSTSEC-2026-0558 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-proto: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.0-beta.1

Before 0.26.0-beta.1, hickory-proto's DNSSEC validation ignored the DNS class when grouping records into RRsets and left records of a class other than IN out of signature checking, then marked them secure if the IN records validated. Someone who can answer or modify DNS responses could add forged records of another class, including a DS record, and have them returned as validated.

From 0.26.0-beta.1 the DNSSEC validation code lives in the hickory-net crate, where this was fixed in hickory-net 0.26.2. No hickory-proto release contains a fix. Users of DNSSEC validation in older hickory-proto releases (directly or through hickory-resolver or hickory-recursor) should move to the 0.26.2 or later releases, which validate through hickory-net.

### RUSTSEC-2026-0556 — NSEC3 NODATA at a zone apex accepted without an NSEC3 record matching the query name
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123600, GHSA-3jvh-8vj5-65rq, RUSTSEC-2026-0549 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-net: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.2

RFC 5155 section 8.5 says a NODATA response proven with NSEC3 needs an NSEC3 record whose hashed owner name equals the hash of the query name, and whose type bitmap has neither the query type nor CNAME.

NSEC3 NODATA validation skipped this when the query name was the zone apex (the SOA owner). If no NSEC3 record matched the query name, no closest encloser was found and no wildcard applied, it still returned a secure result.

An attacker who can answer or modify DNS responses can replay any genuine signed NSEC3 record from the zone together with the zone's signed SOA record, and get a NODATA answer for any record type at the apex accepted as secure. A recursor caches that denial for the negative TTL.

This affects DNSSEC validation through `DnssecDnsHandle` when built with `dnssec-ring` or `dnssec-aws-lc-rs`.

The fix treats such a response as bogus.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-net [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0556.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/ffdfb295d1abaad42df126c8cdd924cac74b81c0 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0557 — Unsigned NSEC or NSEC3 record used in a nonexistence proof when another record at the same name is signed
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123590, GHSA-57pw-897j-v4j6, RUSTSEC-2026-0550 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-net: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.2

When DNSSEC validation collected the NSEC and NSEC3 records from a response's authority section to check a denial of existence, it kept a record if any authority record with the same owner name had validated, not only if that record itself had. A response could therefore pair a correctly signed record with a forged NSEC or NSEC3 record of the same owner name, and the forged record was used in the nonexistence proof.

An attacker who can answer or modify DNS responses can use this to get a forged NXDOMAIN or NODATA answer accepted as secure.

This affects DNSSEC validation through `DnssecDnsHandle` when built with `dnssec-ring` or `dnssec-aws-lc-rs`.

The fix keeps an NSEC or NSEC3 record only when its own signature validated.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-net [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0557.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/68cb330eb7e73a7db0cec9a25888db9f4bcf2086 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0558 — Records with a DNS class other than the query's marked secure without being validated
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123599, GHSA-wjgj-fvg9-65w9, RUSTSEC-2026-0551 | Published: 2026-09-03 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/hickory-net: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.2

When DNSSEC validation grouped a response's records into RRsets, it did not take the DNS class into account, and records of a class other than IN were left out before signatures were checked. If the IN records validated, the records of other classes (such as CH or HS) were marked secure too.

An attacker who can answer or modify DNS responses can add forged records of another class to a response and have them returned as validated. A forged DS record of another class can also get past validation, which lets any DNSKEY for the child zone be accepted and any answer from that zone be forged. How much this matters depends on how the application treats records of an unexpected class.

This affects DNSSEC validation through `DnssecDnsHandle` when built with `dnssec-ring` or `dnssec-aws-lc-rs`.

The fix rejects a whole response if any record in its answer, authority or additional section has a class different from the query's.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/hickory-net [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0558.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/hickory-dns/hickory-dns/commit/d5349ba13a2bd356239c39a6cc9caa10abe06c55 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0292 — Double free / use-after-free in `Chunk` and `InlineArray` removal methods when an element's `Drop` panics
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-04 | Modified: 2026-09-21 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/imbl-sized-chunks: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.2.0

`Chunk::{clear, drop_left, drop_right}` and `InlineArray::{clear, truncate}` drop the removed elements before updating the metadata that records which slots hold live values — the `left`/`right` index pair for `Chunk`, the length field for `InlineArray`. If an element's `Drop` panics during the drop, that update is never reached, so the collection still treats the already-dropped elements as live. When the collection is later dropped (its destructor walks the range described by the stale metadata), or a subsequent operation touches the same slots, those elements are dropped a second time — a double free (CWE-415) / use-after-free (CWE-416) reachable from safe Rust.

The stale field is `left` and `right` for `Chunk::clear`, `left` for `drop_left`, `right` for `drop_right`, and the length field for both `InlineArray` methods.

## Mitigation

Upgrade to `imbl-sized-chunks` 0.2.0 or later, which commits the metadata before dropping any element (fixed in jneem/imbl-sized-chunks#14, released in 0.2.0).

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/imbl-sized-chunks [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0292.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/jneem/imbl-sized-chunks/pull/14 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0536 — Unbounded memory growth from GRAFT messages for unsubscribed topics
- Aliases: CVE-2026-95859, GHSA-g3g5-x568-qvqx | Published: 2026-09-05 | Modified: 2026-11-13 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/libp2p-gossipsub: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.49.5

When a GRAFT control message arrived, every topic it named was added to that peer's set of topics, on the assumption that a peer grafting us must be subscribed. Nothing checked that the peer had subscribed, and subscription filters only apply to SUBSCRIBE messages. A peer can keep sending GRAFTs with new short topic IDs over one connection, and the set grows until the peer unsubscribes or disconnects. Memory grows without bound and the node can be killed for running out of memory.

The fix no longer records topics from GRAFT messages and drops a GRAFT for a topic the peer has not subscribed to. It also makes `MaxCountSubscriptionFilter` the default subscription filter. It is in 0.49.5, and was backported to the 0.50 line before 0.50.0.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/libp2p-gossipsub [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0536.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/libp2p/rust-libp2p/commit/51ed5f00e1b0620ded3444ecadf18477052148ea [WEB]
- https://github.com/libp2p/rust-libp2p/pull/6608 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0280 — `greentic-setup-dev` 1.3.34027618345 was removed from crates.io due to containing malicious code
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-07 | Modified: 2026-09-07 | Severity: None

Greentic users should check their systems nonetheless.

### RUSTSEC-2026-0332 — `WaveFormat::parse` reads past the end of a `&WAVEFORMATEX`
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-08 | Modified: 2026-10-07 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/wasapi: introduced:0.20.0 fixed:0.25.0

`WaveFormat::parse` is a safe function that takes a `&WAVEFORMATEX`, which is
only valid for reads of `size_of::<WAVEFORMATEX>()` (18) bytes. When the header
has `wFormatTag == WAVE_FORMAT_EXTENSIBLE` and `cbSize >= 22`, the function
reinterprets the reference as a `WAVEFORMATEXTENSIBLE` and reads 40 bytes.
Nothing in the signature guarantees that the header sits at the start of a
larger buffer, so safe code can trigger an out-of-bounds read:

```rust
use wasapi::WaveFormat;
use windows::Win32::Media::Audio::WAVEFORMATEX;
use windows::Win32::Media::KernelStreaming::WAVE_FORMAT_EXTENSIBLE;

let header = WAVEFORMATEX {
    wFormatTag: WAVE_FORMAT_EXTENSIBLE as u16,
    nChannels: 2,
    nSamplesPerSec: 48000,
    nAvgBytesPerSec: 384000,
    nBlockAlign: 8,
    wBitsPerSample: 32,
    cbSize: 22,
};
// Reads 22 bytes past the end of `header`.
let _ = WaveFormat::parse(&header);
```

A more likely way to hit this is to copy the header out of a format pointer
returned by WASAPI (`let fmt = unsafe { *ptr };`) and pass `&fmt`. The copy
keeps `cbSize`, but not the 22 bytes that follow it. The bytes read past the
end are returned as the channel mask and subformat of the parsed format.

The flaw was corrected in commit `2562db7`, released in 0.25.0. `parse` is now
an `unsafe fn` that takes a `*const WAVEFORMATEX`, and the caller must
guarantee that the pointer is valid for reads of
`size_of::<WAVEFORMATEX>() + cbSize` bytes. `WaveFormat::parse_from_blob_bytes`
(available since 0.23.0) is the safe alternative and checks the slice length
against `cbSize`.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/wasapi [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0332.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/HEnquist/wasapi-rs/issues/65 [REPORT]
- https://github.com/HEnquist/wasapi-rs/pull/64 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0282 — Double free in `AlignedBox<[T]>::realloc_with_default` when an element's `Drop` panics
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-09 | Modified: 2026-09-09 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/aligned_box: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.3.1

Shrinking an `AlignedBox<[T]>` takes ownership of the buffer out of
`self.container` with `ManuallyDrop::take`, destroys the elements past the new
length, and only then commits the new `Box` back into `self.container`.
`ManuallyDrop::take` moves ownership but not the bits, so until that commit
`self.container` still points at the original buffer.

`T::drop` runs inside the destruction loop and is user code — `T` carries no
bound that would exclude a panicking `Drop`. If it unwinds, the commit is
skipped and `self.container` is left pointing at the buffer whose tail has
already been destroyed. `AlignedBox`'s own destructor then reconstructs a `Box`
from that pointer, drops every element again and deallocates — a double free
(CWE-415) / use-after-free (CWE-416) reachable from safe Rust.

Growing the slice destroys nothing and is unaffected, as is
`realloc_with_value`, which requires `T: Copy` and therefore a `Drop` that
cannot run.

## Mitigation

Update to 0.3.1.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/aligned_box [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0282.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/michaellass/aligned_box/pull/6 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0291 — Double free in `OwnedAlloc::drop_in_place` when the contained value's `Drop` panics
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-09 | Modified: 2026-09-21 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/owned-alloc: introduced:0.0.0-0

`OwnedAlloc::drop_in_place` destroys the contained value by hand, then commits
the ownership transfer with `mem::forget(self)` — which lives inside `into_raw`
and so runs only after the destruction. `T::drop` is user code and may panic. If
it does, the forget is skipped and the still-live `OwnedAlloc` unwinds, whose
destructor drops the same `T` a second time and then deallocates. For a `T` that
owns an allocation, the same block is freed twice — a double free (CWE-415).
That destructor also reads the already-destroyed value through
`Layout::for_value` before deallocating, a use-after-free (CWE-416).

`MaybeUninitAlloc::drop_in_place` delegates to the same function, so both public
entry points are affected. Storing a value whose `Drop` can panic and calling
either is enough — no `unsafe` on the caller's side.

## Fix

No fixed release is available. The crate has had no release since 2018 and the
maintainer has not responded to the report, nor to the one on `lockfree`, which
is published from the same account. `forget_inner` leaks the value instead of
destroying it, which is safe.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/owned-alloc [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0291.html [ADVISORY]
- https://gitlab.com/bzim/owned-alloc/-/issues/1 [REPORT]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0297 — `unzip`: archive extraction is vulnerable to path traversal (zip-slip)
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-09 | Modified: 2026-09-21 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/unzip: introduced:0.0.0-0

### Summary

`Unzipper::unzip` extracts each archive entry to a path built from the entry's
**raw, attacker-controlled name** without any traversal check. A ZIP archive whose
entry names contain `../` components (or an absolute path) can therefore cause
files to be written **outside** the destination directory chosen by the caller —
a "zip-slip" / directory-traversal arbitrary file write (CWE-22 / CWE-23 /
CWE-36).

### Affected versions

All published versions are affected. `unzip` has only ever released `0.1.0`
(published 2017-12-23) and appears unmaintained, so **no fixed version is
available**.

### Proof of concept

A malicious archive with a single entry named `../ESCAPED.txt` extracted via
`Unzipper::unzip` writes `ESCAPED.txt` one level above the destination directory.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/unzip [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0297.html [ADVISORY]
- https://crates.io/crates/unzip [PACKAGE]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0298 — Use-after-free when a future's `Drop` panics while the container is dropped
- Aliases: GHSA-8gw6-724c-9h38 | Published: 2026-09-12 | Modified: 2026-09-22 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/unicycle: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.10.3

`Storage::clear` walks the slab, empties each entry and frees the `Task`
allocation once its reference count reaches zero. `self.tasks.set_len(0)` only
runs after the loop.

Emptying an entry drops the user future it holds, and `T` carries no bounds
excluding a panicking `Drop`. If one unwinds, the length is never committed and
`self.tasks` still holds the pointers already processed. `Drop for Storage`
calls `clear` again, and the second pass dereferences `(*task.as_ptr()).entry`
on allocations the first pass already freed — a use-after-free (CWE-416)
reachable from safe Rust.

`Storage` is reached only through `Unordered`, whose `Drop` calls `clear`. No
particular method call is needed; dropping the container is enough.

## Mitigation

Update to 0.10.3.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/unicycle [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0298.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/udoprog/unicycle/commit/4f1492a266824f8aa66fd6b27fa6188bf826bdfd [WEB]

- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-13 | Modified: 2026-09-13 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/clear_on_drop: introduced:0.0.0-0

This crate is no longer maintained. Use the [zeroize](https://crates.io/crates/zeroize) crate instead.

* Instead of `ClearOnDrop`, use `Zeroizing`
* Instead of `clear_stack`, use `zeroize_stack`

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/clear_on_drop [PACKAGE]

- https://github.com/cesarb/clear_on_drop/commit/5916d6260acbad8a34290dba137f39963f13f467 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0285 — TLS 1.3 handshake messages incorrectly accepted across encryption level boundaries
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123613, GHSA-2mjx-qc3c-rqvc | Published: 2026-09-14 | Modified: 2026-11-09 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:L/I:N/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/rustls: introduced:0.23.13 fixed:0.23.45

Rustls accepted TLS 1.3 handshake messages sent at the wrong encryption level
when they followed a key-changing message in the same record. For example,
a plaintext `EncryptedExtensions` message packed into the same record as the
`ServerHello` was accepted.

RFC 8446 section 5.1 requires that handshake messages do not span key changes,
and that implementations terminate the connection with an "unexpected_message"
alert if they do.

The handshake transcript is still authenticated, so a network-position attacker
cannot use this to alter or complete a handshake; the practical effect is that
a peer could send handshake messages that should be encrypted in plaintext
without rustls rejecting the connection.

This is functionally the same bug as Go's
[GO-2026-4340](https://pkg.go.dev/vuln/GO-2026-4340) (CVE-2025-61730).

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/rustls [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0285.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/rustls/rustls/pull/3265 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0338 — Remote peer can panic quinn-proto with zero-length STREAM frames during unordered reads
- Aliases: GHSA-465w-v9q3-7j98 | Published: 2026-09-14 | Modified: 2026-10-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/quinn-proto: introduced:0.7.0 fixed:0.11.18

When a stream is read in unordered mode (for example through `RecvStream::read(false)` in
quinn-proto or `read_unordered` in `quinn`), quinn-proto proactively deduplicates received data
by recording received ranges in a `RangeSet` through its `replace` method. When an incoming
STREAM frame carried an empty payload, the `Drop` implementation of the iterator returned by
`replace` inserted an empty range into the set, violating the invariant that stored ranges are
non-empty. Subsequent frames overlapping that region then caused a panic while processing the
stream.

A remote peer can trigger this with five small STREAM frames (two of them zero-length) once the
application has put the stream in unordered-read mode. The default ordered read path is not
affected.

The flaw was corrected in 0.11.18 by ignoring empty ranges in `RangeSet::replace`.

Thanks to Otto Hermann ([@ojhermann](https://github.com/ojhermann)) for reporting this issue.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/quinn-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0338.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/quinn-rs/quinn/pull/2868 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0340 — Peer minimum ACK delay can panic quinn-proto when ACK_FREQUENCY transmission is enabled
- Aliases: GHSA-3g6h-r5qx-f6hq | Published: 2026-09-14 | Modified: 2026-10-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/quinn-proto: introduced:0.11.0 fixed:0.11.18

When sending ACK_FREQUENCY frames is enabled through
`TransportConfig::ack_frequency_config(Some(..))`, the peer's transport parameters could cause
`AckFrequencyState::candidate_max_ack_delay` to call `Duration::clamp` with a minimum greater
than its maximum, which panics.

For example, a peer advertising a `min_ack_delay` of 100,000 microseconds and a `max_ack_delay`
of 100 milliseconds passes transport parameter validation. At a 5 ms RTT, the lower bound of the
clamp becomes 100 ms while the upper bound is 25 ms, violating the precondition of `clamp` and
panicking while driving the connection. Both automatically derived and explicitly configured
maximum ACK delays use this calculation.

Sending ACK_FREQUENCY frames is disabled by default, so only endpoints that opt in are affected.

The flaw was corrected in 0.11.18 by computing the delay with explicit minimum and maximum bounds
instead of `clamp`.

Thanks to [@zerox80](https://github.com/zerox80) for reporting this issue.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/quinn-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0340.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/quinn-rs/quinn/pull/2868 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0343 — Outgoing datagram buffer accounting can panic the sender in quinn-proto
- Aliases: GHSA-ppcp-v39w-8jq2 | Published: 2026-09-14 | Modified: 2026-10-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/quinn-proto: introduced:0.11.17 fixed:0.11.18

The limit on memory used to buffer outgoing datagrams, introduced in 0.11.17, subtracted an
evicted datagram's size from the buffered byte counter twice when `Datagrams::send` was called
with `drop = true` (as `Connection::send_datagram` in the `quinn` crate does) while the buffer was
full. Under sustained eviction the counter drifts below its true value: debug builds panic with an
integer underflow, while release builds wrap the counter, drain the whole queue, and then panic on
a desynchronization check in a later `send` call. In `quinn`, this panic occurs while the
connection lock is held, poisoning it and causing a double panic (and thus an abort) when the
connection is dropped.

This can be triggered without a malicious peer, by an application that sends a burst of datagrams
and then idles. It can also be triggered by a peer that keeps the connection alive with PING frames
while withholding acknowledgements, so that the sender's congestion window stays closed and the
outgoing queue never drains. Applications that use `send_datagram_wait` are not affected.

Only 0.11.17 is affected; the flaw was corrected in 0.11.18 by removing the redundant subtraction.

Thanks to [@alexpyattaev](https://github.com/alexpyattaev) for reporting and
[@vcarus](https://github.com/vcarus) for fixing this issue.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/quinn-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0343.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/quinn-rs/quinn/pull/2806 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0286 — Out-of-bounds read when decoding CKA_ALLOWED_MECHANISMS
- Aliases: GHSA-r6vc-97x9-63vv | Published: 2026-09-16 | Modified: 2026-09-17 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/cryptoki: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.10.1 introduced:0.11.0 fixed:0.11.1 introduced:0.12.0 fixed:0.12.1

`cryptoki` treated the CKA_ALLOWED_MECHANISMS ulValueLen byte count as a
CK_MECHANISM_TYPE element count. A valid nonempty attribute returned through
the safe `Session::get_attributes` API could cause construction of an
out-of-bounds slice and undefined behavior.

Possible consequences include a process crash or denial of service and
potential disclosure of adjacent heap words. Upgrade to the fixed patch release
for the cryptoki 0.10, 0.11, or 0.12 line.

### RUSTSEC-2026-0294 — Unsoundness in UTF-8 'String' trait
- Aliases: CVE-2026-92612, GHSA-8mq4-3mwq-qvg6 | Published: 2026-09-18 | Modified: 2026-11-11 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/iceoryx2-bb-container: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.10.0

The String API in iceoryx2-bb-container exposes its contents as mutable bytes through safe APIs, while String::as_str() converts those bytes into a Rust string slice without validating UTF-8. 
An application can therefore create an invalid &str and trigger undefined behavior using entirely safe Rust.

The API methods `as_mut_bytes` and `deref_mut` where removed from the String API and their implementations (PolymorphicString, RelocatableString, and StaticString ) without replacement.
Link to Pull-Request: https://github.com/eclipse-iceoryx/iceoryx2/pull/1956
