- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0294.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/eclipse-iceoryx/iceoryx2/pull/1956 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0328 — `decompress`: tar-family extractors write archive entries without a path-traversal check (tar-slip)
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-19 | Modified: 2026-10-03 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/decompress: introduced:0.0.0-0

The tar-family extractors in `decompress` (`.tar`, `.tar.gz`, `.tar.xz`,
`.tar.bz2`, `.tar.zst`) build each output path from the **raw archive entry path**
and write to it with no traversal check, so a malicious archive can write files
**outside** the destination directory, a "tar-slip" / zip-slip path traversal
(CWE-22 / CWE-23).

In `src/decompressors/tar_common.rs` (`tar_extract`):

```rust
let filepath = entry.path()?;                                    // raw entry path
let filepath = filepath.components().skip(opts.strip).collect::<PathBuf>();
                                     // strips leading components only — keeps `..`
let outpath = to.join(filepath);
// ...
let mut outfile = fs::File::create(&outpath)?;                   // writes anywhere
```

`.components().skip(opts.strip)` removes a fixed number of *leading* path
components but leaves interior `..` components intact so an entry named 
e.g. `../../../../home/<user>/.bashrc`, or an absolute path, resolves **outside** 
`to`. This affects all platforms.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/decompress [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0328.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/rusty-ferris-club/decompress/issues/21 [REPORT]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0306 — `hex_decode_unchecked` AVX2 path reads past `src`
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-20 | Modified: 2026-09-23 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/faster-hex: introduced:0.3.0 fixed:0.10.1

`faster_hex::hex_decode_unchecked` is a safe function. On x86 and x86_64, when AVX2 is enabled, `hex_decode_avx2` keeps decoding while `dst.len() >= 32` and then loads 32 bytes from `src` and 32 bytes from `src[32..]`. Nothing in that loop requires `src` to contain 64 bytes.

A safe caller can pass a short `src` and a `dst` of at least 32 bytes. The AVX2 load then reads outside `src`.

The function has had this shape since 0.3.0. 0.10.0 is the last release with the unbounded AVX2 read. The maintainer agreed to this advisory and shipped the backport in 0.10.1 (pull request 74): the AVX2 loop now requires `src.len() >= 64` before those loads. 1.0.0 rewrites `hex_decode_unchecked` so the decoded length is `min(src.len() / 2, dst.len())`. Both are covered by `>= 0.10.1`. Other architectures take the scalar fallback and are outside this advisory.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/faster-hex [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0306.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/nervosnetwork/faster-hex/issues/73 [REPORT]
- https://github.com/nervosnetwork/faster-hex/pull/72 [WEB]
- https://github.com/nervosnetwork/faster-hex/pull/74 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0309 — `SinglyLinkedList::remove` dereferences a null link
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-20 | Modified: 2026-09-25 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/bun_collections: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.2.1

In versions before 0.2.1, `SinglyLinkedList::remove` is safe and walks the intrusive list with an unchecked dereference. On an empty list, or when `node` is not in the list, `(*current_elm).next` reads a null pointer. That is undefined behavior. The list head is a raw `*mut Node<T>`, and safe code can construct the empty list.

The maintainer fixed this in 0.2.1 by rejecting those two cases with an unconditional `assert!` before the pointer is followed, matching the upstream Zig `unwrap` on the same paths. 0.2.0 was yanked. Versions 0.1.0 through 0.1.13 are still published and still contain the unchecked walk.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/bun_collections [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0309.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/putao520/bao/issues/46 [REPORT]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0293 — Double free / use-after-free in `Consumer::skip` and `Consumer::clear` when an element's `Drop` panics
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-21 | Modified: 2026-09-21 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/ringbuf: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.5.2

`Consumer::skip()` and `Consumer::clear()` are not panic-safe. They drop the
consumed elements in place and only afterwards call `advance_read_index()` to move
the ring buffer's read index past them. If an element's `Drop` panics mid-loop,
`advance_read_index()` is never reached, so the read index still points at the
already-dropped elements. When the ring buffer is later dropped, its destructor
re-visits those slots and drops the same elements a second time — a double free
(CWE-415) / use-after-free (CWE-416) reachable from safe Rust, confirmed under
AddressSanitizer.

`Consumer::clear()` delegates to `Consumer::skip(self.len())`, so both share the
same root cause and the same fix.

## Mitigation

Update to 0.5.2 or later (fixed in agerasev/ringbuf#60).

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/ringbuf [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0293.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/agerasev/ringbuf/pull/60 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0296 — `unzip` is unmaintained
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-21 | Modified: 2026-09-21 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/unzip: introduced:0.0.0-0

The [unzip](https://crates.io/crates/unzip) crate has only ever released a single
version, `0.1.0` (published 2017-12-23), and has seen no further activity. It
appears to be unmaintained, and its known path-traversal ("zip-slip")
vulnerability will not be fixed.

Consider migrating to an actively maintained alternative such as
[zip](https://crates.io/crates/zip).

- https://crates.io/crates/unzip [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0296.html [ADVISORY]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0304 — Finished streaming calls keep reading a stalled request body indefinitely
- Aliases: GHSA-jx5x-3mcr-qp8r | Published: 2026-09-21 | Modified: 2026-09-23 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:P/PR:N/UI:N/VC:N/VI:N/VA:L/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/connectrpc: introduced:0.2.0 fixed:0.8.2 introduced:0.9.0 fixed:0.9.1

Affected versions of `connectrpc` start a background task to read the request body of every client-streaming and bidirectional-streaming call, before the interceptors or the handler run. A call can finish before it has consumed its request: the handler can return without reading all of its request stream, an interceptor can reject the call, or the request timeout can fire. The background task then keeps reading, with no time limit. If the client stops sending part-way through a message, or never sends the rest, the task, the partial message it has buffered (up to `max_message_size`, 4 MiB by default) and the HTTP/2 stream, or the HTTP/1.x connection, are held until the client finishes the stream or closes the connection.

## Impact

A client can use this to exhaust the memory, tasks and file descriptors of a server. With the default limits of the built-in `Server`, one HTTP/2 connection can hold 200 stalled streams, about 800 MiB at the default message size, and the server does not limit the number of connections. A server that authenticates in Tower middleware, before the request reaches the service, is exposed only to callers with a valid credential. A server with no authentication, or one that authenticates in an interceptor, is exposed to any client. This advisory covers client-streaming and bidirectional-streaming calls only.

## Patches

Versions 0.8.2 and 0.9.1 bound the wait. When the handler is gone, the reader frees the partial message and discards the rest of the body for at most 5 seconds and 1 MiB, then drops it, which resets the HTTP/2 stream or closes the HTTP/1.x connection. On `wasm32`, which has no clock, only the 1 MiB limit applies. Versions 0.2.0 to 0.7.0 contain the same reader and are not patched; upgrade to 0.8.2 or later.

## Workarounds

Authenticate in Tower middleware rather than in an interceptor, so a request without a credential is rejected before any reader starts. Set `Server::with_max_connection_age`, which closes each connection, and every stalled body on it, once its age and the grace period have passed. Lowering `Limits::with_max_message_size` and `Server::with_max_concurrent_streams` reduces what one client can hold but does not release a stalled body.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/connectrpc [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0304.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/connectrpc/connect-rust/pull/313 [WEB]
- https://github.com/connectrpc/connect-rust/pull/314 [WEB]
- https://github.com/connectrpc/connect-rust/pull/315 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0301 — Double free in `StackVec::retain` when a predicate or element `Drop` panics
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-22 | Modified: 2026-09-22 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/stack_collections: introduced:0.3.0 fixed:0.3.3

## Summary

`StackVec::retain` committed its new length to `self.len` only after
its internal loop completed. If the retain predicate or a removed
element's `Drop` implementation panicked before the loop finished,
unwinding proceeded with `self.len` still equal to the original,
pre-retain length, leaving either a duplicated or already-destroyed
element inside `0..len`. `StackVec`'s own `Drop` then revisited that
slot, causing a double-drop (and for heap-owning types, a double-free).

## Impact

Affects `StackVec<T, CAP>::retain` for `T: Drop` types where the
predicate or the removed element's destructor can panic, on builds
with unwinding enabled (`panic = "unwind"`). `no_std`/`panic = "abort"`
builds cannot trigger this, since unwinding never occurs.

## Patch

Fixed in 0.3.3 using an unwind-safe backshift guard, matching the
approach `alloc::vec::Vec::retain` uses.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/stack_collections [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0301.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/OverwrittenCode/stack_collections/pull/4 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0302 — `stack-graphs` C API exports are safe `extern "C"` functions
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-22 | Modified: 2026-09-22 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/stack-graphs: introduced:0.0.3-0

`stack_graphs::c` is a public module. From 0.0.3 through the current crates.io release 0.14.1, its pointer-taking entry points are `pub extern "C" fn` rather than `unsafe fn`. Safe Rust can call them.

`sg_stack_graph_free` frees the pointer with `Box::from_raw`. `sg_stack_graph_free(std::ptr::null_mut())` is immediate undefined behavior. The same shape is used by the other `*_free` exports and by getters and mutators that dereference the caller-supplied pointer or pass it to `from_raw_parts` (`sg_stack_graph_nodes`, `sg_stack_graph_add_edges`, and the rest of the pointer-taking functions in `src/c.rs`). Constructors that take no pointer are not part of this issue.

The upstream repository is archived, so a fix cannot be filed there and no patched release exists. The soundness fix is to make every pointer-taking export `unsafe extern "C" fn`, with a safety comment that the pointer is non-null and, for `free`, came from the matching constructor.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/stack-graphs [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0302.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/rustsec/advisory-db/issues/3241 [REPORT]
- https://github.com/github/stack-graphs [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0305 — Use-after-free when XML includes have duplicated entities
- Aliases: CVE-2026-96889, GHSA-cpvr-w4j5-2mpg | Published: 2026-09-23 | Modified: 2026-10-23 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/librsvg: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:2.57.5 introduced:2.57.90 fixed:2.60.3 introduced:2.61.0 fixed:2.61.5 introduced:2.61.90 fixed:2.62.4 introduced:2.62.90 fixed:2.63.2

Librsvg uses libxml2, a C library, to parse XML.  When librsvg parses
an SVG document which has a nested Xinclude, an XML entity declaration
with a duplicate name as an existing one can cause a use-after-free error.

While libxml2 is expanding an internal entity, a recursive XInclude
can parse another document that declares an entity with the same
name. Both parses use the same `XmlState` entity
map on the librsvg side. `entity_insert()` replaces the first entry, whose `Drop`
implementation calls `xmlFreeNode()`. The outer `xmlCtxtParseEntity()`
then keeps using the freed 144-byte `xmlEntity`.

The included parse should not free an entity that the outer parser is still using.

The fix is in commit 8a1b0cd319e9af2d1e9cf878081dd77f227a0504, where
librsvg will no longer free xmlEntity pointers that libxml2 is still
using.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/librsvg [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0305.html [ADVISORY]
- https://gitlab.gnome.org/GNOME/librsvg/-/work_items/1241 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0307 — `uncbv`: archive extraction is vulnerable to path traversal (zip-slip)
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-23 | Modified: 2026-09-24 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/uncbv: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.3.1

### Summary

`uncbv extract` writes each archive entry to `output_dir.join(entry_filename)` using the filename stored in the archive with no check for `..` components or absolute paths. A malicious `.cbv` / `.cbz` archive whose entry name contains `../` (or an absolute path) can therefore cause files to be written outside the output directory chosen by the user. This is a "zip-slip" / directory-traversal arbitrary file write (CWE-22).

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/uncbv [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0307.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/antoyo/uncbv [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0394 — Build environment compiled into the library and readable at runtime
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-23 | Modified: 2026-10-26 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/rustpython-vm: introduced:0.3.0 fixed:0.6.0

The build script of `rustpython-vm` 0.3.0 through 0.5.0 writes every
environment variable of the build into `$OUT_DIR/env_vars.rs`. The crate
compiles that file into `_sysconfigdata`'s `build_time_vars`. That module is
always compiled.

A binary built in CI with secrets in the environment carries those secrets
in clear text. Registry tokens, cloud credentials and API keys are the usual
ones. Anyone with the binary can read them with `strings` or from Python.

Version 0.6.0 removes the dump and the include. Users of earlier versions
should upgrade, rebuild, and ship new binaries. Every secret that was in the
environment of a past build whose output left the build machine should be
treated as exposed.

Jacob Finkelman first reported the issue in
[#8802](https://github.com/RustPython/RustPython/issues/8802).
[Bitbison](https://registry.bitbison.io/cargo/rustpython-vm)'s automated
supply chain scanning then detected credential data from the build
environment flowing into artifacts that third-party software ships in the
wild. The leak was confirmed in the published release binaries of at least four
dependants but others have been impacted.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/rustpython-vm [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0394.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/RustPython/RustPython/pull/8813 [WEB]
- https://github.com/RustPython/RustPython/issues/8802 [REPORT]
- https://github.com/RustPython/RustPython/issues/9236 [REPORT]
- https://crates.io/crates/rustpython-vm/0.6.0 [PACKAGE]
- https://registry.bitbison.io/cargo/rustpython-vm [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0308 — Use-after-free in interned values and cached function results
- Aliases: GHSA-xc3w-55vh-cw3w | Published: 2026-09-24 | Modified: 2026-09-24 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/salsa: introduced:0.23.0 fixed:0.28.5

When an application reads an interned value's fields or uses that value as an input to a tracked function, Salsa must ensure that reusable storage has been validated for the current database revision. This prevents the value's fields and associated cached function results from being freed while in use. Affected versions checked this invariant only with `debug_assert!` when reading fields, and did not check it when using an interned value to access a tracked function's cached result.

Bugs in Salsa's dependency tracking, or an application-provided `Eq` implementation that incorrectly treats a changed query result as unchanged, can cause Salsa to reuse cached results containing stale interned values. If the application accesses these values and then interns additional values, Salsa can free the old fields or cached function results while references to them are still in use. This causes use-after-free through safe Rust APIs. Depending on the consuming application, attacker-controlled input may expose process memory or permit arbitrary code execution.

The fix asserts that an interned value's reusable storage has been validated for the current database revision before allowing an application to read its fields or access a tracked function's cached result using that value as input. If the assertion fails, Salsa panics before returning references to storage that could be freed while in use.

Salsa 0.28.5 and newer address this vulnerability. Dependent applications should update Salsa and rebuild.

Applications that disable garbage collection for all explicitly declared interned structs using `#[salsa::interned(revisions = usize::MAX)]` and do not use implicitly interned query arguments are not affected by this vulnerability.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/salsa [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0308.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/salsa-rs/salsa/pull/1329 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0312 — Excluded iPAddress name constraints with an all-zero mask are not applied
- Aliases: GHSA-39mm-4q6x-3vrx | Published: 2026-09-24 | Modified: 2026-09-28 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:H/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:H/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/x509-validator: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.3.1

An `excluded_subtrees` iPAddress name constraint with an all-zero mask
(`0.0.0.0/0` or `::/0`) does not restrict iPAddress SANs in certificates issued
beneath it. The mask check treated an all-zero mask as matching nothing, when a
`/0` prefix matches every address of its family, so the exclusion was silently
ignored.

CA/Browser Forum Baseline Requirements §7.1.2.5.2 require exactly these
exclusions on every technically constrained sub-CA that may not issue for IP
addresses. As a result, anyone holding (or having compromised) the key of such
a sub-CA can issue a certificate for an arbitrary IP address, and `Validator`
with `RFC5280Policy` and `ServerIdentityPolicy` accepts it for that address. A
`permitted_subtrees` dNSName entry on the same issuer does not prevent this,
because iPAddress SANs are a different name form.

All users of `Validator` with `RFC5280Policy` are affected when a chain can
contain a name-constrained issuer with an all-zero iPAddress exclusion.

The issue is fixed in x509-validator 0.3.1 (commit
[da661f8](https://github.com/namecare/x509-validator/commit/da661f8ecee820e05d089a76ecb654ff52a2c987)).
Users should upgrade to 0.3.1 or later.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/x509-validator [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0312.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/namecare/x509-validator/commit/da661f8ecee820e05d089a76ecb654ff52a2c987 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0310 — Various panics, soundness and resource exhaustion issues
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-25 | Modified: 2026-09-25 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/domain: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.12.3

Version 0.12.3 fixes a large number of panics, soundness issues, and
CPU and memory exhaustion issues in all parts of the crate.

The following is a summary of the issues per affected components.
For details, please see the [release
notes](https://github.com/NLnetLabs/domain/releases/tag/v0.12.3).

## Base

* Limited the number of compression pointers that are followed when parsing
  a compressed name to 255 to avoid following really long chains.
* Limited the length of CNAME chains followed by `Message::canonical_name`
  to 20. This also fixed an integer overflow in this method when ANCOUNT
  was`u16::MAX`.
* Changed `QuestionSection::answer` to skip over all remaining questions
  without parsing the QNAMEs to avoid being slowed down by malicious
  names.
* Added length checks when scanning and parsing variable length record
  data types. This fixes issues with the message builder, which assumes that
  record data is never too large for placing in a message.

## Stub Resolver

* Fixed possible panics in the resolver’s `FoundHosts::qname` and
  `FoundHosts::canonical_name`.
* Fixed various issues in SRV processing.

## Zonefile parsing

* Fixed soundness issues when reading UTF-8 and an integer overflow when
  reading unsigned integers.
* Fixed a panic when reading empty TXT records.

## Client and server transports, DNSSEC signer and validator

Fixed various panics that could be triggered by malicious incoming DNS
messages.

## Zonetree

Fixed various panics and a memory exhaustion issue when updating the zone.

## Acknowledgements

We would like to thank Qifan Zhang, Palo Alto Networks,
Antoni (Tony) Jagodka ([@DrVelvetFog](https://github.com/DrVelvetFog)),
and [@SebastiaanYN](https://github.com/SebastiaanYN) for
reporting issues fixed in this release.

### RUSTSEC-2026-0311 — Stack overflow on deeply nested LaTeX input
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-27 | Modified: 2026-09-28 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/latex-rust: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:1.0.5

The parser and layout engine in affected versions of `latex-rust` recurse once
per level of nesting with no limit. Deeply nested input, such as `\frac{1}{…}`,
braces, `\sqrt{…}`, `\left(`, or superscripts nested hundreds or thousands of
levels deep, exhausts the call stack.

A stack overflow in Rust aborts the process. It is not a panic, so it cannot be
caught with `catch_unwind`. A program that renders LaTeX from untrusted input
can therefore be terminated by a short string. With a release build on an 8 MiB
stack, 700 nested `\frac{1}{…}` (about 7 KB of input) abort version 1.0.4, and
10,000 nested braces (about 20 KB) abort it as well. The threshold is lower on
smaller stacks and in debug builds; on a 2 MiB thread in a debug build, about
35 nested fractions are enough.

```rust
let mut src = String::from("1");
for _ in 0..700 {
    src = format!("\\frac{{1}}{{{src}}}");
}
let _ = latex_rust::parse(&src); // stack overflow, process aborts
```

The fixed versions count nesting depth in the parser and in `layout` and
refuse input that nests more than 32 levels. `parse`, `parse_with_colors`, and
the `latex_to_*` functions return
`ParseError::Malformed("input nests deeper than 32 levels")`, and `layout`
returns `Error::Unsupported { what: "tree nests deeper than 32 levels" }` for a
tree built by hand. The count is of parser recursion levels, and a braced
argument costs two, so the limit admits 15 nested `\frac`, `\sqrt`, or
`x^{…}`, and 31 nested groups, `\left…\right` pairs, or environments. Input
past the limit is refused within about 100 KiB of stack in an optimised build.

Version 1.0.5 fixes this with no API changes and is a drop-in replacement for
1.0.4; upgrade with `cargo update -p latex-rust`. It also includes the 2.0.0
rendering fixes, so rendered output differs from 1.0.4. Versions 2.0.0 and
2.0.1 contain the same fix, with the same limit and messages, together with
breaking API changes relative to 1.x, and also let callers change the limit
with `ParseOptions` and `layout_with_max_depth`.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/latex-rust [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0311.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/jscarr64/LaTeX-Rust/issues/6 [REPORT]
- https://github.com/jscarr64/LaTeX-Rust/pull/10 [WEB]
- https://github.com/jscarr64/LaTeX-Rust/releases/tag/v1.0.5 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0330 — Hybrid Encapsulation from Seed Panics on Short Seed
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-09-28 | Modified: 2026-10-07 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:L/AC:L/AT:P/PR:H/UI:N/VC:N/VI:N/VA:H/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/libcrux-kem: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.0.10

For a hybrid KEM public key of type `PublicKey::WingKemDraft06` or `PublicKey::X25519MlKem768Draft06`, the `PublicKey::encapsulate_derand` function would panic in an indexing operation on a seed input of length shorter than 32 bytes.

# Impact

Applications encapsulating with an attacker controlled seed value could be made to panic. Since the encapsulation seed should be considered a secret of the encapsulating party for the KEM to remain secure, an application should never take the seed value from a potentially attacker controlled source.

# Mitigation

With release of version `0.0.10` of `libcrux-kem` this bug has been fixed and the serialization functions return `InvalidPrivateKey` and `InvalidPublicKey` errors on invalid input buffer lengths.

We recommend users upgrade to `libcrux-kem` version `0.0.10`.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/libcrux-kem [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0330.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/celabshq/libcrux/pull/1595 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0317 — A 512-byte workbook can provoke a multi-gigabyte allocation and abort the process
- Aliases: GHSA-w5x2-6474-pqp4 | Published: 2026-09-29 | Modified: 2026-10-01 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/sheets-diff: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:3.2.0

Affected versions passed caller-supplied bytes to `calamine`'s `Xlsx::new` without first checking
that they were a ZIP archive. `Xlsx::new` tests for password protection on its first line, which
parses the input as an OLE/CFB container, and a sector-count field read from the file's own header
reaches `Vec::with_capacity` without being checked against the file's actual length. A 512-byte input
can therefore request several gigabytes; 9,261,285,372 bytes was measured.

**The oversized allocation is always attempted. Whether it aborts depends on what the allocator can
satisfy.** On a large host with overcommit the reservation is granted untouched and the call returns
an ordinary "not an xlsx file" error, which looks like a malformed file being correctly rejected.
Under a memory limit the same bytes give `memory allocation of N bytes failed` and the process
aborts, which is not a `Result` a caller can handle. Containers with a memory limit, small hosts and
CI runners are where this lands, so testing on a development machine can wrongly suggest the crate is
unaffected.

Every entry point reaches it, not only `compare_bytes`: the path- and reader-based APIs funnel through
the same internal open. Neither `Limits::default()` nor `Limits::hardened()` prevents it, because
`max_input_bytes` bounds the length of the input while the allocation's size comes from a field
inside it.

Fixed in 3.2.0, which declines input that does not begin with the ZIP magic before the parser sees
it, removing the path rather than bounding it. Callers who cannot upgrade can apply the same check
before calling this crate.

The underlying defect is in `calamine`, reported there independently as
[tafia/calamine#714](https://github.com/tafia/calamine/issues/714) and unfixed at the time of
writing. It is reachable from any crate that opens untrusted bytes with `Xlsx::new`.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/sheets-diff [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0317.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/forskscope/sheets-diff-rs/commit/fb70d62c88ca906284703c8071e51951c6ab43e2 [WEB]
- https://github.com/forskscope/sheets-diff-rs/blob/3.2.0/CHANGELOG.md#320---2026-09-29 [WEB]
- https://github.com/tafia/calamine/issues/714 [REPORT]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0492 — Client `Handler` callbacks run for channels the client never opened
- Aliases: CVE-2026-102823, GHSA-47hw-gvq5-r2gm | Published: 2026-09-30 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/russh: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.63.1

On the client side, russh passed channel messages from the server (data, extended data, EOF, close, success, failure, open failure and the exit-status, exit-signal and xon-xoff requests) to the application's `client::Handler` callbacks without checking that the channel number belonged to a channel the client had opened and the server had confirmed. The server side already performed this check.

A malicious or compromised SSH server can therefore send these messages for any channel number, including the next number the client will allocate (they are assigned in order) or one it has already closed. An application that tracks per-channel state can be made to record a false exit status, see a channel close before its real output arrives, or panic when it looks up a channel it does not know.

From 0.63.1 the client ignores channel messages for channels that are not established and confirmed, and handles an open failure only for a channel it is actually opening.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/russh [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0492.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/Eugeny/russh/commit/3430fd26ecafc0dc3705210f5f39a9119fa22774 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0523 — MAX_STREAM_DATA frame can open streams beyond the stream limit
- Aliases: GHSA-53rm-773f-4q8c | Published: 2026-09-30 | Modified: 2026-11-13 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/quinn-proto: introduced:0.11.0 fixed:0.11.19

When a MAX_STREAM_DATA frame named a peer-initiated bidirectional stream that was not open yet, `received_max_stream_data` treated the stream, and every lower-numbered stream, as opened without checking the stream ID against the limit advertised to the peer. RFC 9000 section 4.6 requires closing the connection with `STREAM_LIMIT_ERROR` in this case, and the STREAM and RESET_STREAM handlers already did so.

After the handshake, a peer can send one small MAX_STREAM_DATA frame naming a very large stream index, and `accept` then yields every stream up to that index. In `quinn`, a loop that keeps calling `accept_bi` can be handed more than 2^40 streams. Depending on the runtime, the endpoint runs out of memory, stops accepting new connections, or keeps busy indefinitely, even after the peer has gone away. Clients and servers that accept peer-initiated bidirectional streams are affected. Unidirectional streams are not.

The fix validates the stream ID in `received_max_stream_data` and adds a defensive check where streams are opened implicitly. It is in 0.11.19.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/quinn-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0523.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/quinn-rs/quinn/commit/3eb7465d9e11d7a245e99cde1a4eb872ec26d264 [WEB]
- https://github.com/quinn-rs/quinn/commit/73114d8289d42bd536c790fa00a9beed05a4e5fa [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0524 — Long Retry token can panic or exhaust memory in a QUIC client
- Aliases: GHSA-wppq-2f6r-wfvm | Published: 2026-09-30 | Modified: 2026-11-13 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/quinn-proto: introduced:0.11.0 fixed:0.11.19

A client kept the token from a Retry packet without any length limit and put it into the header of every Initial packet it sent afterwards. Initial datagrams are 1200 bytes. When the token and connection IDs left no room for the minimum payload, `PacketBuilder::new` computed a minimum packet size larger than the maximum, which only a debug assertion checked. On the next `poll_transmit`, debug builds panic, and release builds try to allocate more than 10 GiB in one call. With longer tokens the allocation fails and the process aborts.

A matching Retry packet can be built from the client's first Initial alone. The attacker can therefore be the server being contacted, or anyone who sees that Initial and can answer before the server does. Every application that makes outgoing connections with `quinn-proto` is affected, including all `quinn` clients. Servers do not process Retry packets.

The fix closes the connection with `PROTOCOL_VIOLATION` when an Initial token leaves too little room for frames. That covers Retry tokens and cached address-validation tokens, since both come from the peer. It also reserves room for CONNECTION_CLOSE after large Initial headers. Both changes are in 0.11.19.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/quinn-proto [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0524.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/quinn-rs/quinn/commit/3bba467893ceff64d6cda0b3a2c51a1429e10372 [WEB]
- https://github.com/quinn-rs/quinn/commit/d45b5136ed1d5b2b04ff6456443c0662acf1d0d3 [WEB]
- https://github.com/quinn-rs/quinn/commit/74c33ff75281d7751488e410e21e4f030782b655 [WEB]
- https://github.com/quinn-rs/quinn/commit/a6e78d1c8bf28630f1e92fbcc38f60e0a134b01a [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0538 — Receive loop can spin after a truncated datagram
- Aliases: GHSA-6pp4-4cxf-xf88 | Published: 2026-09-30 | Modified: 2026-11-13 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/quinn-udp: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.5.16 introduced:0.6.0 fixed:0.6.3

Some targets read one datagram per `recvmsg` call instead of using `recvmmsg`: Apple platforms without the `fast-apple-datapath` feature, OpenBSD, NetBSD, Solaris and illumos, and from 0.6 also DragonFly BSD, Redox and GNU/Hurd. On these targets the receive loop set up its `msghdr` only once and looked at the `MSG_TRUNC` flag before checking whether `recvmsg` had failed. After a datagram larger than the receive buffer was dropped, a following `EAGAIN` still saw the old truncation flag. The loop then kept issuing non-blocking receives instead of returning `WouldBlock`.

When the receive buffer is smaller than a datagram that arrives, a remote sender can keep the task that drives the socket busy, before any QUIC packet authentication. The loop ends only when a later datagram fits in the buffer. Whether an application is exposed therefore depends on the receive buffer size it uses. Linux, Android, FreeBSD and Windows use other receive paths and are not affected.

The fix rebuilds the header before every `recvmsg` call and returns receive errors before checking the truncation flag. It is in 0.5.16 and 0.6.3.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/quinn-udp [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0538.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/quinn-rs/quinn/commit/462865a6c33039c26076f7077c7cd7e2f997b9cd [WEB]
- https://github.com/quinn-rs/quinn/commit/6862d3dcc9a9333fc4160481b3e5e5d154d4dd36 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0441 — `pin-utils` is unmaintained
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-01 | Modified: 2026-11-05 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/pin-utils: introduced:0.0.0-0

The [upstream repository] was archived on October 1, 2026, after the
release of version 0.1.1.

For local pinning, replace `pin_mut!` with [`core::pin::pin!`], available
since Rust 1.68. For pin projection, replace `unsafe_pinned!` and
`unsafe_unpinned!` with [`pin-project`] or [`pin-project-lite`].

[upstream repository]: https://github.com/rust-lang/pin-utils
[`core::pin::pin!`]: https://doc.rust-lang.org/core/pin/macro.pin.html
[`pin-project`]: https://crates.io/crates/pin-project

### RUSTSEC-2026-0333 — Resource budgets not enforced on the typed deserialization path
- Aliases: GHSA-4xcc-23fx-w2wj | Published: 2026-10-06 | Modified: 2026-10-08 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/noyalib: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.0.53

`ParserConfig::max_events`, `max_nodes`, `max_total_scalar_bytes`,
`max_merge_keys`, `alias_anchor_ratio` and the alias jump factor were
enforced only by the two `Value` loaders. A typed target with a
default-shaped configuration is served by the streaming deserializer,
which never read those fields, so tightening any of them had no effect
on `from_str::<T>` for a struct target. The default document-length,
depth and alias-count caps were enforced on every path, so no input was
unbounded; the gap affects callers who tightened the other budgets for
hostile input.

Version 0.0.53 charges every budget on the streaming path as well and
adds cross-path parity tests.

Users who cannot upgrade can deserialize into `noyalib::Value` first and
convert with `from_value`, or rely on `max_document_length` and
`max_depth`, which were always applied on every path.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/noyalib [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0333.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/sebastienrousseau/noyalib/pull/470 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0335 — Pre-shared key mode transmits the TGK in cleartext, exposing the SRTP master key
- Aliases: GHSA-fwjg-cm92-j3hj | Published: 2026-10-09 | Modified: 2026-10-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:H/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/mykey: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:2.0.0

`mykey` implements MIKEY (RFC 3830) for SRTP key exchange. Pre-shared key mode
transmitted the TEK Generation Key (TGK) in the clear.

`MikeyMessage::new_psk_init` built its KEMAC payload with `EncAlg::Null` and
placed the TGK in `enc_data` verbatim. Anyone able to read a PSK-Init message
therefore recovers the TGK — and so the SRTP master key — **without knowing the
pre-shared key**.

The transmitted value was never even used by the receiver: `complete_psk`
re-derived the TGK from the pre-shared key and ignored `enc_data` entirely. The
field was write-only; it provided nothing and disclosed everything.

A second consequence: the message authentication key was derived from the TGK
rather than from the pre-shared key, so an attacker who read the TGK off the wire
could also forge a valid message MAC. Integrity was lost along with
confidentiality. That aspect is tracked separately as GHSA-xq78-wvcv-hqvj.

## Exploitability

A MIKEY message is normally distributed as an SDP `a=key-mgmt:mikey` attribute,
which in AES67 and SMPTE ST 2110 deployments is typically announced over SAP
multicast. A passive listener on the media network recovers the session key with
no active attack and no credentials.

RFC 3830 §4.2.3 permits NULL encryption only where "the underlying protocols can
guarantee security", and warns to use it with caution. An SDP file or a SAP
announcement is not such a channel.

## The fix

Pre-shared key mode now follows RFC 3830 §3.1 as key *transport*: the initiator
generates a random TGK, frames it as a Key data sub-payload (§6.13), and encrypts
it with AES-CM (§4.2.3) under an encryption key and 112-bit salt derived from the
pre-shared key (§4.1.4). The message MAC is keyed from the pre-shared key and
verified before anything is decrypted.

This changes the wire format, so both ends of an exchange must be upgraded
together. The incompatibility is silent: an old peer and a new peer complete the
exchange without error and then derive different SRTP keys.

## Workarounds

Use ephemeral Diffie-Hellman mode instead of pre-shared key mode, or carry the
PSK-Init only over a channel that is already confidential and authenticated end
to end.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/mykey [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0335.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/waxspin/mykey/pull/25 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0336 — MIKEY message MAC is never verified on receive, so forged messages are accepted
- Aliases: GHSA-xq78-wvcv-hqvj | Published: 2026-10-09 | Modified: 2026-10-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/mykey: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:2.0.0

`mykey` implements MIKEY (RFC 3830) for SRTP key exchange. No receive path
verified the MIKEY message authentication code.

`crypto::verify_mac` existed and was correct, but it was called only from unit
tests — neither `MikeyMessage::from_bytes` nor `MikeyMessage::complete_psk`
invoked it.

`new_psk_init` computed an HMAC-SHA-1-160 over the serialized message and
appended it, so the tag was present on the wire and looked like integrity
protection. It was decorative: a receiver accepted any message regardless of
whether the MAC was correct, wrong, or absent.

## Consequences

- An attacker able to inject or modify MIKEY messages in transit faced no
  integrity check. Modified RAND, SSRC or security-policy payloads were accepted
  silently. Note that this does not require a man-in-the-middle position: since
  nothing was checked, a wholly forged PSK-Init was accepted.
- Pre-shared key mode therefore provided no mutual authentication, despite the
  documentation claiming that it did. Possession of the pre-shared key was never
  actually proven by either party.
- Replayed init messages were not rejected.

The practical severity was bounded by GHSA-fwjg-cm92-j3hj (cleartext TGK), which
already let a passive observer recover the key material outright without needing
to forge anything.

## The fix

`complete_psk` now verifies the message MAC **before** any decryption, so a
message that fails authentication yields no key material at all. A `MacAlg::Null`
payload, an absent MAC and a truncated MAC are each refused rather than treated
as "no check required".

The authentication key is now derived from the pre-shared key per RFC 3830 §4.1.4
rather than from the TGK — a prerequisite, since verifying against a TGK-derived
key would have been circular while the TGK travelled in the message itself.
Optional §6.9 verification messages were also added, giving the exchange genuine
mutual authentication.

Replay protection is still absent: a captured PSK-Init can be replayed and will
be accepted, because the optional COUNTER timestamp is emitted rather than a
mandatory NTP type and no record of seen timestamps or RAND values is kept. That
gap is tracked at <https://github.com/waxspin/mykey/issues/24> and is not covered
by this advisory.

## Workarounds

Treat parsed MIKEY messages as unauthenticated input: do not rely on pre-shared
key mode for peer authentication or message integrity, and carry it over a
transport that provides both.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/mykey [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0336.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/waxspin/mykey/pull/25 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0337 — Poly1305 panics or computes a wrong tag on attacker-chosen messages of 64 bytes or more
- Aliases: GHSA-xr22-8pqp-gwfh | Published: 2026-10-09 | Modified: 2026-10-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/ic-cipher: introduced:0.2.5 fixed:0.2.20

`Poly1305` in `ic-cipher` absorbs messages of 64 bytes or more four blocks at
a time, summing four products before one reduction. From 0.2.5 to 0.2.19 the
reduction kept its carries in 32-bit integers, which four summed products can
overflow when the message's bytes are large.

This affects `Poly1305` and `ChaCha20Poly1305`, and everything built on them:
the TLS and QUIC ChaCha20-Poly1305 suites in `ic-rustls`, HPKE with that
cipher in `ic-hpke`, and the re-exports in `ic-mac` and `ironcrypto`.

What happens depends on how the crate was compiled:

- With overflow checks on (any debug build, or a release profile with
  `overflow-checks = true`) the multiplication panics, and under
  `panic = "abort"` the process ends. The tag is computed over the ciphertext
  before it is compared, so this happens before authentication and without
  the key: anyone who can deliver a ChaCha20-Poly1305 ciphertext to be opened
  can trigger it.
- With overflow checks off (Cargo's default for release builds) the
  arithmetic wraps and the tag is computed wrong on the affected messages.

Ordinary data does not reach the defect. A message of `0xff` bytes does: in
one measurement against 0.2.19, 28 of 500 `ChaCha20Poly1305::open_detached`
calls on 1,024 bytes of `0xff` under random keys and nonces panicked in a
debug build. ChaCha20-Poly1305 derives a new Poly1305 key for every nonce, so
each message is a fresh attempt.

AES-GCM and AES-GCM-SIV are not affected, and neither are messages shorter
than 64 bytes.

The flaw was corrected in 0.2.20 by carrying in 64 bits.

It was found by running Project Wycheproof's ChaCha20-Poly1305 test vectors
against the library.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/ic-cipher [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0337.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/nervosys/IronCrypto/commit/a8160a18b5c66288f05dc62f5ea2159c687b9416 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0367 —  Insecure Preservation of SUID/SGID Bits during ZIP Extraction Leading to Potential Privilege Escalation
- Aliases: GHSA-vv5m-jxxh-4627 | Published: 2026-10-09 | Modified: 2026-10-16 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:L/PR:L/UI:R/S:U/C:H/I:H/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/zip: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:9.0.0

The `zip` crate preserves privileged Unix permission bits (SUID, SGID, and sticky bits) from ZIP `external_attributes` during extraction, instead of masking them to safe permission bits. If a privileged process (for example, `root` in an installer, CI/CD pipeline, or container build) extracts an attacker-controlled ZIP archive using `ZipArchive::extract()` or the stream extraction API, files can be created as setuid-root or setgid. This behavior allows the creation of SUID/SGID files during extraction, which can lead to local privilege escalation depending on the execution environment.

For further details see: https://github.com/zip-rs/zip2/security/advisories/GHSA-vv5m-jxxh-4627

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zip [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0367.html [ADVISORY]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0368 — zip: `ZipArchive::extract` creates symbolic links whose attacker-controlled targets are outside the destination directory
- Aliases: GHSA-4v9h-p7m9-rh26 | Published: 2026-10-09 | Modified: 2026-10-16 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zip: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:9.0.0

In `zip` 9.0.0-pre2, `ZipArchive::extract` creates a symbolic link from an archive entry after validating only the output entry path. The link target bytes are passed directly to the platform symlink API, so a malicious archive can create an entry inside the destination that resolves to an attacker-chosen path outside it. This behavior was verified on macOS and Linux. On either platform, a subsequent application read or write through that extracted entry accesses the external target with the extracting process's permissions.

For further details see: https://github.com/zip-rs/zip2/security/advisories/GHSA-4v9h-p7m9-rh26

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zip [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0368.html [ADVISORY]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0369 — ZipStreamReader::extract() and ZipArchive::extract() apply Unix permissions through symlinks without confining the target
- Aliases: GHSA-4684-h2q9-2vcp | Published: 2026-10-10 | Modified: 2026-10-16 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/zip: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:9.0.0

`ZipStreamReader::extract()` applies Unix permissions through extracted symlinks without validating the symlink target stays within the destination

For further details see: https://github.com/zip-rs/zip2/security/advisories/GHSA-4684-h2q9-2vcp

References:

- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0369.html [ADVISORY]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0344 — Decompression bomb in `ruzstd`: a tiny input can exhaust memory (OOM)
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-11 | Modified: 2026-10-13 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/ruzstd: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.9.1

A crafted `.zst` input under 128 KB can force `ruzstd` to allocate several gigabytes at once, crashing the process with an out-of-memory condition. Since `ruzstd` is a decoder for untrusted input, a single small file is enough to take down anything decoding it.

The compressed size of a block was capped at 128 KB, but a compressed block's **decompressed** size was never checked against `Block_Maximum_Size = min(Window_Size, 128 KiB)` (RFC 8878). In `execute_sequences` the output was built with no running bound and no flush between blocks, so one block could expand to gigabytes — bypassing both the configured window-size limit and the block-by-block streaming that would otherwise release memory. (A related `u32` counter overflow on such a block caused an additional panic.)

This is a denial of service only; memory access stays in bounds (no corruption, no RCE).

`ruzstd` is also built as a dependency of the Rust standard library via `rustc-dep-of-std`; in the toolchain it is used only to load zstd-compressed debug symbols, so the toolchain-side impact is limited.

**Fixed in 0.9.1** (commit `d3907c9`), which enforces the per-block output bound. Upgrade to `ruzstd >= 0.9.1`.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/ruzstd [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0344.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/KillingSpark/zstd-rs/commit/d3907c960ca4a1aa44cc38340137c5679dd98f5a [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0386 — `AsyncFd::new` and related constructors violate I/O safety
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-13 | Modified: 2026-10-23 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/tokio: introduced:0.3.2 fixed:1.51.6 introduced:1.52.0 fixed:1.53.3

`tokio::io::unix::AsyncFd` provides safe constructors (`new`, `with_interest`, `try_new`, and `try_with_interest`) that accept any `T: AsRawFd` rather than requiring proof of ownership or validity of the file descriptor. Because `RawFd` implements `AsRawFd`, safe code can construct an `AsyncFd` from an arbitrary or closed file descriptor, or close/replace the underlying file descriptor while the `AsyncFd` is still alive.

This violates I/O safety in two ways:

1. `AsyncFd<T>` implements `AsFd` for all `T: AsRawFd` by calling `BorrowedFd::borrow_raw(self.as_raw_fd())`, allowing safe code to obtain an arbitrary `BorrowedFd<'_>` without owning the file descriptor.
2. Tokio's I/O driver stores a pointer to an internal allocation in the `epoll`/`kqueue` user data for registered file descriptors. If the underlying file descriptor is duplicated and then closed while an `AsyncFd` is alive, and the OS reuses that file descriptor number for a another IO resource on the same runtime, dropping the first `AsyncFd` deregisters the reused file descriptor number and frees the first while the original file description remains registered in `epoll`, leading to a use-after-free when a readiness event fires.

Starting in Tokio 1.51.6, 1.53.3, and 1.54.0, `AsyncFd::new`, `AsyncFd::with_interest`, `AsyncFd::try_new`, and `AsyncFd::try_with_interest` are deprecated in favor of the `unsafe` constructors `AsyncFd::register` and `AsyncFd::register_with_interest`. Callers must ensure that the file descriptor remains valid, open, and unchanged for the lifetime of the `AsyncFd`.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/tokio [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0386.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/tokio-rs/tokio/pull/8587 [WEB]
- https://github.com/tokio-rs/tokio/issues/8538 [REPORT]
- https://github.com/tokio-rs/tokio/issues/8539 [REPORT]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0346 — `Debug` impls for client session values expose TLS secrets
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123609, GHSA-c8m5-c952-6cm4 | Published: 2026-10-14 | Modified: 2026-11-09 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:H/PR:H/UI:R/S:U/C:H/I:N/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/rustls: introduced:0.21.0 fixed:0.23.46

The `Debug` implementations of `Tls12ClientSessionValue` and
`Tls13ClientSessionValue` were derived, and so included the TLS 1.2 master
secret or TLS 1.3 resumption PSK in their output.

Custom `ClientSessionStore` implementations that format these values with
`Debug` (for example, when logging) could disclose these secrets to readers of
the logs. This conflicts with rustls's
[key-material redaction policy](https://github.com/rustls/rustls/blob/99f2358cae2954837dbb866faf6727de75489ab9/SECURITY.md#boundary-public-api).

Ordinary connection and built-in session cache `Debug` output is redacted and
is not affected.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/rustls [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0346.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/rustls/rustls/pull/3299 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0347 — Buffered application data sent to outer server after ECH rejection
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123612, GHSA-vp88-798v-h67f | Published: 2026-10-14 | Modified: 2026-11-09 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:L/PR:H/UI:N/S:U/C:L/I:N/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/rustls: introduced:0.23.44 fixed:0.23.46

When a server rejects Encrypted Client Hello (ECH), the client completes the
handshake using the outer ClientHello and authenticates the server against the
ECH config's `public_name`. At the end of that handshake, rustls encrypted and
queued any application data buffered via `ClientConnection::writer()` *before*
queueing the `ech_required` alert.

The application correctly receives
`Error::PeerIncompatible(ServerRejectedEncryptedClientHello)`, but the next
`write_tls()` call (needed to send the alert) also sends the buffered
application data. Plaintext meant for the inner server therefore goes to the
client-facing server named by `public_name`.

That server is authenticated, so this is not exploitable by an unauthenticated
party, but it violates RFC 9849 section 6.1.6, which requires the client to
abort with `ech_required` "before sending any application data to the server".

This affects only clients that:

- use `EchMode::Enable` (GREASE ECH is not affected),
- write application data before the handshake completes, and
- connect to a server that rejects their ECH config (for example, because it is stale).

The underlying ordering bug is older, but before 0.23.44 the client checked
the server certificate against the inner server name rather than `public_name`,
so certificate verification failed first and no data was sent.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/rustls [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0347.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/rustls/rustls/pull/3300 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0348 — Brotli certificate decompression can allocate up to 1 GiB before authentication
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123610, GHSA-h2v8-394m-p7vv | Published: 2026-10-14 | Modified: 2026-11-09 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:H/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:L`
- Affected crates.io/rustls: introduced:0.23.9 fixed:0.23.46

The built-in Brotli certificate decompressor (enabled by the non-default
`brotli` crate feature) bounded the size of the decompressed output, but
accepted a peer-selected history window. Brotli's large-window extension was
enabled, so a five-byte header could cause an allocation of 1 GiB. A peer can
abort a process whose allocator cannot satisfy that request.

No trusted certificate is required by the attacker. Affected configurations are:

- clients that enable the `brotli` feature and connect to a malicious server, and
- servers that enable the `brotli` feature and request client certificates.

Applications that do not enable the `brotli` feature are not affected.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/rustls [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0348.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/rustls/rustls/pull/3297 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0349 — Unauthenticated ServerHello can downgrade later connections to classical key exchange
- Aliases: CVE-2026-123611, GHSA-w44r-93j7-qv28 | Published: 2026-10-14 | Modified: 2026-11-09 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:H/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:L/I:N/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/rustls: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.23.46

The client recorded the server-selected key exchange group for each server
name when processing the ServerHello, before the server was authenticated.
This record is used to predict which key share to send in future ClientHellos
to that server name.

A network attacker can answer one connection attempt with a ServerHello
selecting X25519, and then close the connection. Later ClientHellos for that
name carry only an X25519 key share, while `supported_groups` still lists
X25519MLKEM768 first. If the genuine server accepts that share rather than
sending a HelloRetryRequest for its preferred hybrid group, the handshake
completes with classical-only key exchange and no error. This persists for as
long as the entry stays in the client's session store.

The impact is loss of post-quantum confidentiality for affected connections
("harvest now, decrypt later"). There is no key compromise and no
authentication bypass.

The following are not affected:

- connections to servers which send a HelloRetryRequest for their preferred group,
- clients with resumption disabled (as nothing is stored), and
- other server names.

This behaviour is discussed in
[draft-ietf-tls-key-share-prediction](https://www.ietf.org/archive/id/draft-ietf-tls-key-share-prediction-01.html#name-security-considerations),
so the practical impact depends largely on server policy.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/rustls [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0349.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/rustls/rustls/pull/3355 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0352 — Filesystem sandbox escape when renaming symlinks with trailing slashes
- Aliases: CVE-2026-118688, GHSA-m9f7-9w4p-gv38 | Published: 2026-10-14 | Modified: 2026-10-29 | Severity: None

### RUSTSEC-2026-0365 —  Remote DoS through unchecked `IdList` allocation in negentropy
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-14 | Modified: 2026-10-16 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/negentropy: introduced:0.2.0 fixed:0.4.4 introduced:0.5.0 fixed:0.5.2

## Summary

The `negentropy` crate is vulnerable to denial-of-service through an unchecked, peer-controlled ID count in reconciliation messages.

With the `std` feature enabled, which is the default configuration, a small malformed message can trigger an enormous memory allocation and terminate the application.

## Details

When processing an `IdList` range, the reconciliation parser uses the peer-provided ID count to reserve memory before checking whether the message contains the corresponding IDs:

```rust
let mut their_elems: HashSet<Id> =
    HashSet::with_capacity(num_ids as usize);
```

An attacker can declare an arbitrarily large count without supplying any IDs. The allocation occurs before the parser reaches the ID-reading loop, which would otherwise reject the truncated message.

With Rust's default allocation-error behavior, a failed allocation aborts the process rather than unwinding. Consequently, `catch_unwind` cannot contain the failure.

Both `reconcile` and `reconcile_with_ids` reach the vulnerable parser.

The patched versions validate the count against the remaining message length before allocating:

```rust
if num_ids > (query.len() / ID_SIZE) as u64 {
    return Err(Error::ParseEndsPrematurely);
}
```

## Impact

An unauthenticated remote attacker can terminate an application that exposes reconciliation to untrusted peers without additional validation.

For example, a Nostr relay that forwards `NEG-OPEN` or `NEG-MSG` reconciliation payloads to the vulnerable parser can be taken down by a single client message. Clients processing messages from malicious relays are also exposed.

This specific vulnerability affects builds with the default `std` feature enabled. Builds without `std` use a `BTreeSet` and do not perform the vulnerable preallocation.

A message-size limit alone does not prevent exploitation because the malicious payload can be only a few bytes long.

## Credit

Discovered and fixed by **Alex Gleason** (<https://github.com/alexgleason>).

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/negentropy [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0365.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/nostrdevkit/negentropy/pull/14 [WEB]
- https://github.com/nostrdevkit/negentropy/commit/40f7290cbc5755a5ba3190279fbc848a97866280 [WEB]
- https://github.com/nostrdevkit/negentropy/commit/894a32c0ab9f3efd750b3cf06caee27b3f9c3f12 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0366 — Stack overflow when parsing deeply nested input in pest_grammars
- Aliases: GHSA-7632-4q2x-hvjw | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-10-16 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:L/PR:L/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:L`
- Affected crates.io/pest_grammars: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:2.9.3

## Impact

In affected versions, parsing deeply nested input with `pest_grammars::json::JsonParser` can exhaust the native stack and abort the hosting process. Applications that accept attacker-controlled JSON without suitable resource limits may therefore be vulnerable to denial of service.

The reported reproduction demonstrates this behavior with deeply nested JSON arrays. The nesting required to trigger an overflow depends on the platform, available stack space, and build configuration; there is no portable safe nesting threshold.

## Patches

Upgrade to `pest_grammars` 2.9.3 or later.

Version 2.9.3 requires the updated `pest` runtime and includes regression tests for deeply nested JSON with the optional call budget disabled. Supported std builds automatically check available native stack headroom at parser checkpoints and return a handled stack limit reached error when insufficient space is detected. This protection operates independently of `pest::set_call_limit()`. See the [2.9.3 dependencies](https://github.com/pest-parser/pest/blob/v2.9.3/grammars/Cargo.toml#L16-L18) and [deep-nesting regression tests](https://github.com/pest-parser/pest/blob/v2.9.3/grammars/src/lib.rs#L94-L215).

These checks are best-effort. They do not provide universal stack-overflow protection, apply when stack bounds cannot be determined, or provide native-stack protection in no_std builds.

## Workarounds and defense in depth

Applications unable to upgrade should reject excessive nesting before invoking the affected parser or configure a suitably conservative `pest::set_call_limit()` budget. A call budget is not a portable stack-depth guarantee.

Even after upgrading, applications parsing untrusted input should enforce workload-appropriate input, work, memory, and concurrency limits. Stack checks and call budgets do not enforce elapsed-time deadlines. Where a hard deadline or cancellation is required, use supervised parsing as described in the [security policy: Parsing Untrusted Input](https://github.com/pest-parser/pest/security/policy#parsing-untrusted-input).

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/pest_grammars [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0366.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/pest-parser/pest/pull/1212 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0370 — `zvariant` is unmaintained
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-10-17 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zvariant: introduced:0.0.0-0

The `zvariant` crate is no longer maintained as a standalone crate.

It has been merged into [`zbus`](https://crates.io/crates/zbus) starting with version `6.0.0`, as its `wire` module.

Users should migrate to [`zbus`](https://crates.io/crates/zbus), with its default features disabled if they only need the D-Bus wire format. Users of the GVariant format should migrate to [`zgvariant`](https://crates.io/crates/zgvariant).
