### RUSTSEC-2026-0373 — Missing case-insensitive matching for CSS url function
- Aliases: GHSA-x43w-36pp-96g3 | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-10-17 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/ammonia: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:4.1.8 introduced:4.2.0 fixed:4.2.4

URL filtering, such as relative URLs and URL scheme limits,
is not always applied when CSS sanitization is used.

When the inner URL is quoted, CSS `url("")` is matched case-sensitively,
when it should be matched case-insensitively.
As a result, while `url("")` gets the intended domain name filtering,
`URL("")` does not.

The following example shows how an application needs to be configured
to trigger this bug.

```rust
let mut builder = Builder::new();
// allow inline styles
builder.filter_style_properties(hashset!["background-image"]);
builder.add_generic_attributes(&["style"]);
// notice that "http" URLs aren't allowed
builder.url_schemes(["cid"].into());

// attacker-controlled, presumably
let cleaned = builder.clean(r#"<div style='background:URL("https://evil.com/ping");'>"#);

// this is the expected result
assert_eq!(String::from(&cleaned), r#"<div style="">ok</p>"#);

// this is the result that the vulnerable version actually gets
assert_eq!(String::from(&cleaned), r#"<div style="background-image:URL(&quot;https://evil.com/ping&quot;);">ok</p>"#);
```

This can be used to inject tracking pixels where the application intended to prevent them.
It does not allow XSS, and its usefulness for data exfiltration is limited.

---

This vulnerability was reported by [Louis Li](https://github.com/zhixuli0406).

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/ammonia [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0373.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/rust-ammonia/ammonia/pull/267 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0391 — Data from an abandoned stream leaks into the next stream on a reused context
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd: introduced:0.13.2 fixed:0.13.4 introduced:0.14.0-0 fixed:0.14.3

The `with_context` constructors of the stream encoders and decoders used the
borrowed context as it was. When a stream was abandoned on a context, for
example an encoder dropped before `finish()` after an error, whatever it
left there was carried into the next stream: the input buffered by the
previous encoder (up to 128 KiB, or all of it with `zstdmt`) became the
start of the next frame, and a decoder returned undelivered output of the
previous stream.

An application reusing one context for data that must stay separate, such
as different users' requests, could disclose one user's plaintext to
another. Whoever supplies the data can cause the abandonment with invalid
input.

The flaw was corrected by resetting the context's session in
`with_context`.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0391.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/74ace2dcdca62a2ce0f034895d6079458311e003 [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0392 — Uninitialized memory exposed by `write::Decoder` on a corrupt legacy frame
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-10-26 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd: introduced:0.4.22 fixed:0.8.0

From 0.4.22 to 0.7.x, the writer behind `zstd::stream::write::Decoder` set
its output buffer's length to its full capacity before each decompression
call, then to the position the call reported, and only checked for an error
afterwards. When decompression fails inside a legacy (zstd v0.4 to v0.7)
frame, the bundled zstd library reports the output as fully written although
nothing was written, so stale heap memory, up to the buffer size (32 KiB by
default), was passed to the wrapped writer.

Legacy support is enabled by default, and a few bytes of corrupt legacy
frame are enough. Whether this reaches an attacker depends on where the
application sends the decompressed output.

From 0.8.0, the writer uses zstd-safe's buffers, and the same flaw there is
covered by the zstd-safe advisory for it, fixed in zstd-safe 8.2.0.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0392.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/006e1d1ad1eeaf6b5d4c28329e70217adabcec43 [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0393 — Out-of-bounds read when training a dictionary from inconsistent sample sizes
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-10-26 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd: introduced:0.1.1 fixed:0.4.10

`ZDICT_trainFromBuffer` is never told the length of the samples buffer.
Before 0.4.10, `zstd::dict::from_continuous` (initially `train_dict`) called
it directly: up to 0.2.1 without checking the sample sizes against the
buffer at all, and from 0.3.0 with a sum that wraps around when overflow
checks are off, as in release builds. Sizes that do not describe the buffer
made the trainer read past its end.

This requires the sample sizes to come from an untrusted source. From
0.4.10, training goes through zstd-safe, where the same flaw is covered by
the zstd-safe advisory for it, fixed in zstd-safe 8.2.0.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0393.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/781ea454191dbf178d09c1daf6fc896d15fbaa2f [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0395 — Use-after-free with `CParameter::StableInBuffer`
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd-safe: introduced:4.1.0 fixed:7.4.0 introduced:8.0.0-0 fixed:8.2.0

With `CParameter::StableInBuffer(true)` (`experimental` feature), the C
library keeps a pointer to the input of one streaming call and reads from it
in later calls, including `flush_stream` and `end_stream`, which take no
input. An `InBuffer` only borrows its slice for one call, so safe code could
free it in between, and freed memory was compressed into the frame. The C
library behaves this way since zstd 1.5.4, whether bundled or linked from the
system.

The flaw was corrected by refusing to enable the parameter.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd-safe [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0395.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/53a8a290d8cfb52ff5b29fb6e04a40b5a174cc65 [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0396 — `Seekable` reads a NULL or out-of-bounds seek table
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd-safe: introduced:7.2.2 fixed:7.4.0 introduced:8.0.0-0 fixed:8.2.0

`Seekable` did not record whether it was initialized. On a fresh `Seekable`,
or one whose `init_buff()` failed, `decompress`, `offset_to_frame_index` and
`frame_decompressed_size` made the C library read a NULL seek table. After a
failed re-initialization, `decompress` returned data from the refused buffer
using the previous seek table.

Separately, `frame_decompressed_size(num_frames())`, on `Seekable` and
`SeekTable`, read one entry past the end of the seek table.

The flaw was corrected by refusing to use a `Seekable` until an
initialization succeeded, and by checking the frame index.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd-safe [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0396.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/a9626069d6f2e90b782b8f627f57a361cfe8ef85 [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0397 — `OutBuffer::around_pos` exposes uninitialized memory
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd-safe: introduced:4.0.0 fixed:7.4.0 introduced:8.0.0-0 fixed:8.2.0

`OutBuffer::around_pos` accepted a position past the initialized length of a
`Vec<u8>`. The next streaming call then marked everything before the
position as initialized, so safe code could read stale heap memory between
the vector's previous length and that position.

The flaw was corrected by zero-filling that gap.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd-safe [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0397.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/5827de2459b8ac430e87e860f00c7f3c13023833 [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0398 — `Seekable::decompress` sets a `Vec` length beyond its capacity for offsets past the end
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd-safe: introduced:7.2.2 fixed:7.4.0 introduced:8.0.0-0 fixed:8.2.0

`ZSTD_seekable_decompress` shortens the requested length to the end of the
data without checking that the offset is before it. For an offset past the
end, it returns a wrapped-around length without writing anything, and
`Seekable::decompress` passed that length to `Vec::set_len`.

With the `seekable` feature, whoever supplies the archive, or chooses the
offset, can leave the caller's `Vec<u8>` with a length far beyond its
capacity. Reading it then discloses neighbouring heap memory, and appending
to it corrupts memory. Offsets near `u64::MAX` can also hang the call.

The flaw was corrected by clamping requests to the end of the data, and by
checking every count reported by the C library against the buffer capacity.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd-safe [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0398.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/80506be743fae2ed0c2fe8b21c374c6a2eab0be7 [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0399 — Use-after-free through `init_using_cdict` and `init_using_ddict`
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd-safe: introduced:3.0.0 fixed:7.4.0 introduced:8.0.0-0 fixed:8.2.0

`CCtx::init_using_cdict` and `DCtx::init_using_ddict` (`experimental`
feature, deprecated) took the dictionary as `&CDict<'b>` and `&DDict<'b>`
with only `'b: 'a`, so the dictionary itself could be dropped while the
context still pointed at it. The same mistake in other constructors was
fixed earlier (<https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/issues/365>).

The flaw was corrected by taking `&'a CDict<'b>` and `&'a DDict<'b>`.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd-safe [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0399.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/e00e7928e3e98953fc43418af6d73f6aae2378fb [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0400 — Use-after-free of a dictionary after a one-shot call on a context with an open stream
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd-safe: introduced:3.0.1 fixed:7.4.0 introduced:8.0.0-0 fixed:8.2.0

The one-shot `DCtx` and `CCtx` methods assumed the C library resets the
context, but it leaves an open stream as it is. When a stream was left
unfinished on a context, a one-shot call such as `decompress_using_dict`
could leave the context pointing at the dictionary borrowed for that call,
and the next streaming call resumed the stream against it after it was
freed: decompressed output then contained freed heap memory. On the
compression side, the stream resumed against the one-shot call's buffers.

This requires an application to use one context for both streaming and
one-shot calls without a reset in between. The flaw was corrected by
resetting the session after one-shot calls whenever a stream may be open.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd-safe [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0400.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/d34502a0ed07f80ab2aa4c65059578e0466f2467 [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0401 — Out-of-bounds read in `train_from_buffer` when sample sizes overflow
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd-safe: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:7.4.0 introduced:8.0.0-0 fixed:8.2.0

`ZDICT_trainFromBuffer` is never told the length of the samples buffer, so
`train_from_buffer` checked that the sample sizes add up to it. With
overflow checks off, as in release builds, that sum wrapped around: sizes
totalling 2^64 plus the buffer length passed the check, and the trainer read
past the end of the buffer. `zstd::dict::from_continuous` relies on this
check from zstd 0.4.10; older versions of zstd had their own, covered by a
separate advisory.

This requires the sample sizes to come from an untrusted source. The flaw
was corrected by summing the sizes with overflow checks.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd-safe [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0401.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/781ea454191dbf178d09c1daf6fc896d15fbaa2f [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0402 — Heap buffer overflow in `CCtx::try_clone` on a multithreaded context
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd-safe: introduced:4.1.4 fixed:7.4.0 introduced:8.0.0-0 fixed:8.2.0

`CCtx::try_clone` (`experimental` feature, deprecated) copies tables sized
from the context's applied parameters. Starting a multithreaded frame
(`zstdmt` feature) replaces those parameters with the workers' without
resizing the context's own tables, so after an abandoned single-threaded
frame, the copy wrote up to 512 KiB past a heap allocation, or read past
one. This became reachable with the bundled zstd 1.5.6.

The flaw was corrected by refusing `try_clone` (and the unsafe
`compress_block`) once `NbWorkers` was set on the context.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd-safe [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0402.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/682b8b2967cf4aba5cfe2d940d1e381a3e23211a [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0403 — Uninitialized memory exposed when streaming decompression of a legacy frame fails
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd-safe: introduced:4.0.0 fixed:7.4.0 introduced:8.0.0-0 fixed:8.2.0

When decompression fails inside a legacy (zstd v0.4 to v0.7) frame, the
bundled zstd library reports the output buffer as fully written although
nothing was written. `DCtx::decompress_stream` committed that position even
on error, and for a `Vec<u8>` destination this extended the vector's length
over uninitialized memory.

Legacy support is enabled by default. A 5- or 6-byte corrupt legacy frame is
enough: through `zstd::stream::write::Decoder`, up to 32 KiB of stale heap
memory is then passed to the wrapped writer on the next `write()` or
`flush()`, or when an `auto_flush()`/`on_flush()` decoder is dropped. Whether
this reaches an attacker depends on where the application sends the
decompressed output. `zstd::stream::read::Decoder`, `zstd::decode_all` and
the bulk API are not affected.

Before zstd-safe 4.0.0, output buffers could only be initialized slices.
The zstd crate's own writer had the same flaw from 0.4.22 to 0.7.x, which is
covered by a separate advisory.

The flaw was corrected by only committing the buffer positions of a
successful call.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd-safe [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0403.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/006e1d1ad1eeaf6b5d4c28329e70217adabcec43 [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0404 — Double free in `DCtx::try_clone`
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd-safe: introduced:4.1.4 fixed:7.4.0 introduced:8.0.0-0 fixed:8.2.0

`DCtx::try_clone` (`experimental` feature, deprecated) is a plain copy of the
C context. A context owning a dictionary (from `load_dictionary`,
`ref_prefix` or `init_using_dict`) or a dictionary table (`ref_ddict` with
`RefMultipleDDicts`) ended up with both copies freeing it. A context cloned
mid-frame resumed on uninitialized stream state. The clone also silently lost
the `WindowLogMax` limit.

The flaw was corrected by refusing to clone such contexts, and by carrying
over the parameters the copy leaves out.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd-safe [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0404.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/cee6529b553ec600a8e152aaca15a91598820ecf [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0405 — Out-of-bounds read with an out-of-range `InBuffer::pos`
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd-safe: introduced:7.2.2 fixed:7.4.0 introduced:8.0.0-0 fixed:8.2.0

`InBuffer`'s fields are public, so safe code can set `pos` past the end of
`src`. The position was passed to the C library unchecked, and
`ZSTD_seekable_compressStream`, unlike the other streaming functions, does
not validate it: it read past the end of the input slice, by up to the
frame size, and compressed that memory into the output.

The flaw was corrected by checking the position before every call into the
C library.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd-safe [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0405.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/20d397c0d68d8b4300c5493875ebfa10e355fcdd [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0406 — Use-after-free when changing a multithreaded context with an unfinished frame
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd-safe: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:7.4.0 introduced:8.0.0-0 fixed:8.2.0

When a multithreaded frame (`zstdmt` feature, `CParameter::NbWorkers`) is
left unfinished, its jobs may still be running on the worker threads: the C
library only waits for them when the next frame starts, or when the context
is freed. A session reset makes the context accept changes again in the
meantime, so:

- unloading the dictionary (`load_dictionary`, `ref_cdict`, `ref_prefix`,
  `disable_dictionary`, `init*`, or `reset` with parameters) freed the
  dictionary a worker was still compressing against;
- changing `NbWorkers` made the next frame resize the job table before
  waiting for the old jobs.

The flaw was corrected by refusing these calls while a multithreaded frame
is unfinished. The new `CCtx::wait_for_workers()` waits for the workers
first.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd-safe [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0406.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/e224018a2389915d68ed17f30109bebad36e9734 [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0410 — WebAssembly allocator shim corrupts memory on allocation failure
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-16 | Modified: 2026-10-26 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zstd-sys: introduced:2.0.9 fixed:2.1.2

On wasm32, zstd-sys provides `malloc` and `free` to the C library through a
shim over Rust's allocator. The shim did not check for allocation failure:
it wrote its size header to address 0 and returned address 4, which the C
library used as a valid buffer. `free(NULL)` also read below address 0.

Whenever the instance cannot grow its memory by what a frame asks for, a
small crafted frame (a 9-byte header declaring a 128 MiB window is enough)
made decompression write decoded data over the bottom of the module's
linear memory, instead of failing. The corruption stays within the wasm
sandbox.

The flaw was corrected by returning NULL on allocation failure or size
overflow, and by making `free(NULL)` a no-op.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zstd-sys [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0410.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/commit/165d1625553c236b9700ca794b8a7c51f5d98314 [WEB]
- https://github.com/gyscos/zstd-rs/pull/384 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0548 — `ZipStreamReader` extracts entries under local-header names that can differ from the central directory
- Aliases: CVE-2026-115905, GHSA-m665-v7pm-7grj | Published: 2026-10-17 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/zip: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:9.0.5

A ZIP archive names each entry twice: in the local file header in front of the entry's data, and in the central directory at the end of the archive. `ZipArchive` lists and opens entries by their central-directory names. `ZipStreamReader::visit` and `ZipStreamReader::extract`, like `read_zipfile_from_stream`, read the local headers in order and use the local-header names without comparing them with the central directory.

An archive can therefore show one set of file names to code that inspects it with `ZipArchive` and write files under other names when it is extracted with `ZipStreamReader`. If an application checks an archive one way and extracts it the other way, an attacker can get files written or overwritten inside the destination directory that the check never saw.

The fix adds an opt-in check. `ZipStreamReader::enforce_header_consistency(true)` compares each local header with its central-directory entry (name, sizes, CRC-32, compression method, flags and modification time) and fails extraction on a mismatch; `ZipReadOptions::enforce_header_consistency(true)` and `ZipArchive::validate_header_consistency()` do the same for `ZipArchive`. The check is off by default, so upgrading alone does not change what `ZipStreamReader::new(...)` extracts: applications that extract untrusted archives with `ZipStreamReader` need to turn it on.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zip [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0548.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/zip-rs/zip2/commit/8068ab480ccf8daa7411424e365fcdbe6d09443e [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0414 — Exponential event growth with `ENABLE_WIKILINKS`
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-18 | Modified: 2026-10-28 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/pulldown-cmark: introduced:0.13.0 fixed:0.13.5

When `Options::ENABLE_WIKILINKS` is enabled, a wikilink with a pipe separator
and empty display text, such as `[[a|]]`, leaks its child node's sibling chain
into the content that follows it. Each such link roughly doubles the number of
events the parser yields for the rest of the document, so output grows
exponentially with input size: 16 repetitions of `[[a|]] ` (112 bytes) yield
327,676 events, where the fixed parser yields 65. A document of a few kilobytes
is enough to exhaust memory in a process that collects the events or renders
them to HTML.

Applications that parse untrusted Markdown with `ENABLE_WIKILINKS` set,
including through `Options::all()`, are affected. The option is off by default.
Parsing without it is not affected.

The flaw was fixed in
[#1102](https://github.com/pulldown-cmark/pulldown-cmark/pull/1102) and
released in 0.13.5.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/pulldown-cmark [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0414.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/pulldown-cmark/pulldown-cmark/issues/1179 [REPORT]
- https://github.com/pulldown-cmark/pulldown-cmark/pull/1102 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0381 — Bundled root certificates allow trust in distrusted emSign intermediates
- Aliases: BIT-golang-2026-111943, CVE-2026-111943, GO-2026-6809, RUSTSEC-2026-0382, RUSTSEC-2026-0383 | Published: 2026-10-19 | Modified: 2026-10-29 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/webpki-roots: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.26.11 introduced:0.26.12-0 fixed:1.0.10

Affected versions include `emSign ECC Root CA - G3`, which can allow certificate
paths through distrusted emSign intermediates.

Affected versions may accept certificate paths containing distrusted emSign
intermediate public keys. The private keys corresponding to these intermediates
are now well known.

Version 1.0.10+ removes this root.

The `webpki-roots` 0.26.11 compatibility package contains no independent trust
store and re-exports the roots from its separately resolved 1.x dependency.
That parent dependency must resolve to 1.0.10 or newer; an older parent remains
affected by this advisory.

### RUSTSEC-2026-0453 — Use-after-free in KV backends via leaked `affinitypool::spawn_local` future
- Aliases: CVE-2026-121417, GHSA-pm86-cc75-q463 | Published: 2026-10-19 | Modified: 2026-11-06 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:H/PR:L/UI:N/S:C/C:H/I:H/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/surrealdb: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:3.3.0

The `kv-mem`, `kv-surrealkv` and `kv-rocksdb` backends move non-`'static` borrowed state into affinity-pool closures through `affinitypool::spawn_local`: a `tokio::sync::MutexGuard<Option<TransactionInner>>`, `&Transaction`, `&mut ScanStateKeys/Vals`, `&mut dyn KeyVisitor/ValVisitor`, `RwLockReadGuard`, and a `ReadOptions` holding a raw `rocksdb_snapshot_t*`. In `affinitypool 0.7.0` `spawn_local` is safe and erases the `'pool` lifetime via `async_task::Builder::spawn_unchecked`; the only thing keeping it sound is the `Drop` implementation of the returned future, which blocks until the worker stops using the borrows.

Safe code can leak that future (`mem::forget`, `Box::leak`, `ManuallyDrop`, a reference cycle, or a leaked enclosing future). The borrows then end while the pool worker still uses them, producing a use-after-free on the transaction allocation; AddressSanitizer reports a read of freed mutex data and a write when the worker unlocks the freed `tokio::sync::Mutex` on the pool thread. No `unsafe` is required at the call site, and the affected methods include `count`, `keys`/`keysr`, `scan`/`scanr`, `compact`, shutdown, point reads and writes, and cursor iteration.

3.3.0 upgrades to `affinitypool 0.8.0`, where `spawn_local` is an `unsafe` function and the caller must uphold the leak-freedom invariant (commit `8156ff69`).

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/surrealdb [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0453.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/surrealdb/surrealdb/commit/8156ff691b1861814199e5e3372c81609b68db7f [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0534 — Unbounded backoff state from PRUNE messages for unknown topics
- Aliases: GHSA-vrx6-jcp8-vm7m | Published: 2026-10-19 | Modified: 2026-11-13 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/libp2p-gossipsub: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.50.1

A PRUNE control message names topics and asks for a backoff on each. The handler recorded a backoff for every named topic, including topics the local node had never joined. A peer can send PRUNEs naming many new short topic IDs with a long backoff. Each pair is kept until the backoff expires, up to an hour, even after the peer disconnects, so a stream of PRUNEs, possibly from many peer IDs, can use up the node's memory. Peer scoring does not penalise these messages.

The fix ignores PRUNE entries for topics that are not in the node's mesh. It is in 0.50.1, and was backported to the main branch in libp2p/rust-libp2p#6707.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/libp2p-gossipsub [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0534.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/libp2p/rust-libp2p/commit/9c647828abea0dfc0b4e827dff1e176ec8b44752 [WEB]
- https://github.com/libp2p/rust-libp2p/pull/6707 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0388 — Pingora HTTP/2 :authority and Host header mismatch enables cache poisoning and host-based access control bypass
- Aliases: CVE-2026-105298, GHSA-mgg9-6xw2-jfgp | Published: 2026-10-20 | Modified: 2026-10-26 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:P/PR:N/UI:N/VC:N/VI:N/VA:N/SC:L/SI:L/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/pingora-core: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.9.0

Pingora versions prior to 0.9.0 accepted HTTP/2 requests whose Host header differed from the `:authority` pseudo-header. Request filters, cache keys, and logs that read the request URI (`session.req_header().uri`) saw the `:authority` value, but the upstream received the client-supplied Host value. Pingora also accepted `:authority` values containing userinfo (for example, `user@example.com`). This violates RFC 9113 Section 8.3.1, which requires an intermediary to use the `:authority` value for Host and replace any existing Host field.

A remote, unauthenticated attacker can send a single HTTP/2 request that the application sees as one host and the upstream receives as another. Where request filters, access controls, or cache keys use the URI authority and the upstream routes by Host, this can lead to cache poisoning, where a response from another virtual host is stored under the victim host's cache key and served to other users, or host-based access control bypass, where a request approved for one host is served by a different virtual host on the upstream. Deployments that do not accept HTTP/2 from clients, or that do not use the request authority for security or caching decisions, are not affected.

This flaw was corrected in commits 915590a91811877795531f6ba0927a3138b5cb4b and ffab8302d08d4292d1924eb8fe84647fd5cb8e76. Pingora now returns 400 Bad Request for HTTP/2 requests whose Host differs from `:authority`, that contain more than one Host, or whose authority contains userinfo, and keeps the upstream Host or `:authority` consistent with the request authority. Users should upgrade to Pingora >= 0.9.0.

Note: Cloudflare CDN customers were unaffected, as internal Pingora services at Cloudflare do not have HTTP/2 clients.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/pingora-core [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0388.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/cloudflare/pingora/commit/915590a91811877795531f6ba0927a3138b5cb4b [WEB]
- https://github.com/cloudflare/pingora/commit/ffab8302d08d4292d1924eb8fe84647fd5cb8e76 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0389 — Pingora HTTP/2 server memory exhaustion through unbounded default header-list and concurrent-stream limits
- Aliases: CVE-2026-105297, GHSA-7gpj-mj4q-x62x | Published: 2026-10-20 | Modified: 2026-10-26 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:N/PR:N/UI:N/VC:N/VI:N/VA:H/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/pingora-core: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.8.1

Pingora versions prior to 0.8.1 used the h2 crate's default HTTP/2 server limits, a 16 MiB decoded header-list size and no limit on concurrent streams, unless the application set its own. A remote, unauthenticated client can send 1-byte HPACK references to a near-empty header, making the server allocate hundreds of thousands of header fields per stream across an unlimited number of streams on one connection, and use HTTP/2 flow control to keep those streams open. This can exhaust memory and degrade or terminate the Pingora process.

This affects any Pingora service that terminates HTTP/2 (h2 or h2c) without explicitly setting `max_header_list_size` and `max_concurrent_streams`. The reporter showed that a single connection could get a Pingora process with a 2 GiB memory limit OOM-killed.

This flaw was corrected in commit d193c8d49b8b7c1c1ede93183759caa4f6906bbd. When no `H2Options` are supplied, Pingora now advertises and enforces a 64 KiB decoded header-list limit and at most 100 concurrent streams per connection. Users should upgrade to Pingora >= 0.8.1. Applications that supply their own `H2Options` are not covered by these defaults and must set `max_header_list_size` and `max_concurrent_streams` explicitly, or start from `pingora_core::protocols::http::v2::server::default_h2_options()`. `H2Options::default()` does not include these limits.

Note: Cloudflare CDN customers were unaffected, as internal Pingora services at Cloudflare do not have HTTP/2 clients.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/pingora-core [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0389.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/cloudflare/pingora/commit/d193c8d49b8b7c1c1ede93183759caa4f6906bbd [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0390 — Pingora forwards client-nominated Connection headers to HTTP/1 upstreams, allowing ACL bypass in specific chained deployments
- Aliases: CVE-2026-105299, GHSA-vx4q-2c55-cvmg | Published: 2026-10-20 | Modified: 2026-10-26 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:P/PR:N/UI:N/VC:N/VI:N/VA:N/SC:H/SI:H/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/pingora-proxy: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.9.0

Pingora versions prior to 0.9.0 passed the downstream request through `upstream_request_filter` and wrote it to HTTP/1 upstreams without removing the client's Connection header, other hop-by-hop headers, or the headers Connection nominated, although RFC 9110 Section 7.6.1 requires intermediaries to remove them. The HTTP/2 upstream path already removed Connection and related HTTP/1-specific headers. A client could therefore nominate a header that the application adds in `upstream_request_filter` (for example, `Connection: keep-alive, X-Forwarded-For`). A downstream HTTP/1 intermediary that honors Connection would then strip the Pingora-added header before it reached the origin.

This specifically affects chained deployments where an HTTP/1 intermediary between Pingora and the origin processes Connection nominations (for example, Go's `net/http/httputil.ReverseProxy`) and the origin trusts headers added by that intermediary (for example, X-Forwarded-For for IP-based authorization). A remote, unauthenticated attacker can have those headers removed, potentially bypassing authorization. This is the same class of issue as CVE-2022-31813 in Apache HTTP Server.

This flaw was corrected in commits 28c18e6b6b04dd0bd937234e5950049e6d74cded and ca23f1668eca8e78be7ba62ef0edbd458b40eeee. By default, Pingora now removes hop-by-hop and Connection-nominated headers from upstream requests, and rejects requests whose Connection header nominates Host, X-Forwarded-For, X-Forwarded-Host, X-Forwarded-Proto, or a pseudo-header. This is controlled by `PeerOptions::http_upstream_request_policy`; `HttpUpstreamRequestPolicy::preserve()` restores the old passthrough behavior and should not be used if the origin relies on headers added by Pingora. Users should upgrade to Pingora >= 0.9.0.

Note: Cloudflare CDN customers were unaffected, as no such chained deployment as described above exists with internal Pingora services, and Connection headers are sanitized before arriving at Pingora gateways.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/pingora-proxy [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0390.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/cloudflare/pingora/commit/28c18e6b6b04dd0bd937234e5950049e6d74cded [WEB]
- https://github.com/cloudflare/pingora/commit/ca23f1668eca8e78be7ba62ef0edbd458b40eeee [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0461 — Unbounded tool schema cache in Streamable HTTP server transport
- Aliases: GHSA-4432-vg92-fqh6 | Published: 2026-10-20 | Modified: 2026-11-09 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/rmcp: introduced:3.0.0 fixed:3.6.0

From 3.0.0 until 3.6.0, `StreamableHttpService` cached the result of
`ServerHandler::get_tool` in a process-wide map keyed by the tool name of
incoming `tools/call` requests. The map had no size limit and no eviction, and
it also cached unknown tool names.

An entry was added while the request headers were validated, before the request
could be rejected, so requests answered with HTTP 400 still grew the map. A
client that can reach the endpoint can send `tools/call` requests with distinct
tool names, each up to the default 4 MiB body limit, until the server runs out
of memory. rmcp does not authenticate requests itself, so servers without an
authentication layer in front of the endpoint are exposed to unauthenticated
clients.

Only servers that use the Streamable HTTP server transport and receive requests
at protocol version `2026-07-28` or later are affected.

The flaw was corrected in 3.6.0 by removing the cache. The transport now calls
`ServerHandler::get_tool` on each request.

If you cannot upgrade, put authentication in front of the endpoint, or reject
`tools/call` requests for unknown tool names before they reach
`StreamableHttpService`.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/rmcp [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0461.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/rust-sdk/pull/1349 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0384 — Race condition in `tokio::time::Interval` on Tokio v1.54.0
- Aliases: CVE-2026-115567, GHSA-5wjr-9356-4wc8 | Published: 2026-10-21 | Modified: 2026-10-24 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/tokio: introduced:1.54.0 fixed:1.54.1

## Summary

On the multi-threaded runtime, there is a race condition between `Interval::poll_tick` and the runtime's time driver.

This affects `Interval` only. `Sleep` and `Timeout` are **not** affected.

## Details

In Tokio v1.54.0, a race condition between `tokio::time::Interval::poll_tick` (including `.tick().await`) and the runtime time driver can drop an internal timer entry without synchronizing with the driver thread that is firing it. This causes a data race and use-after-free on the timer's waker and task state in safe Rust, leading to assertion panics, task reference-count corruption, or heap memory corruption.

This bug was introduced by https://github.com/tokio-rs/tokio/pull/8458, and it was fixed by https://github.com/tokio-rs/tokio/pull/8692.

## Impact

This may lead to crashes and use-after-frees in applications using `Interval` on a multi-threaded runtime.

Since this issue is known to lead to crashes in production systems, the v1.54.0 release of Tokio has been yanked.

## Credits

Thank you to [creepycode](https://github.com/ByteZ1337) and [carabistouille](https://github.com/carabistouflette) for reporting this privately using the procedure outlined in our security policy. They both reported the issue independently of each other.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/tokio [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0384.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/tokio-rs/tokio/pull/8692 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0407 — SSRF and local file disclosure via unvalidated URLs
- Aliases: GHSA-23hp-j76v-pcwj | Published: 2026-10-22 | Modified: 2026-10-26 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:H/I:N/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/webpage: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:2.1.0

Applications that pass attacker-controlled URLs to `webpage` versions before
2.1.0 may be vulnerable to server-side request forgery (SSRF) or local file
disclosure.

`HTTP::fetch` and `Webpage::from_url` passed URLs to libcurl without
restricting the URL scheme or validating the resolved destination IP address.
Redirects were followed without checking whether the new destination was
internal. Depending on how the application uses the response, an attacker
could:

- Read local files accessible to the application process using `file://` URLs.
- Cause requests to internal or link-local services (e.g. cloud metadata
  endpoints at `http://169.254.169.254`) via direct private-IP URLs or by
  chaining HTTP redirects through an attacker-controlled server.

Starting in 2.1.0, `HTTP::fetch` and `Webpage::from_url` reject non-HTTP(S)
URL schemes and destinations that resolve to non-public IP addresses, including
after redirects. Applications that intentionally fetch trusted internal
resources can opt in to the previous behavior with `HTTP::fetch_unrestricted`
or `Webpage::from_url_unrestricted`.

Reported by [Aiden Mohan](https://github.com/AidenMohan).

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/webpage [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0407.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/orottier/webpage-rs/commit/5742fbee437c04170d101e10df3534e87730f36d [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0411 — Out-of-bounds read in `Log2Cache::nlog2n()`, `nlog2n_difference()` and `plog2p()`
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-22 | Modified: 2026-10-26 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/dahl-salso: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.7.0

`Log2Cache::new(n)` fills its three tables with entries for `0..=n`, but the
three accessor methods index those tables with `get_unchecked` on a value the
caller supplies. Nothing bounds the argument and nothing documents a bound, so
any value above the `n` the cache was built with reads past the end of the
vector. `CountType` is `u32`, so the read can land up to `u32::MAX` elements
away, and `plog2p()` returns the value it read, disclosing whatever lies there.

For example, `Log2Cache::new(4)` builds tables holding 5 entries (indices
0,1,2,3,4). Then `cache.nlog2n(60000)` reads index 60000, far outside the
allocation. Under AddressSanitizer this faults with `SEGV`; Miri reports it as
undefined behavior at `src/log2cache.rs:42`.

`nlog2n_difference()` is off by one further: in the affected versions `new(n)`
pushes `n` entries into that table rather than `n + 1`, so even
`nlog2n_difference(n)` is out of bounds.

This can be triggered through safe public APIs — `Log2Cache::new()` and any of
`nlog2n()`, `nlog2n_difference()` and `plog2p()` — with no `unsafe` required
from the caller.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/dahl-salso [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0411.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/dbdahl/rust-dahl-salso/issues/2 [REPORT]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0429 — zip: `ZipArchive::extract` aborts the process on a symlink entry with a large declared size
- Aliases: CVE-2026-115909, GHSA-q7v9-x93c-xqv3 | Published: 2026-10-22 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/zip: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:9.0.0

`ZipArchive::extract()` sizes the symlink-target buffer from the uncompressed
size declared in the central directory record, before reading a single byte of
entry data:

```rust
let mut target = Vec::with_capacity(file.size() as usize);
```

Both inputs are attacker-controlled. `ZipFile::size()` returns
`uncompressed_size` verbatim from the central directory, widened to a full `u64`
by a ZIP64 extended-information extra field, and is never cross-checked against
the compressed size, the remaining archive length, or any platform-appropriate
bound for a symlink target. `ZipFile::is_symlink()` is decided entirely by the
`S_IFLNK` bit of the external-attributes field.

`Vec::with_capacity()` is infallible: when the reservation is refused it calls
`handle_alloc_error()` and aborts the process. This is not a Rust panic. It is
not caught by `catch_unwind()`, is unaffected by `panic = "unwind"`, and is not
contained by running the call on a worker thread.

A 112-byte archive is sufficient. One `Stored` entry flagged `S_IFLNK` with a
ZIP64 field declaring `uncompressed_size = 2^62` and a zero-byte payload makes
`extract()` fail with:

```
memory allocation of 4611686018427387904 bytes failed
Aborted (core dumped)          # exit 134
```

The declared value only needs to exceed the host's address space, not its RAM:
`Vec::with_capacity()` maps address space without touching pages, so resident
memory stays flat and the abort happens on the refused reservation itself. Under
a `ulimit -v` cap or a memory-constrained container the threshold drops far
enough that a plain 32-bit `uncompressed_size` with no ZIP64 field suffices.

Applications that extract archives from untrusted input are affected on Unix and
Windows targets with default features and no special configuration: upload
handling, mail-attachment processing, package and artefact ingestion, backup
restore and container-image layer extraction. No confidentiality or integrity
property is affected: there is no memory unsafety, no out-of-bounds access, no
disclosure and no data corruption.

The fix landed in [zip-rs/zip2#984](https://github.com/zip-rs/zip2/pull/984) and
shipped in 9.0.0.

For further details see: https://github.com/zip-rs/zip2/security/advisories/GHSA-q7v9-x93c-xqv3

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zip [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0429.html [ADVISORY]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0457 — OAuth client SSRF filter checks host strings, not resolved addresses
- Aliases: GHSA-5p52-q7pq-9fq2 | Published: 2026-10-22 | Modified: 2026-11-09 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/rmcp: introduced:2.0.0 fixed:3.7.0

From 2.0.0 until 3.7.0, the rmcp OAuth client decided whether it may fetch an
authorization server's metadata URL by inspecting only the host string. It
rejected literal loopback, private and link-local IP addresses and known cloud
metadata hostnames, but it did not resolve other hostnames. A hostname that
resolves to an internal address passed the check.

A malicious MCP server can return protected-resource metadata that names an
authorization server whose hostname resolves to an internal address. The client
then sends GET requests to that address during discovery.

The same release fixes two related bypasses:

- Bracketed IPv6 literals such as `http://[::1]:6379/` were not recognized as
  internal, because the check kept the brackets from the URL host.
- `token_endpoint` and `registration_endpoint` from authorization server
  metadata were not checked at all, and token requests follow redirects. A
  malicious authorization server could make the client send POST requests to
  an internal address.

The impact is client-side SSRF. Responses are not returned to the attacker
directly. Unauthenticated internal HTTP services are reachable. Cloud metadata
endpoints that require headers or IMDSv2 are largely unaffected.

The flaw was corrected in 3.7.0. `AuthorizationManager::new` now builds its HTTP
client with a DNS resolver that applies the address policy to every resolved
address, and checks every IP-literal URL and followed redirect against the same
policy. Loopback and private addresses are allowed only when the MCP server's
own URL names one of that class. Deployments that reach their authorization
server through a hostname that resolves to a private address should use
`AuthorizationManager::new_allowing_private_addresses`.

Clients supplied through `new_with_oauth_http_client` are not covered by the
resolved-address checks. Such clients must enforce their own outbound address
policy.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/rmcp [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0457.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/rust-sdk/commit/ac808abcb40d3c90c743a097cd97c7dfc8f71b5d [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0545 — AES entries returned without checking their authentication code when the data ends early or is empty
- Aliases: CVE-2026-115906, GHSA-8hxf-qr8p-62x4 | Published: 2026-10-22 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/zip: introduced:0.6.0 fixed:9.0.0

For WinZip AES (AE-1 and AE-2) entries, the 10-byte HMAC authentication code was read and checked only after the reader had consumed exactly the entry's declared compressed size. If the archive ended before that size was reached, the read returned end-of-stream and the truncated plaintext was accepted without the check. The authentication code of an entry with no encrypted data was never checked.

AES-CTR ciphertext can be changed bit by bit without the password, and AE-2 entries carry no CRC-32, so an attacker who can modify an archive can alter the decrypted contents of such an entry without any error being reported.

This affects reading AES-encrypted entries with a password (for example `ZipArchive::by_name_decrypt`) with the `aes-crypto` feature.

The fix returns an error when the data ends before the declared size, and checks the authentication code of empty entries.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zip [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0545.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/zip-rs/zip2/commit/ec265db0b0602f8ffb9479327397bddefb7e2216 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0546 — `ZipArchive::extract` can write outside the destination through absolute or chained symlink targets
- Aliases: CVE-2026-115908, GHSA-hjqx-j4rw-g7xv | Published: 2026-10-22 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:H/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/zip: introduced:7.2.0 fixed:9.0.0

Before writing an entry whose path goes through a symlink, `ZipArchive::extract` checks the symlink's target against the destination directory. That check compares the paths as text and does not follow symlinks, and from 7.2.0 it drops the root and prefix of an absolute target before comparing. Symlinks taken from the archive were created without any check of their targets.

A crafted archive can first create a symlink that points outside the destination, then a second symlink whose target looks like a path inside the destination but goes through the first one, and then a file entry under the second symlink. The file is written outside the destination directory, for example over an existing file in the user's home directory, and `extract` still returns `Ok(())`. On Windows, a target on another drive or share whose path otherwise matches the destination passes the check directly (creating symlinks there needs Developer Mode or administrator rights). The attacker has to know or guess the absolute path of the destination directory.

This affects `ZipArchive::extract` and `ZipArchive::extract_unwrapped_root_dir`.

The fix resolves symlink targets one component at a time from the canonicalized destination, following symlinks on the way, and refuses to create a symlink, or write through one, that leads outside the destination.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zip [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0546.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/zip-rs/zip2/commit/09c030eab56e8db8fb3c1c33f554407b30bad8fa [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0547 — Legacy Shrink, Reduce and Implode decoders preallocate the declared uncompressed size
- Aliases: CVE-2026-115907, GHSA-9hj8-73fh-9963 | Published: 2026-10-22 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/zip: introduced:5.1.0 fixed:9.0.0

With the `legacy-zip` feature, the decoders for the Shrink, Reduce and Implode compression methods reserved output capacity for the entry's declared uncompressed size before decoding anything. That size comes from the archive, and a ZIP64 entry can declare any 64-bit value, so reading or extracting a crafted entry of a few bytes panics with "capacity overflow" or fails a huge allocation.

An attacker who can supply an archive can stop a process that reads it, for example through `ZipArchive::extract`.

Only builds with the `legacy-zip` feature are affected; it is not enabled by default.

The fix caps the preallocation at 1 MiB, and such an entry now returns an error.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/zip [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0547.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/zip-rs/zip2/pull/991 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0385 — Non-constant-time mask generation and `u128` comparisons
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-23 | Modified: 2026-10-23 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/ctutils: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.4.4

## Summary

The `ctutils::Choice` type has mask generation functions for each unsigned
integer size (`u8`, `u16`, `u32`, `u64`, `u128`). These are `const fn` and
therefore can't reuse the `asm!`-backed mask generation in the `cmov` crate
that's otherwise used to ensure constant-time.

In releases prior to v0.4.4, the generated code from these functions could
include a branch on commonly used platforms. This has been corrected in this
release and now verified on several platforms using BINSEC.

Additionally, `u128` comparison operations provided via `ctutils::Choice` and
intended to work in constant-time were generating branches on 32-bit targets.
This has been resolved via a special case implementation of these functions
for such targets.

## Impact

Branch instructions on secret values can potentially leak such values via
covert channels. This can enable attackers to potentially recover such secrets.

## Resolution

This has been resolved by calling `Choice::to_u8()` when generating the mask,
which in turn uses `black_box` as an optimization barrier. Verification that
this resulted in the desired change in the generated assembly across a variety
of target CPU architectures was performed by BINSEC, which now runs
automatically in CI using `cargo checkct`.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/ctutils [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0385.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/RustCrypto/utils/pull/1569 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0387 — Multiple timing variability issues in operations intended to be constant-time
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-23 | Modified: 2026-10-24 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/crypto-bigint: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.7.6

## Summary

Several operations intended to be constant-time could execute secret-dependent
branches, depending on the compiler and target CPU. These include subtraction,
zero counting, division, and Montgomery multiplication.

Additionally, `UintRef` equality could terminate at the first unequal limb,
and `BoxedMontyForm::invert` branched on whether an inverse exists.

## Impact

Applications using affected operations on secret integers may leak information
through timing or microarchitectural side channels. This includes information
about intermediate arithmetic values, matching limbs, and invertibility.

Such leakage could potentially enable recovery of cryptographic secrets.
Practical exploitability depends on the downstream algorithm, compiler, target
CPU, and an attacker's ability to observe the side channel.

## Resolution

Upgrade to v0.7.6, which removes the identified branches, updates `ctutils` to
include its constant-time fixes, and adds BINSEC-based regression checks using
`cargo checkct` for arithmetic on multiple targets.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/crypto-bigint [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0387.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/RustCrypto/crypto-bigint/pull/1365 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0408 — Aliasing violations in splice, mutable iteration and cursors
- Aliases: GHSA-924w-mwc9-86m8 | Published: 2026-10-24 | Modified: 2026-10-26 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:L/AC:L/AT:P/PR:N/UI:N/VC:L/VI:L/VA:L/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/skiplist: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:1.3.0

Four code paths create a pointer whose permission is later revoked, then
write through it. Miri reports each as undefined behaviour under both the
Tree Borrows and Stacked Borrows aliasing models (CWE-758). All four are
reachable from safe code with standard `Ord` types.

1. **Splice back-pointer.** `split_off`, `split_off_index` and `append` stored
   the first spliced node's `prev` pointer from a reference to the head, then
   wrote through that same reference, invalidating the stored pointer. A later
   `pop_first` or `pop_front` writes through it. This affects all four
   collections.
2. **`SkipMap::range_mut` back pointer.** With an `Included` or `Excluded`
   upper bound, the back pointer came from a shared reference. `next_back`
   hands out `&mut V` through it, and writing to that value is undefined
   behaviour.
3. **`IterMut::next_back` reads a yielded node.** In `SkipList` and `SkipMap`,
   `next_back` read the predecessor node even after the front end had yielded
   it as `&mut`. The read freezes the caller's reference, and the caller's
   next write through it is undefined behaviour. This affects `iter_mut`,
   `values_mut` and `range_mut` used from both ends.
4. **Cursor list pointer.** `lower_bound_mut` and `upper_bound_mut` on
   `OrderedSkipList` and `SkipSet` (`cursor` feature) took the cursor's list
   pointer, then searched through `&self`. A comparator with interior-mutable
   state, such as a `Cell` call counter, wrote to the list's memory through
   that shared borrow and invalidated the cursor's pointer. The comparator can
   be a fully consistent order. `SkipMap` cursors are not affected.

No miscompilation has been observed in an optimised build. A future compiler
may still optimise on the violated aliasing assumptions.

The flaws were corrected in 1.3.0. Each fix derives the pointer used for
mutation from the collection's long-lived allocation pointers, and each has a
Miri regression test.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/skiplist [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0408.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/JP-Ellis/rust-skiplist/pull/407 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0409 — Use-after-free from a comparator that answers inconsistently
- Aliases: GHSA-cc53-p78w-23w9 | Published: 2026-10-24 | Modified: 2026-10-26 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:P/PR:N/UI:N/VC:L/VI:L/VA:L/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/skiplist: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:1.3.0

`OrderedSkipList`, `SkipSet` and `SkipMap` can free a node that is still
linked when a `Comparator` or `Ord` implementation gives different answers for
the same pair of values during one mutating call. A later call reads the freed
memory (CWE-416). A safe `impl Ord` with the default comparator is enough to
reach it.

The mutating traversals record a precursor at each level: the last node whose
link points at a node comparing not-`Less` than the target. The splice code
assumes every recorded precursor's link points at or past the final position.
If a later comparison reports one of those nodes as `Less`, the walk steps
past it and the assumption breaks. Removals such as `take_first`, `take` and
`remove` then rewire the wrong link and free the target while its real
predecessor still points at it. `insert` builds crossed links and wrong skip
distances from the same precursors.

A comparator that is a pure function of its two arguments cannot trigger
this, even if it is not transitive. The trigger is a stateful or
non-deterministic implementation, for example one that reads a counter, a
cache, a clock or a random source.

The flaw was corrected in 1.3.0. The mutating traversals track the link target
of the lowest recorded precursor and panic with "comparator does not implement
a total order" when a `Less` answer contradicts an earlier one. Every splice
site also checks its precursors before the first write. Each detected
contradiction panics before any link, value or length changes, so the
collection stays valid and safe to drop.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/skiplist [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0409.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/JP-Ellis/rust-skiplist/issues/401 [REPORT]
- https://github.com/JP-Ellis/rust-skiplist/pull/407 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0412 — Arbitrary code execution when rerunning tests from a portable recording
- Aliases: GHSA-vc25-qg58-w57c | Published: 2026-10-26 | Modified: 2026-10-27 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:H/I:H/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/cargo-nextest: introduced:0.9.125 fixed:0.9.150

## Summary

Nextest versions between 0.9.125 and 0.9.149 (inclusive) allow the `cargo nextest run --rerun` (or `-R`) command with a malicious [portable recording](https://nexte.st/docs/features/record-replay-rerun/portable-recordings/) to run arbitrary code during the build. The result is arbitrary code execution with the privileges of the user running `cargo nextest`.

This vulnerability requires that the experimental [run recording](https://nexte.st/docs/features/record-replay-rerun/) functionality is enabled. An attacker can plausibly trick a victim into enabling this feature.

## Details

Nextest's portable recordings contain [build scope arguments](https://nexte.st/docs/design/architecture/recording-runs/#build-scope-arguments). These arguments are deserialized verbatim from portable recordings, and are [not validated](https://github.com/nextest-rs/nextest/blob/70ba2393a05f29c54b72b4badb9635a86f162eb1/nextest-runner/src/record/format.rs#L421-L426). As a result, it is possible to construct an archive with a custom `--config` argument that causes Cargo to execute arbitrary code.

Nextest 0.9.150 records the build scope as structured data, and passes each value to Cargo joined to its flag (for example, `--package=<value>`), so a recording can no longer add other Cargo options.

## Impact

A malicious portable recording leads to arbitrary code execution as the user who runs `cargo nextest run --rerun`, during the build phase, before any test runs.

- The injected configuration takes effect during `cargo build`, so the payload is run even on a warm build cache, and even when no tests are ultimately executed. Inspecting the recorded tests (with e.g. `cargo nextest replay`) before running them does not help, since the code has already run by the time the tests would start.
- Arbitrary code execution happens as the invoking user, without a prompt or trust warning. The only signal is a `rerun: inheriting build scope…` line. On CI, this includes access to whatever secrets are accessible to the job, including repository tokens and deployment credentials.
- A malicious payload can invoke the real compiler afterwards, so the build and tests complete normally. The recording is otherwise a valid, ordinary recording.
- The full Cargo `--config` surface is exposed. This includes `build.rustc-wrapper`, `build.rustc`, `target.*.linker`, `target.*.runner`, `env.*`, and `patch.*` and `source.*.replace-with` (redirecting dependencies to an attacker-controlled repository or registry).
- The delivery mechanism matches the feature's intended use, since portable recordings are designed to be shared across machines. For example, an attacker could pose as a user asking a maintainer for help triaging their flaky test report. Because `--rerun` also accepts process substitution, `cargo nextest run --rerun <(curl …)` would fetch the recording directly from a URL.

Exploitation requires convincing the victim to run `cargo nextest run --rerun` against an attacker-supplied portable recording. The rerun feature is experimental and must be enabled, via e.g. `NEXTEST_EXPERIMENTAL_RECORD=1`.

## Remediations

- Upgrade cargo-nextest to version 0.9.150 or above.
- In your repositories, set the [minimum required version](https://nexte.st/docs/configuration/minimum-versions/) to 0.9.150. In `.config/nextest.toml`:
  ```toml
  nextest-version = "0.9.150"
  ```
  This way, anyone who interacts with your repository will be notified to upgrade their version of nextest.
- If you are using an affected version, do not enable the experimental run recording feature.

## Credit

Reported by [inanna-malick](https://github.com/inanna-malick).

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/cargo-nextest [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0412.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/nextest-rs/nextest/commit/456818b35d906551b2273e635c9b343704caaeaf [WEB]
- https://github.com/nextest-rs/nextest/commit/59ef7afc7887caea50aabed289756820e08f366a [WEB]
- https://nexte.st/changelog/#0.9.150 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0413 — Use-after-free of caller-owned server name in client connection constructors
- Aliases: GHSA-rc3f-4j82-cmrg | Published: 2026-10-26 | Modified: 2026-10-27 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/rustls-ffi: introduced:0.12.0 fixed:0.15.7

In rustls-ffi 0.12.0 through 0.15.6, `rustls_client_connection_new()` and
`rustls_client_connection_new_alpn()` retained a reference to the caller's
`server_name` buffer after returning, rather than copying it. The API
documentation did not require the buffer to outlive the call and as a result
if the caller freed or reused the buffer, later TLS processing read freed
memory.

Clients that free or reuse the `server_name` buffer after the constructor
returns are affected. This is most likely for language bindings that pass
temporary strings. Callers that keep the `server_name` buffer alive for the
lifetime of the `rustls_client_config` are not affected.

Versions 0.12.0 through 0.15.6 are affected. Version 0.15.7+ changed these APIs
to copy the input buffer.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/rustls-ffi [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0413.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/rustls/rustls-ffi/releases/tag/v/0.15.7 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0486 — xattr functions mark unwritten bytes as initialized when given a zero-length buffer
- Aliases: GHSA-jh2m-fhpj-3vr7 | Published: 2026-10-27 | Modified: 2026-11-11 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/rustix: introduced:1.0.0 fixed:1.1.6

The extended-attribute functions in `rustix::fs` (`getxattr`, `lgetxattr`, `fgetxattr`, `listxattr`, `llistxattr` and `flistxattr`) write into a `Buffer` and then mark as many bytes initialized as the system call reported. When the buffer has zero length, the operating system treats the call as a size query: it writes nothing and returns the size of the attribute value or of the name list. rustix still marked that many bytes as initialized.

Passing a `Vec` whose length equals its capacity through `spare_capacity` gives such a zero-length buffer, and rustix then set the vector's length past its capacity over memory that was never written. This is undefined behavior reachable from safe code. With an empty `&mut [MaybeUninit<u8>]` the call panics instead.

From 1.1.6 these functions return an empty result for a zero-length buffer, and new `getxattr_query`, `lgetxattr_query`, `fgetxattr_query`, `listxattr_query`, `llistxattr_query` and `flistxattr_query` functions return the size. The 0.38 series did not have the `Buffer` API and is not affected.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/rustix [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0486.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/bytecodealliance/rustix/commit/8e6aaa6713d2bb64b1fdd8c472e3a32a10a76d70 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0415 — Unbounded memory use on large request bodies
- Aliases: GHSA-pfrg-qg4f-grmv | Published: 2026-10-28 | Modified: 2026-10-29 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/juniper_hyper: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.10.1

The `graphql()` and `graphql_sync()` functions of `juniper_hyper` read the whole body of a POST request into memory before they parsed the body. The functions had no limit on the size of the body. A client could thus send a large body and exhaust the memory of the server. The request needs no authentication.

`juniper_hyper` 0.10.1 reads at most 2 MiB of a request body by default. The functions respond with `413 Payload Too Large` to larger bodies. A `juniper_hyper::BodyLimit` in the extensions of a request changes the limit for the request.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/juniper_hyper [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0415.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/graphql-rust/juniper/pull/1405 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0416 — Stack overflow on deeply nested GraphQL documents
- Aliases: GHSA-34cw-x8g7-rcv5 | Published: 2026-10-28 | Modified: 2026-10-29 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/juniper: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.17.2

The parser of `juniper` called itself once for each level of nesting in a GraphQL document. The parser had no limit on the nesting depth. Deeply nested selection sets, input values, and list types thus overflowed the stack of the thread that parsed the document. Validation and execution followed fragment spreads in the same recursive way. Long chains of fragment spreads thus overflowed the stack in validation and execution.

A stack overflow aborts the whole process. One request can thus stop a server and drop all other requests in progress. The request needs no authentication.

`juniper` 0.17.2 rejects documents that nest deeper than 64 levels or contain more than 15,000 tokens. `RootNode::limit_depth()` and `RootNode::limit_tokens()` change the limits.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/juniper [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0416.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/graphql-rust/juniper/pull/1405 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0417 — Excessive CPU and memory use on crafted GraphQL documents
- Aliases: GHSA-36rq-8957-mjrx | Published: 2026-10-28 | Modified: 2026-10-29 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/juniper: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.17.2

Several parts of `juniper` did work that grew faster than the size of the GraphQL document. A small document could thus use seconds of CPU time or gigabytes of memory. The requests need no authentication.

- `OverlappingFieldsCanBeMerged` compared all pairs of fields with the same response name. The rule also reported one error for each pair of conflicting fields.
- `NoFragmentCycles` and `OverlappingFieldsCanBeMerged` visited a fragment again for each path that spread the fragment. Validation time thus doubled with each fragment that spread the next fragment twice.
- Execution resolved the fields of a fragment once for each spread of the fragment. A document of 1.2 KB could expand into a billion fields.
- The parser removed each token from the front of a vector. Parsing time thus grew with the square of the number of tokens.

`juniper` 0.17.2 fixes the issues. The parser rejects documents with more than 15,000 tokens and operations with more than 15,000 fields after the expansion of fragments. The validation rules visit each fragment once. `OverlappingFieldsCanBeMerged` stops after 100,000 field comparisons and reports at most 100 conflicts. `RootNode::limit_tokens()` and `RootNode::limit_fields()` change the limits.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/juniper [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0417.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/graphql-rust/juniper/pull/1405 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0418 — libz-sys build script leaks environment variables in the build directory
- Aliases: GHSA-8p3x-q3q3-q3v5 | Published: 2026-10-28 | Modified: 2026-10-29 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/libz-sys: introduced:1.0.21 fixed:1.1.31

The build script of `libz-sys` version <= 1.1.30 prints the full environment
of the build, including any secrets it contains, to its stdout when compiled
with `cc@1.6.0` or later on a Unix host. This affects any build that depends on
`libz-sys`. Cargo saves build script stdout under `target/`, so the values end
up in anything that stores or shares the `target/` directory, such as CI caches.

This is similar to the [Miri issue](https://blog.rust-lang.org/2026/09/21/github-actions-leaking-secrets-when-miri-output-is-cached/).

## Details

To decide whether to link against a system zlib, the `libz-sys` build script
compiles a small test program with a compiler command from `cc` and logs it
with:

```rust
println!("running {:?}", cmd);
```

On Unix, `Debug` impl for `std::process::Command` prints every environment
variable explicitly set on the command. Before `cc@1.6.0`, its
`Tool::to_command()` set only a few compiler-specific variables. Starting with
`cc@1.6.0` (released 2026-10-03), `Tool::to_command()` clears the environment
and sets every variable explicitly.

Cargo parses build script stdout for `cargo:` directives, and stores the full
output in the build directory. It also prints it with `-vv`.

The probe runs for a default `libz-sys` build, including when pkg-config finds
zlib, unless one of these applies:

* the target is Windows, Android, Haiku or OpenHarmony
* static linking is requested via the `static` feature or `LIBZ_SYS_STATIC=1`
* the `zlib-ng` features are enabled without `stock-zlib`

## Mitigations

Fixed in `libz-sys@1.1.31`, which no longer logs the probe command.

If you cannot upgrade, here are some workarounds:

* Build with `LIBZ_SYS_STATIC=1` or the `static` feature, which skips the probe.
* Pin `cc` to a release before 1.6.0 (`cargo update cc --precise <version>`).

Note that upgrading `libz-sys` does not remove values already written. If an
affected build already ran with secrets in its environment, clear CI caches and
stored artifacts containing its
[target/build directory](https://doc.rust-lang.org/nightly/cargo/reference/build-cache.html#build-cache),
and consider rotating those secrets.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/libz-sys [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0418.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/rust-lang/libz-sys/pull/280 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0433 — Quadratic parsing of nested image wikilinks with `ENABLE_WIKILINKS`
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-28 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/pulldown-cmark: introduced:0.13.0 fixed:0.13.6

With `Options::ENABLE_WIKILINKS`, handle_wikilink builds a wikilink's target
from the raw text of its body: scan_wikilink_pipe walks it looking for |, then
contains('\0') and from_replace_nuls walk it again.
Once a wikilink is created, disable_all_links only disables Link entries, so
every enclosing `![[` stays live on the wikilink stack.
On `![[![[![[…]]]]]]` each of those openers then resolves in turn and rescans
everything inside it, which is quadratic in the nesting depth.
The event count stays tiny, because in the unpiped case each enclosing image
replaces its body with a fresh text node, so all of that work is thrown away.

Applications that parse untrusted Markdown with `ENABLE_WIKILINKS` set,
including through `Options::all()`, are affected. The option is off by default.
Parsing without it is not affected.

The flaw was fixed in
[#1186](https://github.com/pulldown-cmark/pulldown-cmark/pull/1186) and
released in 0.13.6.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/pulldown-cmark [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0433.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/pulldown-cmark/pulldown-cmark/pull/1186 [WEB]
- https://github.com/pulldown-cmark/pulldown-cmark/pull/1186 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0436 — Panic and unbounded allocation (OOM) in the CFB/OLE2 parser on malformed input
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-28 | Modified: 2026-11-03 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/calamine: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.38.0

The Compound File Binary (OLE2) parser (`src/cfb.rs`), used when reading `.xls` and `.xlsb`
workbooks and when extracting VBA projects from `.xlsx`, can panic or allocate without bound
on crafted input. Both are reachable through the public reader API (`open_workbook`,
`Xls::new`, `Xlsb::new`) on an attacker-supplied file. Impact is denial of service only — no
memory unsafety (safe Rust turns the out-of-bounds read into a bounds-checked panic).

1. **Reachable panic (out-of-bounds slice index).** The directory stream is iterated with
   `.chunks(128)`, but `Directory::from_slice` reads fixed offsets (`buf[..64]`,
   `buf[116..120]`, `buf[120..128]`) without checking the chunk length. A directory stream
   whose length is not a multiple of 128 yields a short final chunk, causing a panic
   (`range end index 64 out of range for slice of length N`). `SectorSize::Small` 64-byte
   mini sectors are a second trigger.

2. **Unbounded allocation / OOM.** The DIFAT loader grows the `difat` vector without bounding
   the number of iterations; a crafted self-referential DIFAT chain drives a multi-gigabyte
   allocation from a tiny file (a 512-byte file attempts an allocation of several gigabytes).

A consumer that parses untrusted or user-supplied spreadsheets can be crashed (thread panic,
or process abort under `panic=abort`) or driven out of memory by a small crafted file.

Fixed in calamine 0.38.0 (commit [b8ec4f0](https://github.com/tafia/calamine/commit/b8ec4f0)); the maintainer confirmed the fix in https://github.com/tafia/calamine/pull/762.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/calamine [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0436.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/tafia/calamine/pull/762 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0419 — Unbounded memory use on large request bodies
- Aliases: GHSA-fj3h-2jc3-973v | Published: 2026-10-29 | Modified: 2026-10-29 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/juniper_warp: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.9.1

The `make_graphql_filter()` and `make_graphql_filter_sync()` filters of `juniper_warp` read the whole body of a POST request into memory before they parsed the body. The filters had no limit on the size of the body. A client could thus send a large body and exhaust the memory of the server. The request needs no authentication.

`juniper_warp` 0.9.1 reads at most 2 MiB of a request body by default. The filters respond with `413 Payload Too Large` to larger bodies. `make_graphql_filter_with_body_limit()` and `make_graphql_filter_sync_with_body_limit()` set another limit.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/juniper_warp [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0419.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/graphql-rust/juniper/pull/1406 [WEB]
