### RUSTSEC-2026-0427 — `sn-releases`: `extract_release_archive` writes archive entries without a path-traversal check (zip-slip)
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-29 | Modified: 2026-10-30 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:H/I:H/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/sn-releases: introduced:0.0.0-0

`SafeReleaseRepoActions::extract_release_archive` builds each output path from the **raw archive entry name** and writes to it with no traversal check, so a malicious release archive can create directories and write files **outside** the destination directory, a "zip-slip" / path traversal (CWE-22 / CWE-23).

In `src/lib.rs` (`extract_release_archive`, zip branch):

```rust
let mut file = archive.by_index(i)?;
let out_path = dest_dir_path.join(file.name());   // file.name() is the raw entry name
if file.name().ends_with('/') {
    std::fs::create_dir_all(&out_path)?;          // no `..` check
} else {
    let mut outfile = std::fs::File::create(&out_path)?;   // writes outside dest
    std::io::copy(&mut file, &mut outfile)?;
}
```

The crate is unmaintained (latest release 0.3.1, 2024-10-22) and no fixed version is available.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/sn-releases [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0427.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/maidsafe/sn-releases [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0425 — `nix-nar`: `Decoder::unpack` writes archive entries outside the destination directory (path traversal)
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-30 | Modified: 2026-10-30 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/nix-nar: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.6.0

`Decoder::unpack` does not safely contain the paths it extracts, so a malicious NAR
archive can create files **outside** the destination directory — a path traversal
(CWE-22).

In `src/dec.rs` (`Decoder::unpack`):

- A symlink entry's target is written without checking where it points
  (`src/dec.rs:153`), so the archive can create a symlink that escapes the
  destination directory.
- The out-of-destination containment check compares path **strings** only and does
  not account for symlinks created earlier in the same unpack
  (`src/dec.rs:176-181`), before reaching the write sinks
  (`std::fs::create_dir` at `src/dec.rs:201` and `OpenOptions::open` at
  `src/dec.rs:218`).
- The parser also accepts entry names containing `/` (`src/parser.rs:138-147`).

Combined, a malicious archive can include a symlink `a` pointing at an external
directory and then an entry named `a/b`, causing `b` to be written to
`<external>/b`, outside the destination. This can create new files anywhere the
process can write (it cannot overwrite existing files), which is escalatable to code
execution by planting files in attacker-chosen locations.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/nix-nar [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0425.html [ADVISORY]
- https://codeberg.org/scvalex/nix-nar-rs/issues/3 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0426 — LLVM introduces timing-vulnerable optimizations into ML-KEM and ML-DSA operations intended to be constant-time
- Aliases: GHSA-r7wm-4vf2-h44w | Published: 2026-10-30 | Modified: 2026-10-30 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/orion: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.17.16 introduced:0.18.0-0 fixed:0.18.1

## Summary

On targets that do not have a conditional move/select operations, LLVM compiles the internal `conditional_sub_u32()` function, into one that will contain a conditional jump on secret-dependent data, which could leak information about encapsulation and signing keys.

`conditional_sub_u32()` is used throughout the field arithmetic of both ML-KEM and ML-DSA.

## Impact

The variable-time jumps have been observed for ML-KEM and ML-DSA on:
- `i586-unknown-linux-gnu`
- `s390x-unknown-linux-gnu`

Additionally, ML-DSA sees the vulnerable LLVM optimization on `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu`, for `hazardous::dsa::mldsa44` and `hazardous::dsa::mldsa87`.

The leakage this LLVM optimization introduces could enable recovery of cryptographic secrets. The practicality of exploiting the leakage depends on the compiler, target CPU used and how "close" to the process using the vulnerable functions the attacker is.

## Resolution

Upgrade to `v0.17.16` or `v0.18.1`. `conditional_sub_u32()` has been re-written to take conditional-move/jump ops availability into account.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/orion [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0426.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/orion-rs/orion/pull/689 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0428 — BLAKE2b produces wrong tag when using `reset()` with a different-length key
- Aliases: GHSA-xcc6-f2jq-43x6 | Published: 2026-10-30 | Modified: 2026-10-30 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/orion: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.17.16 introduced:0.18.0-0 fixed:0.18.1

## Summary

`hazardous::mac::blake2b::Blake2b::reset()` does not correctly reset the state when a key is supplied that has a different length than the one used upon initialization with `hazardous::mac::blake2b::Blake2b::new()`, because it errorneously uses a cached length. 

## Impact

Initialization `hazardous::mac::blake2b::Blake2b` with a key of some length `N`, followed by a reset with a key of length `!= N` will produce an incorrect BLAKE2b tag, causing `hazardous::mac::blake2b::Blake2b::verify()` to fail. This means under honest protocol conditions, parties could experience failing tag verifications.

## Resolution

Upgrade to `v0.17.16` or `v0.18.1`.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/orion [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0428.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/orion-rs/orion/pull/690 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0450 — `ocipkg`: unpacking an OCI archive can write and delete files outside the local image store (path traversal)
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-30 | Modified: 2026-11-06 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/ocipkg: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.4.1

When `ocipkg` unpacks an OCI archive, the destination directory is derived from the
image name, which `ocipkg` reads from the archive's **own** `index.json`
(`org.opencontainers.image.ref.name`). That name is attacker-controlled, and
`ImageName::parse` accepts a hostname of `..`, so the computed destination escapes the
local image store. A "zip-slip"-style path traversal (CWE-22 / CWE-23). This is esclatable to code execution.

In `Artifact::unpack` (`src/image/artifact.rs`), the destination is
`image_dir(name) = data_dir().join(name.as_path())` (`src/local/mod.rs:31`), where the
name comes from `get_name_from_index` (`src/image/layout.rs:114-124`):

```rust
let image_name = self.base.get_name()?;   // from the archive's index.json ref.name
let dest = image_dir(&image_name)?;        // data_dir().join(name.as_path()); hostname ".." escapes
if dest.exists() {
    if overwrite {
        fs::remove_dir_all(&dest)?;        // deletes a directory OUTSIDE the store
    }
}
fs::create_dir_all(&dest)?;
// ... each tar layer is then unpacked under `dest` (also outside the store)
```

## The fix

Fixed in `ocipkg` 0.4.1 by [PR #223](https://github.com/termoshtt/ocipkg/pull/223). The leading
reference component is validated against the `org.opencontainers.image.ref.name` grammar from OCI
Image Specification 1.1.1 on parse and deserialization, and `local::image_dir` revalidates the name
and rejects any non-normal path component (e.g. `..`) before any filesystem mutation, so a crafted
archive can no longer escape the local image store.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/ocipkg [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0450.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/termoshtt/ocipkg/issues/222 [REPORT]
- https://github.com/termoshtt/ocipkg/pull/223 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0459 — `libsqlite3-sys` via C SQLite CVE-2026-119375
- Aliases: BIT-sqlite-2026-119375, CVE-2026-119375, GHSA-72w2-rjgg-q729, RUSTSEC-2026-0502 | Published: 2026-10-30 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:L/AC:L/AT:N/PR:N/UI:P/VC:H/VI:N/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/libsqlite3-sys: introduced:0.6.0 fixed:0.39.0

SQLite versions before 3.54.0 can return uninitialized page-cache memory when a process opens a database file together with a sibling write-ahead-log (`-wal`) file whose page size differs from the database's. A crafted pair of files can make ordinary queries return stale bytes from the process's memory. The attack vector is local and needs the victim to open the files.

As `libsqlite3-sys` bundles SQLite when it is built from its bundled source (the `bundled` feature, which `rusqlite`'s `bundled` feature enables), it is susceptible to the vulnerability. The releases `0.6.0` to `0.38.2` bundle SQLite `3.13.0` to `3.53.2` and are affected; the earlier releases contain no SQLite source. `libsqlite3-sys` was updated to bundle the patched version of SQLite in `0.39.0` (SQLite `3.54.0`) and `0.39.1` (SQLite `3.54.1`), released with [`rusqlite` 0.41](https://github.com/rusqlite/rusqlite/releases/tag/v0.41.0). `libsqlite3-sys` `0.39` declares Rust 1.88 and `rusqlite` `0.41` declares Rust 1.89.

Only a process that opens a database file, with its `-wal`, that a less trusted party could have written is exposed. A build that links a system SQLite instead of the bundled source is not affected by the copy in this crate; its exposure is that of the system library's version. The `bundled-sqlcipher` feature builds SQLCipher instead of SQLite; in `0.39.0` and `0.39.1` it depends on `sqlcipher-src =419.0.6`, which bundles SQLite `3.53.4` and is affected.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/libsqlite3-sys [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0459.html [ADVISORY]
- https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-119375 [WEB]
- https://sqlite.org/bugs/info/2026-08-17T15:54:41Z [WEB]
- https://github.com/sqlite/sqlite/commit/e5bf119821f25be1d7f10ef0a2edbe175289166e [WEB]
- https://sqlite.org/releaselog/3_54_0.html [WEB]
- https://github.com/rusqlite/rusqlite/releases/tag/v0.41.0 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0495 — Agent server signs with confirm-constrained keys without calling `Agent::confirm`
- Aliases: CVE-2026-116794, GHSA-vp5q-ff62-jwqv | Published: 2026-10-30 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/russh: introduced:0.50.0-beta.12 fixed:0.65.0

A key added to an SSH agent with the confirm constraint (what `ssh-add -c` sends) must not be used to sign until the user approves each use. russh's agent server (`russh::keys::agent::server`) accepted the constraint, but when a sign request arrived for such a key it used a fixed `true` in place of the result of `Agent::confirm`, so the application's confirmation step never ran. This started in 0.50.0-beta.12; 0.50.0-beta.8 to beta.11 still called `confirm`, and earlier russh releases have no agent server.

Any process that can reach the agent, including a remote host when the agent is forwarded, can then sign with the protected key without the user's approval, for example to log in elsewhere as the user. The private key itself is not disclosed. Agents whose `Agent::confirm` keeps the default, which always returns `true`, behave the same before and after the fix.

From 0.65.0 the agent server calls `Agent::confirm` for confirm-constrained keys and refuses to sign when it returns `false`. Implementations that left `confirm` unimplemented (for example as `todo!()`) will now reach that code.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/russh [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0495.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/Eugeny/russh/commit/62db6e7defd6d244159da962ccccb029e785ce0f [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0502 — `sqlcipher-src` via C SQLite CVE-2026-119375
- Aliases: BIT-sqlite-2026-119375, CVE-2026-119375, GHSA-72w2-rjgg-q729, RUSTSEC-2026-0459 | Published: 2026-10-30 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:L/AC:L/AT:N/PR:N/UI:P/VC:H/VI:N/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/sqlcipher-src: introduced:419.0.3 fixed:420.0.0

SQLite versions before 3.54.0 can return uninitialized page-cache memory when a process opens a database file together with a sibling write-ahead-log (`-wal`) file whose page size differs from the database's. A crafted pair of files can make ordinary queries return stale bytes from the process's memory. The attack vector is local and needs the victim to open the files.

`sqlcipher-src` ships the SQLCipher amalgamation (SQLCipher is built on SQLite and contains the same `sqlite3WalReadFrame` code) for `-sys` crates to compile, so a build that uses it is susceptible to the vulnerability. The releases `419.0.3` to `419.0.6` bundle SQLCipher `4.19.0` on SQLite `3.53.4` and are affected. `sqlcipher-src` was updated in `420.0.0` (SQLCipher `4.20.0` on SQLite `3.54.1`), which contains the fix. The releases `0.1.0` and `0.1.1` contain no SQLCipher source. `libsqlite3-sys` `0.39.0` and `0.39.1` depend on `sqlcipher-src =419.0.6` for their `bundled-sqlcipher` feature.

Only a process that opens a database file, with its `-wal`, that a less trusted party could have written is exposed.

- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0502.html [ADVISORY]

- https://sqlite.org/releaselog/3_54_0.html [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0432 — `codeq`: decoding untrusted input can allocate memory from an unchecked length prefix (denial of service)
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-31 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:L`
- Affected crates.io/codeq: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.6.3

`codeq` is a serialization library whose `Decode` trait reads length-prefixed values. For `String` and `Vec<u8>`, the length-prefixed `Decode` implementations read a 4-byte big-endian length and allocate a buffer of that size **before** reading the payload. Because the declared length is taken straight from the input with no upper bound and no cross-check against the bytes actually available, a tiny input can force an allocation of up to ~4 GiB. Code that decodes untrusted data through `codeq` can therefore be made to spike memory use and be OOM-killed (CWE-400, uncontrolled resource consumption).

The affected path, in `src/codec/impls/string_impl.rs`:

```rust
impl Decode for String {
    fn decode<R: io::Read>(mut r: R) -> Result<Self, io::Error> {
        let len = r.read_u32::<BigEndian>()? as usize;
        let mut buf = vec![0; len];   // allocates `len` bytes up front
        r.read_exact(&mut buf)?; 
        String::from_utf8(buf).map_err(|e| io::Error::new(io::ErrorKind::InvalidData, e))
    }
}
```

Any code that decodes attacker-controlled bytes through `codeq` can be forcedcto allocate up to ~4 GiB per `decode` call from a 4-byte length prefix. On a service decoding untrusted input this is a cheap denial of service (crash / OOM-kill). The practical severity depends on how the embedding wires `codeq` to untrusted bytes.

## Remediation

Fixed in `0.6.3`, which grows the decode buffers as payload bytes actually arrive instead of pre-allocating the full declared length (commit `9f15313`, "fix: codec: grow decode buffers as payload bytes arrive"). Upgrade to `codeq >= 0.6.3`.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/codeq [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0432.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/drmingdrmer/codeq/issues/4 [REPORT]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0434 — `ant-releases`: extracting a release archive can write files outside the destination directory (path traversal / zip-slip)
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-10-31 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:C/C:L/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/ant-releases: introduced:0.0.0-0

`ant-releases` downloads and unpacks release archives from the `safe_network` / `autonomi` repositories. When it extracts an archive, it builds each output path by joining the archive entry's **own name** onto the destination directory, with no check
for `..` or absolute components — a classic "zip-slip" path traversal (CWE-22 / CWE-23). A crafted archive can therefore create or overwrite files outside the intended extraction directory.

In `extract_release_archive` (`src/lib.rs`), the ZIP branch does:

```rust
let out_path = dest_dir_path.join(file.name());   // raw zip entry name, no containment check
if file.name().ends_with('/') {
    std::fs::create_dir_all(&out_path)?;          // dir created outside dest_dir
} else {
    let mut outfile = std::fs::File::create(&out_path)?;   // file written outside dest_dir
    std::io::copy(&mut file, &mut outfile)?;
}
```

## Proof of concept

A one-entry ZIP whose entry name is `../foo_pwned.txt`, passed to `AntReleaseRepoActions::extract_release_archive` (obtained via the crate's public `<dyn AntReleaseRepoActions>::default_config()`), writes `foo_pwned.txt` as a sibling of
the destination directory, outside it. Confirmed against the published `0.4.3` crate.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/ant-releases [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0434.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/maidsafe/ant-releases [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0430 — Path traversal in `rio-vt` Kitty graphics protocol handler allows out-of-bounds file delete (and read)
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-11-01 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:H/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/rio-vt: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.5.29

The Kitty graphics protocol handler in `rio-vt` builds a filesystem path from an attacker-controlled APC escape sequence and, for the temp-file medium, only checks that the path *contains the substring* `"tty-graphics-protocol"` before it opens, reads, and `remove_file`s it. There is no `..` rejection and no canonicalization, so a path containing the marker substring plus `..` escapes the terminal's temp directory (path traversal, CWE-22/CWE-23).

Because a terminal renders whatever arrives on the PTY (ssh output, a `cat`'d file, CI logs), untrusted output can delete a file outside the temp area and cause its bytes to be read back in.

## Details

The sink is `create_graphic_data()` in `src/ansi/kitty_graphics_protocol.rs`, in the `TransmissionMedium::TempFile` branch, reachable through the public API `rio_vt::ansi::kitty_graphics_protocol::parse`. The path is the base64 APC payload of an `a=t,t=t` command, and the guard is:

```rust
let path = Path::new(path_str);
if !path.is_file() { return Err(GraphicError::FileNotFound); }       // literal path, resolves `..`
// reject /proc/ /sys/ /dev/ (lowercased substring)
if cmd.medium == TransmissionMedium::TempFile
    && !path_str.contains("tty-graphics-protocol") {                 // SUBSTRING, not a component
    return Err(GraphicError::InvalidData);
}
// ...
let mut file = File::open(path)...;          // reads file bytes
if cmd.medium == TransmissionMedium::TempFile {
    let _ = std::fs::remove_file(path);      // deletes the file
}
```

This affects any terminal emulator or application built on `rio-vt` (e.g. the Rio terminal) that renders attacker-influenced output: an out-of-bounds file is deleted, and its contents are read into the process.

## The fix

Fixed in `rio-vt` 0.5.29 (Rio 0.5.29) by [PR #1998](https://github.com/raphamorim/rio/pull/1998). For the temp-file medium the parent directory is resolved with `canonicalize` before any check, the resolved entry must lie inside a temp directory (`temp_dir()`, `/tmp` or `/dev/shm`) and contain the marker, and the delete goes through `unlinkat` on a handle to the validated directory, so neither `..` nor a swapped symlink can redirect it. Path checks for the file medium also run on the file actually opened.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/rio-vt [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0430.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/raphamorim/rio/security/advisories/GHSA-m926-9wrf-jr8w [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/raphamorim/rio/pull/1998 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0431 — Unbounded memory allocation in `evtx` parsing a `.wevtcache` file
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-11-01 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/evtx: introduced:0.11.0 fixed:0.15.1

Affected versions of `evtx` did not bound a per-entry payload length read from a `.wevtcache` file before allocating a buffer sized from it, allowing a tiny crafted file to force a multi-gigabyte allocation and abort the process (out-of-memory denial of service, CWE-400).

## Details

In `WevtCacheReader::next_entry` (`src/wevt_templates/wevtcache.rs`), the per-entry payload length is read as a `u64` straight from the file and used to size a zeroing buffer **before** any data is read:

```rust
let len = usize::try_from(len_u64)?;   // u64 taken directly from the entry header
let mut buf = vec![0u8; len];          // unbounded allocation, sized from the file
reader.read_exact(&mut buf)?;          // only fails AFTER the allocation
```

The only check on `len` is that it fits in `usize`; there is no check that it fits within the bytes remaining in the file. An entry header declaring a ~4 GiB length therefore makes the crate request a ~4 GiB allocation from a file of only ~25 bytes.

The sink is reachable through the public API `evtx::wevt_templates::wevtcache::for_each_crim_blob(path, f)` (and the `WevtCacheReader::next_entry` it drives). The `.wevtcache` reader first shipped in `0.11.0`, and every published version from `0.11.0` through `0.14.0` is affected. The code lives in the `wevt_templates` feature, which is enabled by default through the `evtx_dump` feature. The fix landed in `0.15.1`: `0.15.0` was tagged but never published to crates.io (its Windows build failed), so `0.15.1` is the first patched release available. The Python bindings (`evtx` on PyPI) carry the fix as of `0.15.0`.

## Reproduction

A ~25-byte file is sufficient: the `.wevtcache` magic (`WEVTCACH`), a `version` (u32 LE) of `1`, an `entry_count` (u32 LE) of `1`, then one entry consisting of a `kind` byte and a `len` (u64 LE) set to a large value (e.g. `0xFFFFFFF0`), with no payload bytes following. Passing that file to `for_each_crim_blob` triggers the oversized allocation before the short read fails.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/evtx [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0431.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/omerbenamram/evtx/issues/334 [REPORT]
- https://github.com/omerbenamram/evtx/pull/335 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0435 — Unbounded memory allocation in `thrift` binary protocol `read_bytes`
- Aliases: BIT-thrift-2026-43868, CVE-2026-43868, GHSA-2f9f-gq7v-9h6m | Published: 2026-11-02 | Modified: 2026-11-02 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/thrift: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.23.0

Affected versions of the `thrift` crate size a buffer directly from an attacker-controlled 4-byte length read off the wire, before reading any payload and with no upper bound, allowing a hostile peer to force a large eager allocation or an immediate abort (denial of service, CWE-400 / CWE-789). This is the Rust implementation's instance of the CVE-2020-13949 pattern, published by the Apache Thrift PMC as CVE-2026-43868.

## Details

In `TBinaryInputProtocol::read_bytes` (`src/protocol/binary.rs`), the length is read as a `i32` from the transport and cast straight to `usize` to size a zeroing buffer **before** any payload is read:

```rust
fn read_bytes(&mut self) -> crate::Result<Vec<u8>> {
    let num_bytes = self.transport.read_i32::<BigEndian>()? as usize; // length from the wire
    let mut buf = vec![0u8; num_bytes];                               // unbounded eager allocation
    self.transport.read_exact(&mut buf).map(|_| buf).map_err(From::from)
}
```

There is no check that `num_bytes` is non-negative or that it fits within the data actually available:

- A length of `0x7FFFFFFF` forces a ~2 GiB allocation; `0x20000000` forces 512 MiB; and so on, from only a 4-byte input.
- A negative length casts to a huge `usize` (up to `usize::MAX`), causing an immediate allocation failure / abort.

`read_string` routes through `read_bytes`, so string fields are affected the same way. The sink is reachable from any peer sending binary-protocol data, so this is an unauthenticated, network-reachable denial of service.

## The fix

From `thrift` 0.23.0 onward, `read_bytes` goes through `read_binary_len`, which rejects negative sizes and enforces `TConfiguration::max_string_size` (default 100 MiB), alongside default message, frame, and recursion limits.

A separate, related gap on the non-strict message-name path was published as CVE-2026-58389 and fixed in 0.24.0; this advisory covers the `read_bytes` unbounded allocation (CVE-2026-43868), fixed in 0.23.0.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/thrift [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0435.html [ADVISORY]
- https://lists.apache.org/thread/zj76dtwnbbs1m7z3focf4wd51pqpsmn9 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0442 — Apache Arrow Rust: Improper validation of RunEndEncoded arrays allows out of bounds reads
- Aliases: CVE-2026-111959, GHSA-gqmm-qrjq-4c7h | Published: 2026-11-02 | Modified: 2026-11-05 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/arrow-data: introduced:33.0.0 fixed:57.3.2 introduced:58.0.0 fixed:58.4.1 introduced:59.0.0 fixed:59.3.2 introduced:60.0.0 fixed:60.1.0

Improper validation of RunEndEncoded arrays via `ArrayData::validate_values` can
allow reading data beyond the bounds of the child values array when reading the
last value via APIs such as `TypedRunArray::value`.

Patched versions now properly validate the contents of RunEndEncoded arrays to prevent
allowing reading out of bounds of the specified array data.

Credit to Adam Korczynski of Ada Logics for reporting this and Anthropic for finding
this.

### RUSTSEC-2026-0452 — Out-of-bounds read when decoding internal keys shorter than 16 bytes
- Aliases: CVE-2026-134303, GHSA-9fm9-c4h4-8g93 | Published: 2026-11-03 | Modified: 2026-11-21 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:C/C:H/I:N/A:L`
- Affected crates.io/surrealkv: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:1.2.0

`InternalKeyRef::from_encoded` is a safe constructor whose only length check is `debug_assert!(encoded.len() >= 16)`, which is compiled out in release builds. Its accessors perform unchecked length arithmetic on the slice: `user_key()` slices `&encoded[..len - 16]`, `trailer()`/`seq_num()` pass `len - 16` and `timestamp()` passes `len - 8` to `read_u64_be`, which computes `buffer.as_ptr().add(offset)` and performs an 8-byte load. For a short slice the subtraction underflows `usize`, the pointer wraps below the allocation and eight bytes before the buffer are read (undefined behavior; confirmed with AddressSanitizer as a `heap-buffer-overflow`). In debug builds the same call panics instead.

The same invariant is missing at the on-disk decode boundary: `BlockIterator::seek_next_entry` rebuilds the current key from block bytes with upper-bound checks only, and `BlockIterator::key()` wraps it in `InternalKeyRef`, which `surrealdb`'s `kv-surrealkv` backend then reads through `key_ref.timestamp()`. A crafted or corrupted SSTable therefore reaches the same unchecked subtraction without any `unsafe` at the call site.

`1.2.0` rejects internal keys shorter than their 8-byte trailer before any accessor computes an offset and makes the accessors bounds-checked (fix in commit `ac642f9`, `surrealdb` 4.0.0 ships `surrealkv` 1.2.0).

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/surrealkv [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0452.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/surrealdb/surrealkv/commit/ac642f945d63c9c9f7ad35f480995fd516f83fb6 [WEB]
- https://github.com/surrealdb/surrealkv/pull/497 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0454 — Data race and double free via unsound `Send`/`Sync` in `reblessive`
- Aliases: GHSA-m9qr-4rcq-3jhc | Published: 2026-11-03 | Modified: 2026-11-06 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:L/AC:H/PR:L/UI:N/S:C/C:H/I:H/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/surrealdb-core: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:4.0.0

`dbs::Executor` drives every SurrealQL statement through `reblessive::tree::TreeStack`. In `reblessive 0.4.3` the runtime types unconditionally assert `Send`/`Sync` (`unsafe impl Send/Sync for Stack`, `TreeStack` and `Runner`), while the public entry points accept any future without a `Send` bound and store it type-erased in a bump allocator.

Two safe-code consequences follow. First, `Runner::step_async`/`finish_async` return driver futures tied to the stack borrow rather than to `&mut self`, so a driver future can be duplicated; because `Stack` is `Sync` those futures are `Send`, and polling two copies on different threads runs `drive_top_task` concurrently — racy `Cell`/`UnsafeCell` access, double `pop_task()`/`pop_dealloc()` (double drop / double free) and a non-atomic `Option::take`. Second, `TreeStack: Send` lets safe code move a driver whose type-erased future captures `!Send` state (`Rc`, `RefCell`) to another thread while the creating thread keeps an alias of that state.

No `unsafe` is needed at the call site. `surrealdb-core`'s own query futures are `Send` only because of the unsound `unsafe impl Send for TreeStack`, while the captured `&mut tree::Stk` is deliberately `!Send`.

The unsound dependency was removed in 4.0.0 and the parser now uses plain recursion (commit `75407c4bcc`).

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/surrealdb-core [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0454.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/surrealdb/surrealdb/commit/75407c4bccc6d6679d7d6e7a18af0065afdded7d [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0456 — Panic on an HEVC tile layout that does not match the active SPS
- Aliases: GHSA-rcff-5x58-7w2p | Published: 2026-11-04 | Modified: 2026-11-09 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/heifer-hevc-dec: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.3.27

An HEVC picture parameter set (PPS) stores explicit tile column widths and row
heights computed against the sequence parameter set (SPS) active when it was
parsed. A crafted stream can redefine that SPS afterwards, so that the tile sizes
add up to more than the picture. The decoder then indexed past its coding tree
block tables: Rust's bounds checks turned this into a panic, or the picture was
decoded wrongly. A panic aborts the decoding thread, or the whole process when
built with `panic = "abort"`. The crate forbids `unsafe` code, so there is no
memory corruption.

Any application that decodes untrusted HEIC files or HEVC streams with
`heifer-hevc-dec`, directly or through the `heifer` crate, is affected.

The flaw was corrected in 0.3.27 (commit
[`71dfc67`](https://github.com/HarpeLm/Heifer/commit/71dfc67f1e3ae3c4b7e4cf633e83a26c94b06a92)):
a tile layout that does not match the picture size is rejected with
`Error::Invalid` at decode time.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/heifer-hevc-dec [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0456.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/HarpeLm/Heifer/commit/71dfc67f1e3ae3c4b7e4cf633e83a26c94b06a92 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0458 — Memory exhaustion when parsing a crafted `iloc` box
- Aliases: GHSA-3243-948x-x627 | Published: 2026-11-04 | Modified: 2026-11-09 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/heifer-isobmff: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.3.27

`heifer-isobmff` read the item and extent counts of the `iloc` box (item
locations) from the file and allocated memory for them before checking that the
box was large enough to hold that many entries. Extents that declare no field at
all take zero bytes each, so a small crafted file could declare an enormous
number of them. A 42 KB file made parsing allocate several gigabytes, which can
exhaust memory and abort the process.

Any application that parses untrusted HEIF/HEIC files with `heifer-isobmff`,
directly or through the `heifer` crate, is affected: `HeifFile::parse` and every
decoding and metadata function built on it.

The flaw was corrected in 0.3.27 (commit
[`1a8426e`](https://github.com/HarpeLm/Heifer/commit/1a8426e1b41160fb48d8baf8be858238570a8093)):
item and extent counts are bounded by the size of the box, extents that carry no
field are rejected, and the total number of extents is capped.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/heifer-isobmff [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0458.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/HarpeLm/Heifer/commit/1a8426e1b41160fb48d8baf8be858238570a8093 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0460 — Panic on chroma QP offsets enabled with an empty offset list
- Aliases: GHSA-rmc9-8qp4-gjhx | Published: 2026-11-04 | Modified: 2026-11-09 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/heifer-hevc-dec: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.3.27

An HEVC Range Extensions stream can set `cu_chroma_qp_offset_enabled_flag` in a
slice while its picture parameter set carries an empty chroma QP offset list.
The decoder then read the list at the signalled index, which panicked on the
out-of-range access. A panic aborts the decoding thread, or the whole process
when built with `panic = "abort"`. The crate forbids `unsafe` code, so there is
no memory corruption.

Any application that decodes untrusted HEIC files or HEVC streams with
`heifer-hevc-dec`, directly or through the `heifer` crate, is affected.

The flaw was corrected in 0.3.27 (commit
[`1a8426e`](https://github.com/HarpeLm/Heifer/commit/1a8426e1b41160fb48d8baf8be858238570a8093)):
with an empty list there is no offset to apply, and the offset is zero.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/heifer-hevc-dec [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0460.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/HarpeLm/Heifer/commit/1a8426e1b41160fb48d8baf8be858238570a8093 [WEB]

  linking), the maintainer suggests forking the repository from

### RUSTSEC-2026-0462 — Unbounded response body buffering in Streamable HTTP clients
- Aliases: GHSA-c9f9-cp8h-jcj5 | Published: 2026-11-05 | Modified: 2026-11-09 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:R/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:H`
- Affected crates.io/rmcp: introduced:0.2.0 fixed:3.9.0

Before 3.9.0, the built-in Streamable HTTP clients in rmcp read non-streaming
HTTP response bodies into memory with no size limit. Both the reqwest client
(`transport-streamable-http-client-reqwest`) and the Unix socket client
(`transport-streamable-http-client-unix-socket`) were affected:

- `application/json` responses to POST requests were read in full before
  parsing.
- Non-2xx responses were read in full for error reporting.

A malicious or compromised MCP server can answer with a body that never ends,
for example a chunked `application/json` response that streams whitespace. The
client buffers it until the process runs out of memory. This works during
`initialize`, before any authentication. On desktop or agent hosts, this
terminates the whole host application.

Server-sent event streams are not affected. Each event is already capped by
`max_sse_event_size`, and events are not accumulated.

The flaw was corrected in 3.9.0. Successful JSON bodies are limited to 16 MiB
and non-success bodies to 64 KiB. Bytes are counted while streaming, after
decompression. An oversized `Content-Length` is rejected before the body is
read, and an oversized body is rejected with
`StreamableHttpError::ResponseBodyTooLarge` instead of being truncated.

If you cannot upgrade, use a custom `StreamableHttpClient` that enforces a
response body limit, or connect only to trusted MCP servers.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/rmcp [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0462.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/rust-sdk/commit/3e0156c1eaf1573a1b878e2a8998d2fa2d0b1439 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0443 — Hidden file disclosure through Windows short names
- Aliases: CVE-2026-134347, GHSA-8hcm-m7r7-frwc | Published: 2026-11-06 | Modified: 2026-11-21 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/actix-files: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.7.4

On Windows volumes with 8.3 short names, `actix_files::Files` can serve hidden
files through their short-name aliases. The default path check rejects segments
that start with a dot, but an alias such as `ENV~1` or `GIT~1` passes this check.

A remote client can read files such as `.env` or `.git/config` in the served
directory even when `use_hidden_files()` is not enabled. This can expose secrets
or source code.

Upgrade to actix-files 0.7.4 or later. As a workaround, remove sensitive files
and directories from the served directory.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/actix-files [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0443.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/actix/actix-web/commit/f2c83122d244e4ba38514ee3fe1fd42a110dc3f0 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0445 — Degraded secret zeroization capabilities
- Aliases: GHSA-mxcm-mrw6-c446 | Published: 2026-11-06 | Modified: 2026-11-06 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/vodozemac: introduced:0.11.0 fixed:0.11.2

Versions 0.11.0 and 0.11.1 of vodozemac have degraded secret zeroization
capabilities, due to changes in third-party cryptographic dependencies
(chacha-20poly1305, sha2, hmac), which moved secret zeroization capabilities
behind a feature flag while vodozemac disabled the default feature set.

## Impact

The degraded zeroization capabilities could result in the production of more
memory copies of encryption secrets and secrets could linger in memory longer
than necessary. This marginally increases the risk of sensitive data exposure.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/vodozemac [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0445.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/matrix-org/vodozemac/pull/448 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0446 — Request smuggling through malformed chunked request trailers
- Aliases: CVE-2026-134342, GHSA-2q7f-mf9f-5qhr | Published: 2026-11-06 | Modified: 2026-11-21 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/actix-http: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:3.20.2

The HTTP/1 chunked decoder accepts a bare line feed after the final chunk as
the start of a trailer instead of rejecting the malformed request.

When an upstream proxy reuses a connection and either pipelines the requests or
does not take steps to validate the chunked stream, the decoder can consume the
next request's headers as trailer data. It can then parse that request's body as
a new request. An attacker can use this difference in request parsing to bypass
access controls enforced by a proxy or web application firewall.

Upgrade to actix-http 3.20.2 or later.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/actix-http [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0446.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/actix/actix-web/commit/feed12e9ec59b39abe90590bf48ae6399ed45b56 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0447 — Compressed request bodies can exhaust memory before extractor limits apply
- Aliases: CVE-2026-128242, GHSA-qph8-7859-gq2f, RUSTSEC-2026-0448 | Published: 2026-11-06 | Modified: 2026-11-14 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/actix-web: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:4.18.2

Body extractors do not pass their size limits to the decompression layer.
A small compressed request can therefore cause a large allocation before the
extractor rejects the decoded body. A remote attacker can use this to exhaust
memory or CPU resources and cause denial of service.

This affects applications that read compressed request bodies with the
corresponding compression feature enabled.

Upgrade to actix-web 4.18.2 or later with actix-http 3.20.1 or later.
As a workaround, reject compressed request bodies at a reverse proxy or in
middleware before body extraction.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/actix-web [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0447.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/actix/actix-web/commit/0d0be61bd757027bfcecbc53df1b7a600f21ecd0 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0448 — Unbounded decompression can exhaust memory and CPU resources
- Aliases: CVE-2026-128242, GHSA-qph8-7859-gq2f, RUSTSEC-2026-0447 | Published: 2026-11-06 | Modified: 2026-11-15 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/actix-http: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:3.20.1

The `encoding::Decoder` stream can expand compressed input into a large output
buffer before a caller can enforce its body size limit. An attacker who controls
a compressed request or response can cause excessive memory allocation and CPU
use, resulting in denial of service.

This affects applications that decode untrusted gzip, deflate, Brotli, or Zstandard
bodies with the corresponding compression feature enabled.

Upgrade to actix-http 3.20.1 or later and configure `Decoder::limit()` when using
the decoder directly. Actix Web users must also upgrade to actix-web 4.18.2 or
later so that extractors pass their limits to the decoder.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/actix-http [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0448.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/actix/actix-web/commit/41b54d23ea04f66758c15de7e4ab18325cd25280 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0451 — Degraded secret zeroization capabilities
- Aliases: GHSA-v27v-f96q-qhjq | Published: 2026-11-06 | Modified: 2026-11-06 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/vodozemac: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.11.2

In version 0.11.1 and earlier of vodozemac, the Olm root key ratchet did not
zeroize its HKDF output after use. Each ratchet step left a copy of the new root
key and chain key in freed heap memory.

## Impact

An attacker who can read the process's memory, for example through a separate
memory-disclosure bug, a core dump, or swapped-out pages, may recover root keys
and chain keys from earlier ratchet steps. A leaked chain key allows decrypting
the messages in that chain, weakening Olm's forward secrecy for affected
sessions.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/vodozemac [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0451.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/matrix-org/vodozemac/pull/446 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0455 — Large-window Brotli bodies bypass decompression memory limits
- Aliases: CVE-2026-134341, GHSA-7p9w-p5qm-5gc5 | Published: 2026-11-06 | Modified: 2026-11-21 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/actix-http: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:3.20.3

With the `compress-brotli` feature enabled, the decoder accepts the non-standard
large-window Brotli format. A crafted stream can cause its internal buffer to
grow to about 1 GiB before the decoded output reaches the configured size limit.
An 850-byte request can cause this allocation, and concurrent requests can
exhaust process or host memory.

Actix Web enables Brotli support by default. Applications that read request
bodies through `Bytes`, `String`, `Json`, or `Form` can be affected even when
extractor size limits are configured. The allocation occurs before the handler
runs, so authentication performed in the handler does not prevent it.

Upgrade to actix-http 3.20.3 or later. As a workaround, reject requests with
`Content-Encoding: br` at a reverse proxy or in middleware before body
extraction, or disable the `compress-brotli` feature throughout the dependency
graph.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/actix-http [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0455.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/actix/actix-web/commit/61c6a15c1c0e505a5cd6842610a5f1faea432a83 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0503 — eazip: symlink-target validation checks the wrong argument, allowing extraction path escapes on Windows
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-11-07 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:C/C:N/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/eazip: introduced:0.2.5 fixed:0.2.7

When extracting archives, `eazip` validates symlink entries before writing
them. The validation function `validate_symlink()` (`src/utils/mod.rs`) scans
for backslash and NUL bytes, but passes the symlink's *name* where the
symlink's *target* was intended:

```rust
memchr2(b'\\', b'\0', name.as_bytes()) // should be target.as_bytes()
```

As a result, symlink *targets* containing backslash or NUL bytes pass
validation. On Windows, backslash is a path separator, so targets such as
`..\..\..\Windows\system32\evil.exe` also defeat the remaining depth check,
which splits on `/` only. A crafted archive can therefore create symlinks
that resolve outside the extraction directory — including UNC targets such as
`\\host\share\...` — redirecting writes to arbitrary filesystem locations
chosen by the attacker.

Applications that extract untrusted archives with `eazip` on Windows targets
are affected; on Unix targets backslash is not a separator, which limits
exposure there.

The vulnerability was introduced in version 0.2.5 and fixed in 0.2.7
([a1phyr/eazip@0e4caa13](https://github.com/a1phyr/eazip/commit/0e4caa13d59f5ba15aaac332f5534260c3b8fc43)),
which rejects backslash and NUL bytes in the symlink target. It was
privately reported to the maintainer on 2026-11-06 and credited in the
0.2.7 release notes.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/eazip [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0503.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/a1phyr/eazip/releases/tag/v0.2.7 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0480 — Panic on a GCP launch endorsement without TDX measurements
- Aliases: CVE-2026-40693, GHSA-gwf2-8595-qw97 | Published: 2026-11-10 | Modified: 2026-11-11 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:L/AC:H/AT:P/PR:L/UI:N/VC:N/VI:N/VA:H/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/tdx_workload_attestation: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.2.0

`GcpTdxHost::verify_launch_endorsement()`, available with the `host-gcp-tdx`
feature, read `uefi_golden.tdx.measurements[0]` without checking that the
golden measurement contains a TDX measurement. If a launch endorsement's
golden measurement had no TDX measurements, the call panicked with an index
out of bounds instead of returning an error.

The panic is reached only after the endorsement's signing certificate and
signature have been verified. Triggering it therefore requires a correctly
signed endorsement that lacks TDX measurements.

Intel describes the issue as improper input validation and classifies it as
denial of service, with a CVSS 4.0 base score of 5.7, in INTEL-SA-01520.

The flaw was corrected in
[#87](https://github.com/IntelLabs/tdx-workload-attestation/pull/87), released
in 0.2.0. The function now returns `Error::ParseError` when the TDX
measurement structure is missing or empty.

Reported by [Borys Tsyrulnikov](https://borystsyrulnikov.dev).

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/tdx_workload_attestation [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0480.html [ADVISORY]
- https://www.intel.com/content/www/us/en/security-center/advisory/intel-sa-01520.html [WEB]
- https://github.com/IntelLabs/tdx-workload-attestation/pull/87 [WEB]
- https://github.com/IntelLabs/tdx-workload-attestation/releases/tag/v0.2.0 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0481 — `verify_x509_cert` does not check the certificate validity period
- Aliases: CVE-2026-42067, GHSA-w4mf-755j-wjwc | Published: 2026-11-10 | Modified: 2026-11-11 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:L/AC:L/AT:P/PR:N/UI:N/VC:N/VI:H/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/tdx_workload_attestation: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.2.0

`tdx_workload_attestation::verification::x509::verify_x509_cert()`, available
with the `host-verification` feature, checked that `issuer_cert` issued `cert`
and verified the certificate signature. It did not check the certificate's
validity period, so it accepted expired and not-yet-valid certificates.

With the `host-gcp-tdx` feature, this function validates the GCP launch
endorsement signing certificate against the GCE confidential-computing TCB
root certificate. A signing certificate outside its validity period was
therefore still accepted during launch endorsement verification.

Intel describes the issue as improper certificate validation and classifies
it as escalation of privilege, with a CVSS 4.0 base score of 5.9, in
INTEL-SA-01520.

The flaw was corrected in
[#86](https://github.com/IntelLabs/tdx-workload-attestation/pull/86), released
in 0.2.0. The function now returns `Ok(false)` when the current time is
outside the certificate's `notBefore`/`notAfter` range.

Reported by [Borys Tsyrulnikov](https://borystsyrulnikov.dev).

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/tdx_workload_attestation [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0481.html [ADVISORY]
- https://www.intel.com/content/www/us/en/security-center/advisory/intel-sa-01520.html [WEB]
- https://github.com/IntelLabs/tdx-workload-attestation/pull/86 [WEB]
- https://github.com/IntelLabs/tdx-workload-attestation/releases/tag/v0.2.0 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0498 — Transaction readers accept trailing bytes, so a parsed transaction can carry another transaction id
- Aliases: GHSA-j8hr-pjhm-6h27, RUSTSEC-2026-0500 | Published: 2026-11-10 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:L/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/bsv-rs: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.8.5 introduced:0.9.0-beta.1 fixed:0.9.0-beta.7.0

`Transaction::from_binary` and `Transaction::from_hex` read one transaction
from the front of their input, did not check that the input ended there, and
kept the whole input as the transaction's cached serialization: `to_binary`,
`to_hex` and `id()` then answered for bytes that are not the transaction's.
`Transaction::from_json`, given a `hex` field, reads it through `from_hex` and
answers the same. `from_ef`, `from_hex_ef` and `parse_script_offsets` also
read a prefix.

An application that takes bytes or hex from outside, parses them with these
readers and then uses `id()` or the serialization could be given one
transaction under another identity: the same parsed fields, a different id.
An application that passes them only bytes it produced or has already checked
is not affected.

0.8.5 refuses a byte after the transaction; `Transaction::from_reader` reads
from the front of a longer buffer. Where upgrading is not possible, parse with
`Transaction::from_binary`, call `tx.invalidate_caches()` (public from 0.3.16)
and require `tx.to_binary() == bytes`.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/bsv-rs [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0498.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/Calhooon/bsv-rs/blob/v0.8.5/CHANGELOG.md [WEB]
- https://github.com/Calhooon/bsv-rs/issues/840 [REPORT]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0500 — BEEF validation answers valid for a raw entry that is not a transaction
- Aliases: GHSA-j8hr-pjhm-6h27, RUSTSEC-2026-0498 | Published: 2026-11-10 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/bsv-rs: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.8.5 introduced:0.9.0-beta.1 fixed:0.9.0-beta.7.0

`Beef::merge_raw_tx` and `BeefTx::from_raw_tx` accept raw bytes without a
`Result`. For bytes that are not exactly one transaction (a transaction
followed by bytes, a cut transaction, bytes that are no transaction at all),
`Beef::sort_txs`, `verify_structure`, `verify_valid` and `is_valid` could
answer valid, even with no BUMP and no parent in the BEEF, and, where the entry
carried a BUMP index, treat the hash of those bytes as a proven transaction id;
an entry that names it as a parent was answered valid too.

The wire readers (`Beef::from_binary`, `verify_stream`,
`Transaction::from_beef`, `from_atomic_beef`) parse every entry and are not
affected. Reaching this needs an application that passes bytes it did not
parse to `merge_raw_tx` or `BeefTx::from_raw_tx` and then relies on the verdict
or on the transaction the BEEF hands back.

0.8.5 refuses such an entry at all four verdict doors, with or without a BUMP
index, and every entry that names it as a parent. Where upgrading is not
possible, do not rely on `is_valid` or `verify_valid` alone for such an entry:
check that the bytes are exactly one transaction before merging them.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/bsv-rs [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0500.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/Calhooon/bsv-rs/blob/v0.8.5/CHANGELOG.md [WEB]
- https://github.com/Calhooon/bsv-rs/pull/852 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0497 — Transaction::from_json holds an input txid that is not 64 hex digits and writes the input as spending the zero txid
- Aliases: GHSA-84xq-3662-pvx5 | Published: 2026-11-11 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:L/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/bsv-rs: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.8.6 introduced:0.9.0-beta.1 fixed:0.9.0-beta.7.0

`Transaction::from_json`, for a document with no `hex` member, held each
input's `txid` member as given. An input `txid` that is not 64 hex digits
(`"zz"`, `""`, 63 or 65 digits, characters that are not hex digits) answered
`Ok`: `source_txid` and `to_json` gave the text back, while `to_binary` wrote
the input as spending the txid of 32 zero bytes and `id()` hashed those bytes.
Documents naming different texts were one transaction, spending an outpoint
none of them named.

An application that reads a peer's transaction document with `from_json` and
relies both on the text of its inputs and on its bytes or `id()` is affected;
one that reads only documents it produced is not.

0.8.6 refuses such a txid (`Invalid txid of input N: expected 64 hex digits`).
Where upgrading is not possible, require each input's `txid` to be 64 ASCII hex
digits before calling `from_json`.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/bsv-rs [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0497.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/Calhooon/bsv-rs/blob/v0.8.6/CHANGELOG.md [WEB]
- https://github.com/Calhooon/bsv-rs/issues/845 [REPORT]
- https://github.com/Calhooon/bsv-rs/pull/856 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0499 — Signature::from_der reads a signature with bytes after it, a wrong sequence length or an unneeded zero as the signature
- Aliases: GHSA-9c8r-76f3-m426 | Published: 2026-11-11 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:L/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/bsv-rs: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.8.6 introduced:0.9.0-beta.1 fixed:0.9.0-beta.7.0

`Signature::from_der` read a DER-encoded ECDSA signature leniently. It read as
the signature: the signature followed by other bytes; a sequence length
shorter than its contents, or longer with bytes after them (a length byte with
its high bit set among them); and an R or S preceded by a zero byte it did not
need. It read a lone-zero R or S as zero.

`messages::verify`, `compat::bsm::verify_message_der`,
`auth::Certificate::verify`, `auth::VerifiableCertificate::verify`,
`wallet::ProtoWallet::verify_signature` and
`kvstore::KVStoreInterpreter::verify_signature` read through it, and each
verified such a form of a valid signature. Given one signature a key holder
made, anyone could produce other byte strings that verify as that signature
over the same data. No form verifies over other data: an application that acts
on the verdict alone is not affected; one that compares, hashes or stores a
signature's bytes is.

0.8.6 refuses each form as `Error::InvalidSignature`. A signature's bytes are
still not unique after the patch: the same signature with S replaced by n - S
verifies, so an application that keys on the bytes also requires
`Signature::is_low_s()`.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/bsv-rs [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0499.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/Calhooon/bsv-rs/blob/v0.8.6/CHANGELOG.md [WEB]
- https://github.com/Calhooon/bsv-rs/issues/842 [REPORT]
- https://github.com/Calhooon/bsv-rs/pull/846 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0510 — Panic when decoding malformed Ed25519 keys from Base64
- Aliases: GHSA-pqp6-f7wc-6498 | Published: 2026-11-11 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/vodozemac: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.11.2

In version 0.11.1 and earlier of vodozemac, Ed25519PublicKey::from_base64() and
Ed25519SecretKey::from_base64() panic on malformed input instead of returning an
error, if the input decodes to 31 or 33 bytes instead of the expected 32 bytes.
An attacker who can supply a key to an application using vodozemac can crash it.

## Impact

Ed25519 public keys are routinely received from other Matrix users and servers,
so a malicious party can publish a crafted key that crashes clients parsing it.

The impact is limited to denial of service; no key disclosure or signature
forgery is possible.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/vodozemac [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0510.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/matrix-org/vodozemac/pull/453 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0544 — libs-sys: heap buffer overflow
- Aliases: CVE-2026-85091 | Published: 2026-11-11 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/libz-sys: introduced:1.1.25 fixed:1.1.33

zlib versions 1.3.1.2 through 1.3.2 contain a heap buffer overflow vulnerability in the gz_vacate() function when processing non-blocking gzwrite() operations with stale external buffer pointers. Attackers can trigger the overflow by calling gzprintf() or gzvprintf() after a write stall, causing an unchecked memmove() to write beyond the internal input buffer boundary.

- https://crates.io/crates/libz-sys [PACKAGE]

- https://github.com/rust-lang/libz-sys/commit/b161d77050c00c6eeb7dd4b66ea14d4524d73dc4 [WEB]
- https://github.com/rust-lang/libz-sys/commit/8827cadbe2b560233909212527c291827fb34512 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0501 — Shamir backup recovery accepts a share at x = 0, so a supplied share can substitute the recovered private key
- Aliases: GHSA-cwxv-4866-5gq2 | Published: 2026-11-12 | Modified: 2026-11-12 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:H/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/bsv-rs: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.8.7 introduced:0.9.0-beta.1 fixed:0.9.0-beta.7.0

`KeyShares::from_backup_format` (the Shamir backup share reader, public since
0.2.0, the crate's first release) accepted a share whose x coordinate is 0,
and `KeyShares::recover_private_key` interpolates the shares at x = 0 to
recover the key. At an x of 0 the interpolation answers that share's y value
alone, so an attacker who supplies one share in that form — with the share's
integrity tag ground to match the honest set's — chooses the recovered key
outright. The non-canonical zero x = p works the same way, and a repeated x
past the threshold window also passed.

An application that recovers a Shamir backup from shares it did not produce —
a custodian's medium, a share pasted by hand, a set restored from another
writer — could be handed the attacker's key as the recovered one. A set the
crate's own writer produces never carries such a share; the door is the
reader.

0.8.7 refuses such a set before interpolating (the 0.9 line's next prerelease
carries the same refusal). Where upgrading is not possible, before recovering
split each share on `.` into four fields, base58-decode the x field, and
refuse the set if it decodes to zero (the one-character field `1`, or a value
at or above the field prime) or if two shares carry the same x.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/bsv-rs [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0501.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/Calhooon/bsv-rs/blob/v0.8.7/CHANGELOG.md [WEB]
- https://github.com/Calhooon/bsv-rs/issues/854 [REPORT]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0528 — UB can be reached from safe code
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-11-12 | Modified: 2026-11-13 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/ref-swap: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.1.3

The variance of `RefSwap` and `OptionRefSwap` were incorrect, possibly allowing use-after-free.

`OptionRefSwap::into_inner` did not check for null.

The fix is to make `RefSwap` and `OptionRefSwap` invariant, and to change `OptionRefSwap::into_inner` to return an option.
It is available in version 0.1.3.
If your project still compiles with 0.1.3 then it is not affected by the unsoundness on version 0.1.2

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/ref-swap [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0528.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/Nitrokey/ref-swap/releases/tag/v0.1.3 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0533 — Incorrect handling of embedded SVG and MathML leads to mutation XSS
- Aliases: GHSA-934p-vp7m-gx3c | Published: 2026-11-12 | Modified: 2026-11-13 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/ammonia: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:3.3.5 introduced:4.0.0 fixed:4.1.9 introduced:4.2.0 fixed:4.2.5

The MathML text integration points (`mi`, `mo`, `mn`, `ms`, `mtext`) and SVG `foreignObject` can be made to contain a tag in the other one's namespace (a MathML integration point with a child in the SVG namespace, or vice versa) without the root tag that switches out of the HTML namespace. If the attacker can construct input that parses differently in as HTML than as foreign XML, they can inject arbitrary JavaScript code into the user's browser.

For example:

- The user writes `<math><mi><svg><gadget></svg></mi></math>`.
- The application enables `math`, `mi`, and the gadget, but not `svg`.
- Ammonia filters the input to `<math><mi><gadget></mi></math>`.
- Because svg is now gone, the gadget switches from being parsed as SVG to being parsed as HTML.
- The gadget is written in such a way that it exploits the parsing differences between HTML and MathML.

As far as we know, the gadget can only be written using a tag that is parsed as raw text in HTML. These [elements] are:

- title
- textarea
- xmp
- iframe
- noembed
- noframes
- plaintext
- noscript
- style
- script

Applications that do not explicitly allow any of these tags should not be affected, since none are allowed by default.

[elements]: https://github.com/servo/html5ever/blob/a027c2835472d518893c906dc29c42200af3c960/html5ever/src/tree_builder/rules.rs

---

This vulnerability was reported by Roger Meyer.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/ammonia [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0533.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/rust-ammonia/ammonia/pull/269 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0529 — KVStoreInterpreter::verify_signature_legacy answers true for every token and every key
- Aliases: GHSA-mj2m-jxgv-r446 | Published: 2026-11-13 | Modified: 2026-11-13 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:H/A:N`
- Affected crates.io/bsv-rs: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.8.9 introduced:0.9.0-beta.1 fixed:0.9.0-beta.7.0

`KVStoreInterpreter::verify_signature_legacy(&PushDrop, &PublicKey)`, public
and deprecated since 0.2.0 (the crate's first release), read neither argument
and answered `true`. A KVStore token signed by nobody, a signed token checked
against a key other than its controller's, and a PushDrop with no fields each
passed. Its documentation said it delegated to the `KVStoreFields`-based
verifier; it did not.

An application that checks, with this function, a KVStore token it did not
write (an overlay lookup's answer, or a transaction output a peer hands it) and
acts on `true` accepts a value no controller signed, attributed to whatever
controller the token names. No caller in the crate itself uses the function.
This was run on 0.2.0, 0.5.0, 0.8.0, 0.8.7, 0.8.8, 0.9.0-beta.1 and
0.9.0-beta.7 as crates.io serves them, and the source of all 63 published
versions carries the same constant body (the yanked 0.3.21 included).

0.8.9 answers `true` only for a token whose controller field is the
given key and whose last field is that key's signature over the others (the
0.9 line's next prerelease carries the same fix). There is no workaround:
upgrade to 0.8.9, or, on the 0.9 line, to its next prerelease.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/bsv-rs [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0529.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/Calhooon/bsv-rs/blob/v0.8.9/CHANGELOG.md [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0552 — Bare LF in chunked trailers can consume pipelined requests or stall body decoding
- Aliases: GHSA-9w4r-xc43-v236 | Published: 2026-11-16 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:P/PR:N/UI:N/VC:L/VI:L/VA:L/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/actix-http: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:3.21.1

Affected versions of `actix-http` skip a bare line feed (LF) inside an HTTP/1.1
chunked trailer instead of rejecting the invalid line ending. Trailer lines
must end with a carriage return and line feed (CRLF).

For example, after `0\r\nX\n`, the parser continues reading trailer data. If a
request follows on the same connection, its head can be consumed as part of the
trailer, so that request is never dispatched. If no more bytes arrive, body
decoding waits for more input instead of reporting an error. A remote client
can trigger these effects without authentication.

Request smuggling additionally requires an HTTP intermediary that forwards the
malformed body but uses a different request boundary. The tested HTTP
intermediaries either rejected the input or consumed and normalized it before
forwarding. Proxy access-control bypass, cache poisoning, and effects on other
users were not demonstrated.

Upgrade to `actix-http` 3.21.1 or later. The fix rejects bare LF and invalid
control bytes in trailers before consuming the following request, while
preserving partial reads and valid CRLF-terminated trailers.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/actix-http [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0552.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/actix/actix-web/releases/tag/actix-http-v3.21.1 [WEB]
- https://github.com/actix/actix-web/security/advisories/GHSA-9w4r-xc43-v236 [ADVISORY]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0553 — Malformed quoted chunk extensions can end HTTP/1.1 request bodies early
- Aliases: GHSA-9543-cj4x-h76q | Published: 2026-11-16 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:P/PR:N/UI:N/VC:N/VI:L/VA:N/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/actix-http: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:3.21.3

Affected versions of `actix-http` do not track quoted strings when parsing
HTTP/1.1 chunk extensions. A carriage return inside a quoted extension value
is treated as the end of the chunk-size line, even if the closing quote is
missing. For example, `0;x="\r\n\r\n` is accepted as the end of a chunked body,
and a following pipelined request is dispatched on the same connection.

A remote client can trigger this without authentication. CR and LF are invalid
inside HTTP quoted strings, including after a backslash; they must be rejected
rather than interpreted as framing or accepted as quoted data.

Request smuggling requires an upstream intermediary that forwards the malformed
input while using a different request boundary. Dispatching two requests on the
sender's own connection does not by itself demonstrate an authorization bypass
or an effect on another user. Proxy bypass, cache poisoning, and cross-user
response mixing were not demonstrated by the reproduction.

Upgrade to `actix-http` 3.21.3 or later. The fix preserves quote and escape state
across reads and rejects invalid control bytes inside quoted strings and quoted
pairs before consuming the following body or request bytes.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/actix-http [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0553.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/actix/actix-web/releases/tag/actix-http-v3.21.3 [WEB]
- https://github.com/actix/actix-web/security/advisories/GHSA-9543-cj4x-h76q [ADVISORY]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0554 — Partial requests on keep-alive connections can bypass the request header timeout
- Aliases: GHSA-54pm-2wm5-hpmg | Published: 2026-11-16 | Modified: 2026-11-17 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V4: `CVSS:4.0/AV:N/AC:L/AT:N/PR:N/UI:N/VC:N/VI:N/VA:L/SC:N/SI:N/SA:N`
- Affected crates.io/actix-http: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:3.21.2

Affected versions of `actix-http` apply the request header timeout only to the
first request on an HTTP/1 connection. When data arrives for a later request,
the dispatcher clears the idle keep-alive timer without starting a request
header timer.

A remote client without authentication can complete one request, send one byte
of a second request, and leave the connection open indefinitely without sending
more data. The same problem occurs when a complete pipelined request is followed
by a partial request in the same read. Repeating this can exhaust connection or
file-descriptor limits and prevent new connections from being accepted. The
effect depends on the server's resource limits and upstream connection controls.

Upgrade to `actix-http` 3.21.2 or later. The fix applies the configured request
header timeout to later partial requests, including those left in the buffer
after a complete pipelined request. Additional bytes do not renew the deadline.
The timeout covers request headers, not upload bodies.

If an upgrade is not possible, disable HTTP keep-alive, for example with
`HttpServer::keep_alive(KeepAlive::Disabled)` in `actix-web`. Connection limits
can reduce resource use but do not make stalled connections expire.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/actix-http [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0554.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/actix/actix-web/releases/tag/actix-http-v3.21.2 [WEB]
- https://github.com/actix/actix-web/security/advisories/GHSA-54pm-2wm5-hpmg [ADVISORY]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0560 — STARTTLS response injection allows a man-in-the-middle to forge SMTP replies
- Aliases: GHSA-q3jf-wmp2-47pg | Published: 2026-11-17 | Modified: 2026-11-18 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:H/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:L/A:L`
- Affected crates.io/lettre: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.11.24

A man-in-the-middle can append replies of their own to the cleartext reply to
`STARTTLS`. The client takes them for the server's replies to the commands it
then sends over TLS, so the attacker can, for example, make a message that the
server rejected look accepted.

Only connections upgraded with `STARTTLS` are affected (`Tls::Required` and
`Tls::Opportunistic`, as set up by `starttls_relay`). Implicit TLS
(`Tls::Wrapper`, as set up by `relay`) is not.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/lettre [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0560.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/lettre/lettre/commit/b404122f1b51cdfa32662661849aa3a22e02af38 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0561 — Missing dot-stuffing after a bare LF or CR allows SMTP command injection
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-11-17 | Modified: 2026-11-18 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/lettre: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.11.24

Someone who controls the content of a message with bare LF or CR line endings
can end it early with `\n.\n` on a server that takes a bare LF for a line
ending. What follows is read by the server as SMTP commands, which can be used
to send other messages over the same authenticated connection.

This affects messages passed to `send_raw`, and message bodies that are sent
without being encoded. The LF line endings of a `String` body are converted to
CRLF before it is sent.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/lettre [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0561.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/lettre/lettre/commit/3d9a6e6a467c6ce00bd39ada6aa1a00e57540a1d [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0562 — Panic on an `EHLO` reply line of only white space allows denial of service
- Aliases: GHSA-23wm-4vvw-r7p2 | Published: 2026-11-17 | Modified: 2026-11-18 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/lettre: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.11.24

A malicious or compromised server can reply to `EHLO` with a line holding
nothing but white space, causing the client to panic.

Applications that connect only to a trusted server over implicit TLS are not
exposed; the risk applies to clients that may connect to untrusted servers, or
that use `STARTTLS` or no TLS, where a man-in-the-middle can forge the reply.

### RUSTSEC-2026-0563 — Messages for an address with a quoted local part are sent to a different address
- Aliases: GHSA-62w5-58xq-p27j | Published: 2026-11-17 | Modified: 2026-11-18 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/lettre: introduced:0.11.0 fixed:0.11.24

A message whose sender or recipient has a quoted local part is sent with a
different address in its envelope than the one the application was given:
`"d "@example.com` becomes `d@example.com`. Someone who can choose such an
address can have a message delivered to another mailbox than the one the
application validated.

Addresses without a quoted local part are not affected.

### RUSTSEC-2026-0564 — Panic on a mailbox display name containing CR or LF allows denial of service
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-11-17 | Modified: 2026-11-18 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/lettre: introduced:0.10.0-rc.5 fixed:0.11.24

A `Mailbox` whose display name contains a CR or LF panics when it is formatted
or put in a header such as `From` or `To`. Someone who can choose a display
name, for example the name of a user a message is sent to, can crash the
thread or task building the message.

Applications that take display names only from trusted input are not exposed.

### RUSTSEC-2026-0565 — Unbounded buffering of an SMTP reply allows a malicious server to exhaust memory
- Aliases: GHSA-pmvc-x69f-5p9f | Published: 2026-11-17 | Modified: 2026-11-18 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/lettre: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.11.24

A malicious or compromised server can send a reply line that never ends, which
the client buffers until memory runs out. A reply made of many short lines also
keeps a CPU core busy: for a few seconds per reply since 0.11.22, for as long
as the server wants before it.

Applications that connect only to a trusted server over implicit TLS are not
exposed; the risk applies to clients that may connect to untrusted servers, or
that use `STARTTLS` or no TLS, where a man-in-the-middle can forge the reply.

### RUSTSEC-2026-0583 — Panics during RSA private-key construction, validation, or re-encoding
- Aliases: GHSA-3hj7-mch7-c7cc | Published: 2026-11-19 | Modified: 2026-11-20 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:L`
- Affected crates.io/sad-rsa: introduced:0.0.0-0 fixed:0.10.4

`RsaPrivateKey` private-key construction, validation, and serialization can panic across three
classes of inputs, as well as when re-encoding certain valid keys:

- `RsaPrivateKey::from_components` and PKCS#1 or PKCS#8 private-key decoding (DER or PEM) can panic
  during validation when a supplied factor equals 1, because `RsaPrivateKey::validate()` let a
  factor of 1 reach a later check that panicked.
- `RsaPrivateKey::from_primes` and `RsaPrivateKey::from_p_q` can panic during construction when a
  supplied factor is 0, 1, or even.
- Prime recovery in `RsaPrivateKey::from_components` can panic when the private exponent is 0.
- For a key, including a valid one, whose first prime occupies more limbs than the second,
  `crt_coefficient`, `to_pkcs1_der`, `to_pkcs8_der`, and `serde` serialization can panic.

An application that imports untrusted private keys or factors, or re-encodes such keys, can lose a
worker, or the whole process when built with `panic = "abort"`. Public-key parsing is not affected.

Fixed in 0.10.4, which rejects factors below 2, even factors, repeated factors, and a zero private
exponent during prime recovery, and computes the CRT coefficient and re-encodes valid two-prime
keys when the first prime occupies more limbs than the second.

If you cannot upgrade to 0.10.4, isolate the entire import, construction, CRT calculation, or
serialization in a separate process and treat an abnormal exit as an operation failure, or use
`catch_unwind` around the entire operation when panics unwind.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/sad-rsa [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0583.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/sadco-io/sad-rsa/releases/tag/v0.10.4 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0584 — gRPC `IpKeyExtractor` keys on client-supplied forwarding headers
- Aliases: GHSA-r5c9-r265-ccg3 | Published: 2026-11-19 | Modified: 2026-11-20 | Severity: None
- CVSS_V3: `CVSS:3.1/AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N/S:U/C:N/I:N/A:L`
- Affected crates.io/tokio-rate-limit: introduced:0.5.0 fixed:0.11.1

`tonic_middleware::IpKeyExtractor` is documented as rate limiting gRPC requests by client IP address,
but on a standard tonic server it keys on client-supplied `x-forwarded-for` and `x-real-ip`
headers. In 0.5.0–0.9.1, the extractor reads those headers directly; in 0.10.0–0.11.0, it first
checks for a bare `std::net::SocketAddr` request extension—which `tonic::transport::Server` never
supplies, as it provides `TcpConnectInfo` instead—and falls back to the same headers. Direct
clients can therefore choose their rate-limit key to bypass per-IP rate limits and increase the
number of tracked keys; without forwarding headers, 0.5.0–0.9.1 allow requests without limiting
them and 0.10.0–0.11.0 reject them by default unless `.fail_open()` is enabled.

Applications are affected if they use `GrpcRateLimitLayer` with `IpKeyExtractor` (the
`tonic-support` feature). The HTTP middleware's `IpKeyExtractor` reads `ConnectInfo<SocketAddr>` and
is not affected.

Version 0.11.1 fixes `IpKeyExtractor` to honor a server-supplied `SocketAddr` override first and then
read the TCP peer address from tonic's `TcpConnectInfo` (which `tonic::transport::Server` attaches
to plaintext connections and connections using its built-in TLS), without ever reading forwarding
headers. Deployments behind a trusted proxy can use the new opt-in `ForwardedIpKeyExtractor`, which
reads a single configured header (`x-forwarded-for` or `x-real-ip`) and is only correct when that
proxy sets the header as documented.

If you cannot upgrade to 0.11.1, use a custom key extractor that reads the peer IP from tonic's
connection information for the transport in use (including TLS) and rejects requests without a
verified peer address before invoking the limiter, or restrict backend access to a trusted proxy
that strips all incoming `x-forwarded-for` and `x-real-ip` fields and sets one `x-forwarded-for`
value to the verified client IP on every request.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/tokio-rate-limit [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0584.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/sadco-io/tokio-rate-limit/releases/tag/v0.11.1 [WEB]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0585 — ML-KEM does not perform the FIPS 203 input checks
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-11-20 | Modified: 2026-11-20 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/pqcrypto-mlkem: introduced:0.0.0-0

`pqcrypto-mlkem` wraps PQClean's ML-KEM implementation, which does not perform
the input checks required by FIPS 203:

- encapsulation does not perform the encapsulation key modulus check
  (section 7.2), so encapsulation keys with coefficients that are not reduced
  modulo q are accepted;
- decapsulation does not perform the decapsulation key hash check
  (section 7.3), so a decapsulation key whose stored hash or embedded
  encapsulation key has been altered is accepted.

With the Wycheproof ML-KEM vectors, 108 / 112 / 116 invalid encapsulation keys
(ML-KEM-512 / -768 / -1024) and 2 invalid decapsulation keys per parameter set
are accepted.

This does not disclose key material. It is a FIPS 203 non-conformance, and an
affected implementation can disagree with a conforming one about which keys
are valid.

The crate is unmaintained (see RUSTSEC-2026-0161) and no patched version is
expected. Migrating to a maintained implementation, such as the
[`ml-kem`](https://crates.io/crates/ml-kem) crate, is recommended.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/pqcrypto-mlkem [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0585.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/RatioLabsEU/pqc-field-notes/tree/main/advisories/2026-mlkem-input-validation [ADVISORY]

### RUSTSEC-2026-0586 — ML-DSA verification accepts non-conforming signatures with the `avx2` feature
- Aliases:  | Published: 2026-11-20 | Modified: 2026-11-20 | Severity: None
- Affected crates.io/pqcrypto-mldsa: introduced:0.0.0-0

With the default `avx2` feature on x86_64 (non-Windows) and a CPU that supports
AVX2, `pqcrypto-mldsa` verifies signatures with PQClean's AVX2 implementation of
ML-DSA. That implementation predates two upstream fixes
([pq-crystals/dilithium#113](https://github.com/pq-crystals/dilithium/issues/113),
3 June 2026):

- the verifier does not check that the infinity norm of `z` is below
  `γ1 − β` (FIPS 204, Algorithm 8, line 13), so it accepts signatures that
  conforming verifiers reject;
- `use_hint_avx()` mishandles a boundary case, so one class of valid
  signatures is rejected.

Creating an accepted out-of-bound signature requires the private key. The
practical effect is that verification results differ between this crate with
AVX2 and conforming implementations, including this crate without AVX2.

The Wycheproof ML-DSA verification vectors reproduce both defects: with AVX2,
37 / 41 / 49 invalid vectors (ML-DSA-44 / -65 / -87) are accepted and one valid
vector per parameter set is rejected.

The crate is unmaintained (see RUSTSEC-2026-0166) and no patched version is
expected. Disabling the `avx2` feature avoids the affected code. Migrating to a
maintained implementation, such as the [`ml-dsa`](https://crates.io/crates/ml-dsa)
crate, is recommended.

References:
- https://crates.io/crates/pqcrypto-mldsa [PACKAGE]
- https://rustsec.org/advisories/RUSTSEC-2026-0586.html [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/RatioLabsEU/pqc-field-notes/tree/main/advisories/2026-mldsa-avx2-verifier [ADVISORY]
- https://github.com/pq-crystals/dilithium/issues/113 [REPORT]
